<details><summary>Baseline answer</summary>

Below are the trade‑related agreements that are **explicitly mentioned in the supplied excerpts** as involving the Republic of Serbia:

| # | Agreement (as named in the text) | Parties | Main purpose / scope (as described) | Where it appears in the source |
|---|-----------------------------------|---------|-------------------------------------|--------------------------------|
| 1 | **Free Trade Agreement between the EFTA States and the Republic of Serbia** | European Free Trade Association (Iceland, Liechtenstein, Norway, Switzerland) + Serbia | Establishes a free‑trade area, liberalises trade in goods (GATT XXIV), promotes investment, services, intellectual‑property protection, government‑procurement liberalisation and sustainable‑development objectives. | [2] – objectives; [4] – trade‑facilitation; [7] – government procurement; [8] – environment‑friendly investment; [1] – overall intent |
| 2 | **Bilateral agreements on trade in agricultural products** (one between each individual EFTA State and Serbia) | Each EFTA State + Serbia (five separate agreements) | Form the agricultural‑goods component of the overall EFTA‑Serbia free‑trade area. | [5] – states that these bilateral accords “form part of the instruments establishing a free trade area” |
| 3 | **Free Trade Agreement between the Republic of Albania and Serbia and Montenegro** | Albania + Serbia + Montenegro | A separate multilateral FTA that includes Serbia (alongside Montenegro) and Albania. The excerpt only gives the title; no substantive provisions are quoted. | [6] – title of the agreement |
| 4 | **Marrakesh Agreement establishing the WTO (and related WTO agreements)** | All WTO members, including Serbia and the EFTA States | The FTA expressly notes that none of its provisions may be interpreted as exempting the parties from their WTO obligations. | [1] – reference to “the Marrakesh Agreement establishing the WTO” |

### How to use this list
- **For a comprehensive view of Serbia’s trade framework**, start with the EFTA‑Serbia FTA (Item 1) and its complementary agricultural accords (Item 2). These together create the full free‑trade area between Serbia and the four EFTA economies.  
- **If you need to track Serbia’s regional FTAs in the Balkans**, the Albania‑Serbia‑Montenegro agreement (Item 3) is the other multilateral pact mentioned.  
- **All of these agreements operate within the broader WTO system**, as reinforced by the reference to the Marrakesh Agreement (Item 4).  

*No other Serbia‑specific trade agreements are cited in the provided material.*

### Q2: What is AI governance?

| | Baseline | Graph-Enriched |
|---|---|---|
| Time | 4.03s | 2.99s (+0.19s expansion) |
| Score avg | 4.8/5 | 4.2/5 |
| Expanded docs | — | 1 |
| **Winner** | **✓** |  |

> **Judge:** Answer A offers a slightly more complete view with operational detail on human oversight and presents its sources more clearly, giving it the edge.

<details><summary>Baseline answer</summary>

**AI governance** is the collective set of policies, processes, standards and institutional arrangements that steer how artificial‑intelligence systems are designed, built, deployed and used.  It is understood as a **multi‑stakeholder responsibility** that brings together governments, industry, academia, civil society and international bodies (e.g., the UN) to coordinate action and ensure that AI serves the public interest [2].  

Rather than being only a defensive, compliance‑oriented activity, contemporary thinking reframes AI governance as a **proactive, value‑creating function** that can become a source of competitive advantage for organisations, linking risk mitigation with innovation and business success [5].  

At the operational level, AI governance also means **embedding human oversight into AI agents**—providing people, in line with security standards and personal preferences, the right to intervene when an autonomous system makes a decision they do not wish to accept [8].  

In sum, AI governance is the coordinated, multi‑stakeholder framework that defines the rules, values and control mechanisms governing AI throughout its lifecycle, balancing safety, accountability, innovation and societal benefit.  

**Sources**  
- Multi‑stakeholder coordination definition [2]  
- Reframing as proactive, strategic function [5]  
- Human‑intervention aspect of governance [8]

</details>

<details><summary>Graph-enriched answer</summary>

AI governance is the collection of policies, structures, and processes that guide how artificial‑intelligence systems are designed, built, deployed, and used.  It is conceived as a **shared, multi‑stakeholder responsibility** that brings together governments, industry, academia, civil society and the United Nations system to coordinate action and set common rules [2].  Rather than being merely a defensive, compliance‑oriented activity, contemporary thinking frames AI governance as a **proactive, value‑creating strategic function** that can drive business success and innovation while managing risks [5].  In practice, AI governance bodies are often organized as multi‑stakeholder entities—though some observers note that a small number of corporations can exert outsized influence over them [3].  

In short, AI governance is the framework that ensures AI technologies are developed and used responsibly, ethically, securely, and in line with societal values, through coordinated oversight by a broad set of actors.

</details>

### Q3: What are india AI policies?

| | Baseline | Graph-Enriched |
|---|---|---|
| Time | 9.20s | 8.32s (+0.16s expansion) |
| Score avg | 4.8/5 | 4.8/5 |
| Expanded docs | — | 0 |
| **Winner** |  |  |

> **Judge:** Both answers cover the same set of policies with equal detail, accuracy, and citation quality, making them indistinguishable in overall quality.

<details><summary>Baseline answer</summary>

**India’s AI policy landscape is built around a series of strategic documents, funding programmes and implementation‑focused recommendations that together aim to make AI a “scalable problem‑solver” for the country while ensuring safety, inclusiveness and global leadership.**  

Below is a concise synthesis of the main elements that appear in the supplied sources.

| Policy / Initiative | Core Features | Status / Notes |
|--------------------|---------------|----------------|
| **National Strategy for Artificial Intelligence (June 2018)** | • Sets a “strong basis for future regulation” of AI. <br>• Highlights priority sectors: healthcare, agriculture, education, smart cities & infrastructure, smart mobility & transportation. | First high‑level roadmap; does **not** create binding legal obligations. |
| **Digital Personal Data Protection Act (2023)** | • Provides the legal framework for personal data handling, which underpins trustworthy AI. | Enacted law; shapes AI‑related data practices. |
| **India AI Mission (2024)** – $1.25 bn allocation | • Builds **computing‑infrastructure capacity** (e.g., free GPUs, cloud resources). <br>• Funds **innovation & dataset creation** for AI models. <br>• Emphasises **safe and trusted AI** (advisories on deep‑fakes, generative AI). <br>• Supports **skilling, reskilling and AI education**. | Launched March 2024; operational funding now flowing. |
| **Task‑Force Recommendations (2023‑24)** | a) Create an **inter‑ministerial National AI Mission** to coordinate activities. <br>b) Set up **digital data banks, marketplaces & exchanges** for cross‑industry data. <br>c) Contribute to **operational standards** for AI systems. <br>d) Enact **enabling policies** – data‑ownership rules, sharing rights, tax incentives. <br>e) Develop an **AI education strategy** and **reskilling programmes**. <br>f) Participate actively in **international AI governance** and bilateral AI partnerships. | Recommendations have been incorporated into the 2024 AI Mission and are guiding future regulations. |
| **Principles‑Based, Flexible Framework** (as of writing) | • India has **no binding AI statutes** yet; instead it pursues a **principles‑based approach** to encourage responsible innovation. <br>• The framework is intended to be adaptable to rapid AI advances. | Mirrors the situation in Israel (no binding law) and reflects the “regulating under uncertainty” stance. |
| **Infrastructure & Support Measures** (from “Full Stack Sovereign AI” and “AI Factory” notes) | • **Free GPU access** for researchers and startups. <br>• Direct **funding for AI model development**. <br>• Investment in **energy & compute infrastructure** to sustain large‑scale AI workloads. | Described as “unprecedented” support for sovereign AI capabilities. |
| **Strategic Positioning in Global AI Governance** (multiple briefings) | • Leverage India’s **digital public‑infrastructure experience** (UPI, Aadhaar, digital identity). <br>• Avoid extremes: not as restrictive as Europe, not laissez‑faire like the U.S., nor state‑controlled like China. <br>• Seek a **balanced, inclusive, and export‑oriented AI ecosystem**. | Guiding principle for how India shapes both domestic policy and its voice in international fora. |

### Key Themes Across the Policies

1. **Sector‑wide AI Enablement** – Health, agriculture, education, smart cities, and mobility are repeatedly singled out as priority domains where AI should deliver public value.  
2. **Infrastructure First** – Massive investment in compute (free GPUs, cloud capacity) and data‑exchange platforms to create a “sovereign AI stack.”  
3. **Human‑Capital Development** – Dedicated AI education strategies, skilling and reskilling programmes, and incentives for talent development.  
4. **Safety & Trust** – Explicit focus on “safe and trusted AI,” with advisories on deep‑fakes and generative AI, and the development of operational standards.  
5. **Governance Model** – A **principles‑based, flexible** regulatory posture, coordinated through an inter‑ministerial mission, complemented by data‑ownership policies and tax incentives.  
6. **International Engagement** – Active participation in global AI policy discussions and bilateral partnerships to share best practices and co‑develop solutions.  

### Bottom Line

India’s AI policy framework is **progressive and multi‑layered**: a strategic national mission backed by a sizable budget, strong infrastructure and talent support, sector‑specific application goals, and a principled yet non‑binding regulatory stance designed to foster responsible innovation while positioning the country as a global AI governance stakeholder.  

**Sources:** [1] (National Strategy 2018, 2024 AI Mission, Digital Personal Data Protection Act, principle‑based framework); [6] (Task‑Force recommendations); [2], [7] (free GPUs, funding, infrastructure); [3], [5], [8] (strategic positioning in global AI landscape).

</details>

<details><summary>Graph-enriched answer</summary>

**India’s AI policy landscape is built around a mix of strategic road‑maps, funding programmes, data‑governance measures and sector‑wide enable‑enabling actions rather than a single, binding AI law. The main elements that have been announced or enacted to date are:**

| Policy / Initiative | Core Features | Status / Timing |
|--------------------|---------------|-----------------|
| **National Strategy for Artificial Intelligence (June 2018)** | Sets a “strong basis for future regulation” and focuses AI use‑cases in health‑care, agriculture, education, smart cities & infrastructure, and smart mobility/transport. | Framework document; no binding legislation attached. |
| **Digital Personal Data Protection Act (2023)** | Provides a legal regime for personal data protection that will also shape how AI systems can collect, process and share data. | Enacted law; applies to AI‑driven data processing. |
| **India AI Mission (announced March 2024)** | • Budget of **US $1.25 billion**.<br>• Finances computing‑infrastructure capacity, free GPU access, AI‑model funding, dataset creation, and “safe and trusted AI”.<br>• Supports skilling, innovation hubs and sovereign AI research. | Operational; funding already allocated. |
| **AI‑related advisory notices (2024)** | Issue guidance on deep‑fakes and generative‑AI risks, signalling a precautionary approach while the regulatory framework is still evolving. | Ongoing advisory practice. |
| **Task‑Force Recommendations (2023‑24)** | a) Create an **inter‑ministerial National AI Mission** to coordinate AI activities.<br>b) Set up **digital data banks, marketplaces and exchanges** for cross‑industry data.<br>c) Develop **operational standards** for AI‑based systems.<br>d) Enact **enabling policies** – data‑ownership/sharing rules, tax incentives for AI innovation.<br>e) Draft an **AI education strategy** and **reskilling programmes**.<br>f) Participate in **international AI‑governance discussions** and **bilateral partnerships** for social‑economic AI solutions. | Recommendations awaiting formal adoption; many have been reflected in the AI Mission and related programmes. |
| **Free‑GPU & Infrastructure Support** (as highlighted in “Designing the AI Factory” and “India’s AI Infrastructure and Government Policy”) | The government provides **free access to GPUs** and funds for building AI compute capacity, aiming to create a sovereign AI ecosystem. | Ongoing under the AI Mission. |
| **Principles‑Based, Flexible Framework** (as noted in the “Overview of AI policy in 10 jurisdictions”) | India currently has **no binding AI regulations**; instead it pursues a **principles‑based approach** that encourages responsible innovation while the regulatory scaffolding is built. | Current regulatory posture. |

### Key Themes Across the Policies

1. **Strategic Investment & Sovereign Compute** – Massive public funding (US $1.25 bn) plus free GPU provision to build domestic AI infrastructure and reduce reliance on foreign cloud providers.  
2. **Data Enablement** – Creation of public data banks/marketplaces and a legal framework (DPDP Act) to ensure data can be shared safely for AI development.  
3. **Human‑Capital Development** – Dedicated AI education and reskilling programmes to build a skilled workforce.  
4. **Safety & Trust** – Explicit budget line for “safe and trusted AI” and advisory notices on deep‑fakes and generative AI.  
5. **Governance Model** – A **principles‑based, flexible** regime rather than prescriptive, binding rules; coordinated through an inter‑ministerial mission and active participation in global AI governance forums.  

**In short, India’s AI policy is a progressive, multi‑pronged strategy that combines a national roadmap, substantial public investment, data‑sharing infrastructure, human‑skill development, and a principle‑driven regulatory outlook, rather than a single, enforceable AI law.**  

**Sources:**  
- National Strategy for AI, Digital Personal Data Protection Act, and AI Mission details – [1]

### Q4: what is AI diplomacy?

| | Baseline | Graph-Enriched |
|---|---|---|
| Time | 2.89s | 3.52s (+0.18s expansion) |
| Score avg | 4.5/5 | 4.8/5 |
| Expanded docs | — | 5 |
| **Winner** |  | **✓** |

> **Judge:** Both answers are complete and specific, but Answer A avoids potentially extraneous citations, making it marginally more accurate.

<details><summary>Baseline answer</summary>

**AI diplomacy** is the set of diplomatic activities that revolve around artificial intelligence. It encompasses three inter‑related dimensions:

1. **AI as a diplomatic topic** – the way states negotiate, set norms, and cooperate on the development, governance, and ethical use of AI [3].  
2. **AI as a diplomatic tool** – the use of AI‑driven technologies (e.g., data analytics, automated reporting, decision‑support systems) to enhance traditional diplomatic work such as negotiation, reporting, and strategic communication [4], [5].  
3. **AI as a factor reshaping the diplomatic environment** – the broader geopolitical and geo‑economic changes that AI creates, which diplomats must understand and respond to [6], [7].

In short, AI diplomacy is how countries manage their international relationships regarding artificial intelligence, treating AI simultaneously as a subject of policy, a set of instruments for diplomatic practice, and a transformative force in the global arena. [3][6][7]

</details>

<details><summary>Graph-enriched answer</summary>

**AI diplomacy** is the set of diplomatic activities that revolve around artificial intelligence. It encompasses how states *manage their international relationships concerning AI*—from negotiating norms, standards and governance frameworks to cooperating on research and development, and to addressing the security, ethical and economic implications that AI creates. In practice, AI diplomacy can be understood through three inter‑linked lenses [6][7]:

| Lens | What it means for diplomacy |
|------|------------------------------|
| **AI as a diplomatic topic** | AI itself becomes a subject of diplomatic dialogue – countries discuss regulation, data‑sharing, export controls, human‑rights impacts, etc. |
| **AI as a diplomatic tool** | Diplomats use AI‑powered analytics, forecasting, translation, and decision‑support systems to enhance reporting, negotiation and strategic communication. |
| **AI as a factor reshaping the environment** | The rise of AI alters the geopolitical and geo‑economic landscape, creating new power dynamics that diplomats must understand and respond to. |

Thus, AI diplomacy is **both a field of policy (the “what”) and a set of instruments (the “how”) that together shape how nations conduct diplomacy in an AI‑driven world** [3].

</details>

The 2018 reports build on this trajectory, showing continued rapid growth. | 【1】【3】 |

### Overall Picture for 2018
- **Massive growth in mobile broadband** – nearing half the world’s population with an active subscription.
- **Policy momentum** – governments are urged to create enabling environments (leadership, training, legal reform, tax relief) to sustain that growth.
- **Technical evolution** – IPv6 adoption is advancing, preparing the network for continued scale.
- **Economic relevance** – improved affordability is increasingly linked to broader economic development.
- **Governance crossroads** – 2018 is widely framed as a “tipping point,” where decisions on multi‑stakeholder governance, regulation, and standards will shape the Internet’s future trajectory.

These points together constitute the most comprehensive view of the Internet’s state in 2018 that can be drawn from the supplied documents.

| **“A Tipping Point for the Internet: 10 Predictions for 2018” (Briefing Papers)** | Forecasts on Internet governance and digital policy | • Frames 2018 as a **tipping point** for the Internet and its governance, suggesting major shifts in policy, regulation, and stakeholder dynamics are expected during the year【2】【6】【8】. |
| **“A Tipping Point for the Internet: Predictions for 2018” (stand‑alone article)** | Same thematic focus as the briefing papers | • Reinforces the view that 2018 marks a pivotal moment for Internet governance, implying heightened attention to issues such as AI infrastructure, gender rights online, and broader digital‑policy debates【2】. |

### Synthesis

- **Scale of Connectivity:** Mobile broadband is the dominant growth driver, with an estimated **4.4 billion** active subscriptions by year‑end, reflecting a massive expansion of the mobile digital economy【4】.
- **Policy & Governance Momentum:** Multiple sources label 2018 as a **tipping point** for Internet governance, indicating that 2018 is expected to see significant policy debates and reforms, especially around AI infrastructure, gender‑rights online, and broader digital‑policy frameworks【2】【6】【8】.
- **Technical Evolution:** IPv6 deployment continues to advance, with the Internet Society’s 2018 report documenting broader adoption across countries and service providers, signaling progress toward a more future‑proof Internet architecture【5】.
- **Affordability & Development:** The 2018 Affordability Report highlights the link between wider, cheaper Internet access and economic growth, reinforcing the need for policies that lower cost barriers【7】.
- **Strategic Recommendations:** The UN Broadband Commission’s 2018 report outlines concrete actions for governments and stakeholders—leadership, training, universal‑service reforms, digital‑skill building, legal‑framework adaptation, and tax reductions—to sustain and accelerate broadband expansion【4】.

**Overall picture:** 2018 is portrayed as a pivotal year in which broadband reach, especially via mobile networks, expands dramatically; technical foundations (IPv6) mature; and policy discussions intensify, all within a broader agenda to make the Internet more affordable, inclusive, and supportive of sustainable development.

| Agreement | Main purpose / scope | Key elements highlighted in the texts |
|-----------|---------------------|----------------------------------------|
| **Stabilisation and Association Agreement (SAA) with the European Union** | A political‑economic treaty that brings Serbia closer to the EU by aligning Serbian legislation and institutions with EU standards and by supporting regional stability. | • Commitment to the UN Charter, OSCE Helsinki Final Act, Madrid & Vienna conference documents, the Paris Charter and the Stability Pact for South‑Eastern Europe. <br>• Emphasis on free‑market principles, sustainable development and EU‑backed economic reforms. <br>• Obligation for Serbia to approximate its legislation to that of the Community (EU) in relevant sectors (e.g., oenological rules) and to contribute to political, economic and institutional stability in the country and the region【1†L1-L9】【6†L1-L4】. |
| **Free Trade Agreement (FTA) between the EFTA States (Iceland, Liechtenstein, Norway, Switzerland) and Serbia** | A comprehensive free‑trade area that liberalises trade in goods, services, investment, government procurement and intellectual‑property protection, while embedding sustainable‑development and labour‑rights objectives. | • Goal to liberalise trade in goods in line with GATT 1994 (Art. XXIV) and to expand trade in services and investment opportunities【2†L2-L5】. <br>• Guarantees fair competition, protection of intellectual‑property rights and gradual opening of government‑procurement markets【2†L5-L7】. <br>• Explicit commitment to sustainable development, and to the coherence of trade, environment and labour policies【5†L1-L2】. <br>• Reference to ILO obligations – Serbia (and the EFTA parties) must implement ratified ILO conventions and work toward ratifying the fundamental conventions【3†L1-L3】. <br>• The FTA, together with complementary agricultural agreements, establishes a free‑trade area aimed at “spurring prosperity and sustainable development”【2†L1-L3】. |

**In short:**  
- The **Stabilisation and Association Agreement** is Serbia’s EU‑oriented framework for political, economic and legislative convergence.  
- The **Free Trade Agreement with the EFTA States** creates a free‑trade area that liberalises trade, investment and procurement while embedding sustainable‑development, environmental and labour standards.  

Both accords are designed to integrate Serbia more closely with European markets and institutions, promote stable and sustainable growth, and align Serbian law and practice with EU/EFTA norms.

</details>

It covers political, economic, institutional and sector‑specific cooperation and sets out a road‑map for aligning Serbian law with the EU acquis. | • Full implementation of the UN Charter, OSCE Helsinki Final Act and other European security‑cooperation documents.<br>• Approximation of Serbian legislation to EU law in the “relevant sectors” (e.g., oenological rules) [1][6].<br>• Contribution to political, economic and institutional stability in Serbia and the wider region [6].<br>• Alignment of bilateral relations with the EU‑Member‑State relationship model [7]. |

### How the two agreements fit together
* **Political‑security dimension** – The SAA ties Serbia to the EU’s broader European‑security architecture (UN Charter, OSCE, Helsinki Final Act, Stability Pact, etc.) and to the EU‑wide “European Partnership” that identifies priority reforms for Serbia [1].

### In short

1. **A Stabilisation and Association Agreement with the EU**, which obliges Serbia to align its legislation with EU law, support regional stability and pursue EU‑defined political and economic reforms.

### Q12: What is Diplos position on AI governance?

| | Baseline | Graph-Enriched |
|---|---|---|

| Score avg | 4.2/5 | 5.0/5 |
| Expanded docs | — | 3 |

> **Judge:** Answer A covers all major themes, adds broader strategic context, and cites a wider range of sources, making it more complete and specific while remaining accurate.

<details><summary>Baseline answer</summary>

Diplo’s stance on AI governance can be summed up in three inter‑linked pillars that repeatedly appear across its work and public statements:

1. **Human‑rights‑centred, philosophically informed regulation**  
   - The Diplo Foundation’s Executive Director, Jovan Kurbalija, repeatedly stresses that AI‑governance frameworks must move beyond a narrow focus on “data” and instead be grounded in broader philosophical questions about what it means to be human and how technology should serve society [1].  
   - This perspective is echoed in the 2023 IGF session “Bottom‑up AI and the right to be humanly imperfect,” where Kurbalija highlighted the need to blend philosophy, ethics and cultural context when shaping AI rules [7].

2. **Multistakeholder, rights‑based, and data‑balanced approach**  
   - In the IGF 2025 “Diplo/GIP” session, Diplo called for AI systems that **uphold human rights**, **reflect diverse perspectives**, and are built on **balanced data** rather than on a single dominant dataset [2].  
   - The same session stressed the importance of strengthening multistakeholder fora (like the IGF) by creating incentives and formats that draw broader, more active participation from governments, the private sector, civil society and academia [2].

3. **Capacity‑building and practical integration into diplomacy**  
   - Diplo’s AI Lab, launched in 2018, is described as a “multifaceted initiative” that conducts research, produces policy analysis, and delivers capacity‑development programmes to help diplomats understand and shape AI policy [3].  
   - The Lab also partners with other institutions to explore AI as a diplomatic tool, a negotiation topic, and a factor that reshapes the diplomatic environment [3].  
   - This practical focus is reinforced by Diplo’s broader mission to govern AI, the internet and emerging technologies, positioning human wisdom alongside AI power [4].

**In short, Diplo advocates for an AI‑governance model that is:**  

- **Human‑rights‑centric and philosophically reflective**, questioning data‑centric dominance;  
- **Inclusive and multistakeholder**, ensuring diverse voices and balanced data shape rules; and  
- **Supported by capacity‑building and diplomatic integration**, so that policymakers worldwide can implement and enforce those rules effectively.  

These elements together define Diplo’s overall position on AI governance.  

**Sources:** [1] [2] [3] [4] [7]

</details>

<details><summary>Graph-enriched answer</summary>

Diplo (through the Diplo Foundation, its AI Lab and the Global Internet Policy (GIP) programme) frames AI governance as a **multistakeholder, rights‑based endeavour that must balance global coordination with local realities**.  

* **Philosophical and human‑centred focus** – Jovan Kurbalija, the Foundation’s Executive Director, repeatedly asks whether the current “data‑first” model is adequate and stresses the need to bring philosophy, culture and the right to be imperfect into AI policy discussions【1†L1-L3】【7†L1-L4】.  

* **Human‑rights and diversity as core criteria** – The 2025 IGF session highlighted that AI systems should “uphold human rights, reflect diverse perspectives, and be built on balanced data”【2†L1-L4】.  

* **Inclusive multistakeholder governance** – Diplo stresses the importance of the private sector, civil society and other actors in shaping AI rules and calls for “enhanc[ing] the relevance and impact of multistakeholder forums—such as the IGF—in AI governance”【2†L4-L7】.  

* **Capacity‑building and diplomatic integration** – The AI Lab’s mandate includes research, capacity development, and analysis of AI’s impact on diplomacy, positioning AI as both a diplomatic tool and a negotiation topic【3†L1-L4】.  

* **Strategic alignment with broader internet and development goals** – Diplo’s work on AI is situated within its wider agenda on internet governance, digital standards and sustainable development, linking AI policy to overall digital infrastructure and development objectives【4†L1-L2】【6†L1-L3】.  

**In short, Diplo’s position is that AI governance should be:**  

1. **Multistakeholder and inclusive**, drawing on governments, the private sector, academia and civil society.  
2. **Grounded in human‑rights and ethical principles**, ensuring AI respects diverse cultural contexts and the “right to be imperfect.”  
3. **Balanced between global coordination and local needs**, avoiding a one‑size‑fits‑all data‑centric model.  
4. **Integrated with diplomatic practice and capacity‑building**, so that AI becomes a constructive element of foreign‑policy decision‑making.  

These points together capture Diplo’s overarching stance on AI governance as reflected in the cited materials.

</details>

| Expanded docs | — | 5 |

> **Judge:** Answer A covers the key facets of digital diplomacy more thoroughly, cites sources for each point, and provides concrete platform examples, giving it higher completeness, specificity, and source usage.

<details><summary>Baseline answer</summary>

Digital diplomacy is the practice of using digital technologies—principally the internet and social‑media platforms—to conduct diplomatic activities and pursue foreign‑policy objectives.  It encompasses a wide range of actions, such as communicating official messages and managing a country’s image online, holding virtual meetings and negotiations, creating digital platforms for public‑diplomacy and cultural exchange, and addressing new agenda items that arise from the digital age (e.g., privacy, data protection, cybersecurity).  In essence, digital diplomacy represents the transformation of traditional diplomatic tools through the adoption of networked, online technologies, complementing conventional methods while reshaping how diplomats engage with foreign publics and other states【3†L1-L4】【7†L1-L4】.

</details>

<details><summary>Graph-enriched answer</summary>

**Digital diplomacy** – also referred to as e‑diplomacy or web diplomacy – is the practice of using digital technologies—principally the Internet and social‑media platforms—to conduct diplomatic activities and pursue foreign‑policy objectives.  It encompasses:

* **Communication and public outreach** – posting statements, engaging audiences, and managing a country’s image and reputation on platforms such as Twitter, Facebook, Instagram, etc. [3][7]  
* **Virtual negotiations and mediation** – holding meetings, negotiations and conflict‑resolution processes online, often with the aid of collaborative digital tools. [4]  
* **Agenda‑setting on emerging digital issues** – bringing topics such as privacy, data protection, cybersecurity, and broader internet‑governance onto the diplomatic agenda. [4][7]  
* **Complementing traditional diplomacy** – acting alongside conventional diplomatic instruments (embassies, official visits, etc.) to “manage change through digital tools and virtual collaborations.” [4][5]  

In short, digital diplomacy is the strategic use of the internet, social media, and related digital platforms to communicate, negotiate, and shape policy in the networked, data‑driven environment of contemporary international relations. [3][4][7]

</details>

### Q19: ITU’s core mandate is to ‘ensure networks and technologies

| | Baseline | Graph-Enriched |

| Score avg | 5.0/5 | 4.2/5 |

> **Judge:** Answer A covers all key aspects, provides detailed bullet points and multiple citations, making it more complete, specific, and better sourced than the briefer Answer B.

<details><summary>Baseline answer</summary>

ITU’s core mandate is to **ensure that the world’s telecommunications networks and the underlying technologies work together in a coordinated, interoperable and universally‑accessible way**.  It does this by:

* **Coordinating the shared global use of the radio‑frequency spectrum and satellite orbits** – the “air‑waves” that all wireless networks rely on【4】.  
* **Developing and promoting voluntary technical standards and regulations** that make devices, networks and services interoperable across borders【5】【6】.  
* **Facilitating affordable and universal access to ICT infrastructure**, especially in developing countries, so that the benefits of broadband, wireless, satellite and other communications technologies can be realised for social, economic and environmental development【1】【2】【3】.  

In short, the ITU’s mission is to “promote, facilitate and foster affordable and universal access to telecommunication/information and communication technology networks, services and applications… for sustainable growth,” which reflects its core role of ensuring that networks and technologies are globally compatible, accessible and sustainably deployed【1】.

</details>

<details><summary>Graph-enriched answer</summary>

ITU’s core mandate is to **ensure that telecommunications networks and technologies are globally interoperable, universally accessible and seamlessly interconnected**. The Union coordinates the shared use of the radio‑spectrum, assigns satellite orbits, sets voluntary technical standards and international regulations, and works to improve communications infrastructure—especially in developing countries—so that devices, networks and services can inter‑connect and be affordable for all users [4][5][6].

</details>

> **Judge:** Answer A offers a more thorough, detailed overview with concrete examples and a well‑structured citation table, while both are accurate.

<details><summary>Baseline answer</summary>

A **multistakeholder platform for dialogue** is a convening space that deliberately brings together a broad range of actors — government representatives, private‑sector companies, civil‑society organisations, technical experts and academia — so they can discuss, negotiate and co‑create policies or solutions on a given issue.  

The sources repeatedly describe such platforms as the **primary mechanism for inclusive, cross‑sectoral conversation**:

* **WS #97** calls the multistakeholder platform “the most important … you should use it as a platform to have a dialogue … with each other”【1】.  
* **WS #137** stresses that “we need the dialogue” and frames it as a multistakeholder effort【5】.  
* **WS #64** notes that there is “no better platform … for a true example of multi‑stakeholder interaction”【7】.  
* The **Internet Governance Forum (IGF) model** is repeatedly cited as a concrete example of such a platform, used to “facilitate dialogue across different stakeholder groups”【4】【6】 and to “provide a platform for direct exchange between policymakers, regulators, businesses and civil society”【3】.  
* Networking sessions (e.g., **Networking Session #37**) highlight that these platforms “facilitate informal networking and dialogue that can lead to better understanding and collaboration” among human‑rights, legal, regulatory, and civil‑society actors【2】.

In short, a multistakeholder platform for dialogue is an inclusive forum—often modeled on the IGF—that enables governments, industry, civil society and other interested parties to meet, share perspectives, and work toward shared outcomes.

</details>

<details><summary>Graph-enriched answer</summary>

A **multistakeholder platform for dialogue** is a convening space that deliberately brings together the full spectrum of actors — government representatives, private‑sector firms, civil‑society groups, technical experts, and sometimes academia — so they can discuss, negotiate, and co‑create policies, standards, or solutions on a given issue.  

Key characteristics drawn from the provided material are:

| Feature | Evidence from the context |
|---------|---------------------------|
| **Inclusive participation** – “private entities, civil society, and government agencies” share the same floor. | [8] “This platform serves as a conduit for dialogue and collaboration among private entities, civil society, and government agencies.” |
| **Informal networking that builds understanding** – enables actors to meet in ways not possible in their home countries. | [2] “These platforms facilitate informal networking and dialogue that can lead to better understanding and collaboration…Mwape highlights how multi‑stakeholder platforms create valuable opportunities for different actors to meet and engage in ways that might not be possible in their home countries.” |
| **Structured around a recognized model** – the Internet Governance Forum (IGF) model is repeatedly cited as a template. | [4] “Using multi‑stakeholder platforms like IGF model to facilitate dialogue across different stakeholder groups.”<br> [6] “Using multi‑stakeholder platforms like the IGF model for dialogue.” |
| **Purpose‑driven sessions** – workshops, networking sessions, and open forums are organized to focus on specific topics (AI governance, illegal content, digital public infrastructure, cyber peace). | [1] “the most important, the multistakeholder platform… you should use it as a platform to have a dialogue.”<br> [3] “As a multi‑stakeholder initiative the digital dialogues provides a platform for direct exchange between policymakers, regulators, businesses and civil society.” |
| **Goal of collaborative outcomes** – the dialogue is seen as essential for designing future digital ecosystems, combating illegal content, and fostering cyber peace. | [5] “We need the dialogue.”<br> [7] “There is no better platform for a true example of multi‑stakeholder interaction.” |

**In short:** a multistakeholder platform for dialogue is any organized forum—often modeled on the IGF—that deliberately assembles diverse stakeholder groups to enable open, balanced, and constructive conversation, thereby producing more inclusive and effective digital policy and governance outcomes.

</details>

<details><summary>Baseline answer</summary>

Sorina Teleanu is a senior digital‑policy expert and diplomat who currently serves as **Director of Knowledge at the DiploFoundation**【6†L1-L2】.  

Her career spans more than 15 years at the intersection of technology and global governance.  From 2011‑2016 she was the **alternate representative of the Romanian Government to ICANN’s Governmental Advisory Committee**【2†L1-L3】, and she has held a range of policy‑making, research, capacity‑development and stakeholder‑engagement roles.  

Key positions and activities include:  

* **Academic and teaching work** – Visiting professor at the College of Europe, where she teaches a course on digital diplomacy【2†L4-L6】.  
* **Multistakeholder governance** – Chair of the Executive Committee of the South‑Eastern European Dialogue on Internet Governance (SEEDIG) and member of the UN Secretary‑General’s Multistakeholder Advisory Group for the Internet Governance Forum (IGF)【2†L4-L6】.  
* **Capacity‑building background** – Alumna of DiploFoundation’s Internet Governance Capacity‑Building Programme, ICANN’s Fellowship Programme (now on the fellowship selection committee), the Internet Society’s Next Generation Leadership programme and the European Summer School on Internet Governance【4†L1-L3】.  
* **National policy experience** – Former advisor to the Romanian Parliament on ICT‑related legislation and policies【4†L4-L5】.  
* **Thought leadership** – Author of a book on the Global Digital Compact and frequent moderator or speaker at international forums on AI, internet governance and satellite technologies【7†L1-L2】【8†L1-L2】.  

In sum, Sorina Teleanu is a Romanian‑born digital diplomat, scholar and policy leader who advises governments, teaches future diplomats, and guides research and capacity‑building on internet and AI governance through her role at DiploFoundation and a broad portfolio of multistakeholder engagements.

</details>

<details><summary>Graph-enriched answer</summary>

Sorina Teleanu is a senior internet‑governance and digital‑policy expert who currently serves as **Director of Knowledge at the DiploFoundation**【5】.  

Her career spans more than 15 years of work at the intersection of technology and global governance. Highlights include:

* **Governmental experience** – From 2011 to 2016 she was the alternate representative of the Romanian Government to ICANN’s Governmental Advisory Committee (GAC)【2】.  
* **Multistakeholder roles** – She has been a member of the UN Secretary‑General’s Multistakeholder Advisory Group for the Internet Governance Forum (IGF) and chaired the Executive Committee of the South‑Eastern European Dialogue on Internet Governance (SEEDIG)【2】.  
* **Academic work** – She is a visiting professor at the College of Europe, where she teaches a course on digital diplomacy【2】.  
* **Capacity‑building and leadership programmes** – She is an alumna of DiploFoundation’s Internet Governance Capacity‑Building Programme, ICANN’s Fellowship Programme (now a member of its selection committee), the Internet Society’s Next Generation Leadership programme, and the European Summer School on Internet Governance【4】.  
* **Public‑facing activities** – She regularly moderates and authors sessions at major fora (e.g., IGF 2023 “Bottom‑up AI and the right to be humanly imperfect”【6】, IGF Parliamentary Track “author of book on Global Digital Compact”【7】, and “Policy Meets Tech: Unpacking satellite technologies”【8】).  

In sum, Sorina Teleanu is a diplomat‑scholar and policy‑maker who combines governmental, multistakeholder, academic, and capacity‑building expertise to shape internet governance, digital diplomacy, and emerging‑technology policy.

</details>

### Q24: what is digital diplomacy?

| | Baseline | Graph-Enriched |

| Expanded docs | — | 5 |

> **Judge:** Answer B covers more dimensions (medium, purpose, scope) and provides clearer categories and examples, while citing sources more thoroughly.

<details><summary>Baseline answer</summary>

Digital diplomacy is the practice of using digital technologies—especially the Internet, social‑media platforms, and other information‑communication tools—to carry out diplomatic activities and achieve foreign‑policy objectives.  It encompasses:

* **The medium** – the Internet, social media, virtual meeting platforms, and other ICTs that enable communication, negotiation, public outreach, and cultural exchange [2][3][4][5][6].  
* **The purpose** – advancing diplomatic goals such as image and reputation management, policy advocacy, crisis response, and the negotiation and representation functions of traditional diplomacy [7][8].  
* **The scope** – not only the use of online tools for outreach (e‑diplomacy) but also the application of diplomatic techniques to manage cyberspace‑related challenges (cyber‑diplomacy), covering new agenda items like privacy, data protection, and cybersecurity [5][7].

In short, digital diplomacy is “the use of the Internet and social media to conduct diplomatic activities and solve foreign‑policy problems,” complementing conventional diplomatic methods with networked, virtual collaborations [4][8].

</details>

<details><summary>Graph-enriched answer</summary>

Digital diplomacy is the practice of using digital technologies—especially the Internet, social‑media platforms and other information‑communication tools—to conduct diplomatic activities and pursue foreign‑policy objectives.  It encompasses the way diplomacy is reshaped by online environments (e.g., new policy topics such as privacy, data protection and cybersecurity), the use of social‑media channels for communication, virtual negotiations and public‑diplomacy outreach, and the creation of digital platforms for cultural exchange and reputation management【2†L1-L4】【4†L1-L5】.  In short, it is “the use of the Internet and social media for achieving diplomatic objectives,” reflecting both a change in the diplomatic environment and the deployment of new digital tools to support traditional diplomatic functions【5†L1-L4】.

</details>

### Q27: what is ping pong diplomacy?

| Expanded docs | — | 5 |

> **Judge:** Both answers fully address the question with accurate, though similarly general, information and comparable citation usage, resulting in equivalent overall scores.

<details><summary>Baseline answer</summary>

Ping‑pong diplomacy is the practice of using sport—specifically table‑tennis matches—as a tool of international diplomacy to ease tensions and open channels of communication between rival states.  

In the early 1970s the United States and the People’s Republic of China arranged an exchange of table‑tennis players. The U.S. team’s visit to China and the ensuing friendly matches were portrayed as a symbolic gesture of goodwill that “broke down barriers” between the two ideologically opposed nations. This informal sporting contact helped pave the way for high‑level diplomatic talks and ultimately the normalisation of U.S.–China relations [2][4].  

The episode is now regarded as a classic example of “sports diplomacy,” showing how a seemingly minor, even accidental, sporting event can foster cultural exchange, mutual understanding, and peace‑building on the international stage [1][3][5].  

In short, ping‑pong diplomacy refers to the 1970s U.S.–China table‑tennis exchanges that acted as a catalyst for diplomatic reconciliation and the restoration of formal relations between the two countries.

</details>

<details><summary>Graph-enriched answer</summary>

Ping‑pong diplomacy is the informal, sport‑based diplomatic initiative that took place in the early 1970s when the United States and the People’s Republic of China exchanged table‑tennis teams.  The visits and friendly matches between American and Chinese players were framed as a symbolic gesture of goodwill that helped break the diplomatic stalemate between the two Cold‑War rivals.  The exchange opened the door to high‑level talks, fostered cultural understanding, and ultimately paved the way for the normalisation of U.S.–China relations [2][4].  

In short, ping‑pong diplomacy is a celebrated example of **sports diplomacy**—the use of sport to promote international understanding, cultural exchange, and peace. [1][3]

</details>

In addition to leading DiploFoundation, he **heads the Geneva Internet Platform (GIP)**, a hub for multi‑stakeholder dialogue on internet policy [1][5][6][8].

He also serves as **Head of the Geneva Internet Platform (GIP)**, the multi‑stakeholder forum that supports the development of a free, open and secure Internet.  

In addition to his leadership roles, Kurbalija is an academic lecturer: he teaches **Internet Governance and Digital Diplomacy** in the MA in Contemporary Diplomacy at the University of Malta and has lectured on diplomacy at the Mediterranean Academy of Diplomatic Studies in Malta. <details><summary>Baseline answer</summary>

The chapter begins by looking at how the United Nations itself first **reacted** to the wave of change that is challenging the multilateral order.  According to the text, the UN’s early response was a mix of practical and policy‑oriented measures that were taken by its various bodies and agencies:

* The UN system “looked beyond the practical and policy responses that emerged from within the UN system and raise questions about the very idea of multilateralism” — highlighting that the first step was an internal assessment of how the organization should adapt to new pressures [1].

* Specific institutional actions are listed in the table of contents: the **UN Secretary‑General’s moral authority**, the **UN Security Council and General Assembly** deliberations, and the work of agencies such as **WHO, UNDP, OCHA**, and others [2].

These internal steps constitute the “initial reactions” – a combination of statements, policy adjustments, and procedural changes aimed at preserving the UN’s role.

At the same time, the chapter points out that this internal activity is taking place amid a **contest‑ation of multilateralism**.  The text notes several external forces that are questioning or undermining the multilateral model:

* A rise in **nationalism, populism and a preference for unilateral action** around the world, which “poses formidable challenges” to the UN and the broader multilateral system [1].

* The broader environment includes “diplomatic hubs being challenged” and a “contested multilateralism” that questions the legitimacy and effectiveness of collective decision‑making [2].

In short, the UN’s initial reactions were an internal scramble to articulate policy, leverage the Secretary‑General’s moral authority, and mobilise its agencies, while the contestation of multilateralism is driven by growing nationalist and unilateral tendencies that threaten the very foundations of collective diplomacy. [1][2]

</details>

<details><summary>Graph-enriched answer</summary>

**Initial reactions from within the UN system and the contestation of multilateralism** refer to the first set of responses that UN bodies and officials produced when the global order began to shift toward rising nationalism, populism and a preference for unilateral action.  

- The chapter opens by **cataloguing the UN’s early policy moves, statements and practical steps** that were meant to defend the multilateral project (e.g., new resolutions, programme adjustments, and diplomatic outreach) [1].  
- At the same time, the authors **question the very premise of multilateralism**, noting that the surge of unilateralism and “nationalist‑populist” politics is challenging the legitimacy and effectiveness of the UN system [1].  
- In the table of contents this theme is singled out as a distinct section (page 9), signalling that the authors will analyse both **the UN’s internal reaction** (through agencies such as WHO, UNDP, OCHA, the moral authority of the Secretary‑General, and the Security Council/General Assembly) **and the broader contestation** of multilateralism by states and other actors [2].  

In short, the “initial reactions” are the UN’s immediate diplomatic, procedural and policy‑level counter‑measures, while the “contestation of multilateralism” captures the growing push‑back from nationalist and unilateral forces that question or undermine the multilateral framework. [1][2]

</details>

<details><summary>Baseline answer</summary>

**Trade‑agreement‑based ways to address WTO‑related problems**

| WTO issue (as described in the excerpts) | Trade‑agreement solution(s) mentioned in the context | How the solution helps |
|---|---|---|
| **Fragmented or inconsistent rules that hinder cross‑border digital commerce** | • Multilateral digital‑trade provisions in WTO‑based agreements (e.g., the TRIPS Agreement, the Information Technology Agreement (ITA and ITA II), the Agreement on Technical Barriers to Trade (TBT))<br>• New “digital trade agreements” that set common standards for data exchange and security | By establishing consistent, predictable rules, these agreements lower barriers for businesses, create a trustworthy environment for data flows, and reduce regulatory uncertainty [1][2][5]. |
| **Unfair trading practices – IP theft, forced technology transfer, industrial subsidies, state‑owned‑enterprise distortions, overcapacity** | • A WTO‑focused working group created by the EU and United States to pursue WTO reform and tackle the listed unfair practices [6] | A dedicated negotiation platform can revise existing rules or add new provisions that curb abusive practices, thereby restoring a level playing field. |
| **General obstacles to trade and lack of a level playing field** | • The WTO’s core function as a forum for negotiating agreements that “reduce obstacles to international trade and ensure a level playing field for all” [3][4] | Ongoing negotiations (e.g., through Ministerial Conferences) produce updated agreements that address emerging trade barriers and promote equitable competition. |
| **Implementation gaps at the regional level** | • Regional trade agreements that support the WTO Trade Facilitation Agreement (TFA) and address specific challenges of member countries [7] | Regional pacts can pilot or complement WTO rules, making it easier for countries to adopt and enforce facilitation measures tailored to their circumstances. |
| **Need for technical assistance and capacity building** | • WTO’s mandate to provide technical assistance, training, and cooperation with other international organisations [5] | Strengthening the capacity of developing members helps them comply with WTO rules and benefit from trade‑related digital agreements. |

### Summary of the “trade‑agreement solutions”

1. **Adopt and expand multilateral digital‑trade provisions** (TRIPS, ITA/ITA II, TBT, GATS, etc.) to create uniform standards for IP, data security, and technology transfer.  
2. **Negotiate new digital‑trade agreements** that explicitly lower cross‑border data‑flow barriers and embed reliable, secure frameworks for data exchange.  
3. **Launch targeted WTO reform working groups** (e.g., the EU‑U.S. initiative) to revise rules that address IP theft, forced technology transfer, subsidies, and state‑owned‑enterprise distortions.  
4. **Leverage regional trade agreements** to implement WTO Trade Facilitation measures where global consensus is still evolving, allowing faster, context‑specific solutions.  
5. **Utilize WTO’s technical‑assistance and capacity‑building programmes** to help all members, especially developing economies, meet the obligations of these agreements and fully participate in the digital economy.

Together, these agreement‑based approaches aim to reduce trade obstacles, ensure a level playing field, and build the trust needed for a thriving global digital marketplace.

</details>

<details><summary>Graph-enriched answer</summary>

**Trade‑agreement‑based ways to address the WTO’s current challenges**

| What the solution does | How it works (trade‑agreement mechanism) | WTO‑related source |
|------------------------|-------------------------------------------|--------------------|
| **Create a level playing field** | Negotiated multilateral accords set common rules that limit “unfair” practices such as forced technology transfer, industrial subsidies or state‑owned‑enterprise distortions. | 【6】 |
| **Reduce trade‑obstacle “friction”** | The WTO’s ministerial conferences provide a forum where members can negotiate new or revised agreements that lower tariffs, harmonise standards and simplify customs procedures. | 【3】, 【4】 |
| **Provide predictable, transparent rules** | Core WTO texts – the Marrakesh Agreement, GATT, GATS, the Information‑Technology Agreements (ITA I & II), the Technical Barriers to Trade (TBT) Agreement and the TRIPS Agreement – codify the rights and obligations of members, making the system more stable for businesses. | 【5】 |
| **Facilitate digital trade and data flows** | New digital‑trade provisions (often added to existing agreements or negotiated in “digital trade” chapters) establish secure, reliable frameworks for cross‑border data exchange, thereby lowering barriers for e‑commerce and cloud services. | 【1】, 【2】 |
| **Strengthen dispute‑settlement and legal certainty** | The WTO Dispute Settlement System, described as a “cornerstone” of the multilateral trading system, gives members a rule‑based avenue to resolve conflicts, which in turn boosts confidence in the overall regime. | 【8】 |
| **Address sector‑specific or regional challenges** | Regional or sector‑specific agreements (e.g., climate‑smart trade facilitation, regional trade‑facilitation measures) can pilot solutions that later feed into WTO‑wide reforms. | 【7】 |
| **Launch a reform work‑group** | By establishing a dedicated working group, WTO members can systematically review and amend the organization’s rules to tackle emerging issues such as IP theft, overcapacity and state‑driven market distortions. | 【6】 |

**In short:**  
Trade‑agreement solutions to WTO‑related problems centre on (1) negotiating and updating multilateral accords that set uniform, transparent rules (GATT, GATS, TRIPS, ITA, TBT, etc.); (2) using digital‑trade chapters to lower data‑flow barriers and build trust; (3) leveraging the WTO’s dispute‑settlement mechanism for predictability; (4) employing regional or sector‑specific pacts to test targeted reforms; and (5) creating a dedicated WTO reform working group to address unfair practices and structural imbalances. These mechanisms together aim to reduce obstacles, ensure a level playing field, and sustain confidence in the global trading system.

</details>

<details><summary>Baseline answer</summary>

India’s AI politics can be described as a coordinated, state‑driven effort to make artificial‑intelligence a pillar of the country’s **economic, security and informational power**, while shaping an *independent* global governance stance that draws on its existing digital public‑infrastructure strengths.

| Dimension | Core political thrust | How it is being pursued |
|-----------|----------------------|------------------------|
| **Economic productivity** | AI is seen as a catalyst for growth and a way to boost the nation’s competitiveness. | The government plans to deliver **free AI‑driven services** – such as virtual doctors, tutors and agronomists – to every citizen through the **Aadhaar identity stack** before opening the market to commercial, disruptive applications【1†L1-L3】. |
| **Military / defence** | AI is framed as essential to “military power” and national security. | Policy discussions (e.g., the Invest India Fireside Chat) repeatedly stress AI’s role in defence and information control【4†L1】. |
| **Information control** | Controlling the flow of data and narratives is a strategic objective. | The same AI‑enabled service rollout and the emphasis on “information control” are part of a broader state‑led data‑governance agenda【4†L1】. |
| **Global AI governance positioning** | India wants a **balanced, sovereign** stance – not as restrictive as the EU, not as laissez‑faire as the US, and not as state‑dominated as China. | The “Strategic AI Positioning” dialogue highlights leveraging India’s proven digital public‑infrastructure (UPI, digital identity) to craft a **home‑grown governance model** while avoiding the pitfalls of other major regimes【3†L1-L3】【6†L1-L3】. |
| **Ethical & sectoral regulation** | Development of sector‑specific safeguards on privacy, ethics and security. | The National AI Strategy calls for **domain‑specific ethical councils** that will draft guidelines for each industry (e.g., finance, health)【5†L1】. |
| **Infrastructure & R&D support** | Direct state investment to build a sovereign AI stack. | Policies include **free GPU allocations, funding for large‑scale models, and energy‑infrastructure upgrades** to sustain AI research and deployment【7†L1-L3】. |

### Summarized picture of “India AI politics”

1. **Strategic priority** – AI is treated as a national‑interest technology that underpins growth, defence and narrative control.  
2. **Public‑service first** – The state will initially use AI to deliver free, Aadhaar‑linked services to citizens, establishing a broad user base and data foundation.  
3. **Sovereign governance model** – India aims to craft its own AI regulatory framework, drawing on its digital‑identity and payments successes, while deliberately steering a middle path between the EU, US and Chinese models.  
4. **Institutional safeguards** – Sector‑specific ethical councils will shape privacy, security and ethical rules, ensuring that AI deployment aligns with national values and international best practices.  
5. **State‑backed infrastructure** – Direct provision of compute resources, funding, and energy capacity is intended to nurture a domestic AI ecosystem that can compete globally.

In short, **India’s AI politics** is a state‑led, multi‑layered strategy that couples ambitious public‑service deployment with a sovereign, balanced regulatory approach, all aimed at cementing AI as a cornerstone of the nation’s economic, defence and informational capabilities.

</details>

<details><summary>Graph-enriched answer</summary>

**India’s AI politics can be described as a “strategic‑national‑interest” approach that blends state‑driven service delivery with a globally‑balanced governance model.**  

| Dimension | What the policy says | Political implication |

| **Citizen‑centric service delivery** | The strategy calls for **free AI‑powered public services** (e.g., doctors, tutors, agronomists) delivered through the **Aadhaar digital‑identity stack** before any commercial‑disruptive applications are launched【1†L1-L3】【2†L1-L3】. | AI is positioned as a welfare tool, reinforcing the political narrative that the state can provide high‑value services directly to the populace, thereby gaining political legitimacy and social trust. |
| **Leveraging existing digital public infrastructure** | India’s “successful digital public infrastructure experience” – UPI, digital identity, etc. – is to be the backbone for AI governance, giving the country a comparative advantage【3†L1-L3】【6†L1-L3】. | Politically, this underscores a continuity narrative: the same state‑run platforms that have driven financial inclusion are now the foundation for AI, reinforcing the idea of a “digital sovereign” ecosystem. |
| **Balanced regulatory posture** | The policy explicitly seeks a middle way, **avoiding the overly restrictive European model, the laissez‑faire American approach, and the state‑controlled Chinese system**【3†L1-L3】【6†L1-L3】. | This signals a diplomatic stance that India wants to be a global AI governance leader, shaping standards that suit its own model while remaining attractive to international partners. |
| **Domain‑specific ethical oversight** | The National AI Strategy calls for **domain‑specific ethical councils** to draft sector‑specific guidelines on privacy, ethics and security【5†L1-L2】. | Politically, this creates institutional bodies that can be staffed with technocrats and policymakers, allowing the government to claim “responsible AI” while retaining policy control. |
| **State‑backed R&D and infrastructure** | The government will provide **free GPUs, funding for AI models, and energy‑infrastructure support** to build a “full‑stack sovereign AI” ecosystem【7†L1-L3】. | This reflects a political commitment to nurture a domestic AI industry, reduce dependence on foreign cloud providers, and generate strategic employment and export opportunities. |
| **Strategic global positioning** | India aims to **position itself globally in AI governance** by showcasing its digital‑public‑infrastructure expertise and by charting an independent regulatory path【3†L1-L3】【6†L1-L3】. | This is a diplomatic move to claim a seat at the table of international AI rule‑making, enhancing India’s soft power and economic leverage. |

### In short
India’s AI politics is a **state‑centric, strategically‑driven agenda** that:

1. **Treats AI as a national security and development priority** (economy, defence, information control).  
2. **Delivers free, AI‑enabled public services via Aadhaar**, reinforcing the welfare narrative.  
3. **Builds on existing sovereign digital infrastructure (UPI, Aadhaar)** to create a home‑grown AI ecosystem.  
4. **Adopts a “middle‑ground” regulatory philosophy**, rejecting the extremes of Europe, the US and China.  
5. **Institutes sector‑specific ethical councils** to manage privacy, ethics and security.  
6. **Invests in hardware, funding and energy** to develop a sovereign AI stack.  
7. **Seeks a leadership role in global AI governance** that reflects India’s unique model.

All of these elements together constitute what can be called **“India’s AI politics”: a blend of strategic state control, welfare‑oriented service provision, and a calibrated, internationally‑engaged regulatory stance.**

### A concise roadmap (derived from the WAIGF messages)

1. **Define a shared vision** – Adopt the inclusive, human‑centric, resilient, and safe digital future narrative that was repeatedly affirmed ( [1] , [2] , [4] ).

| Pillar | What it means | How to act on it (from the WAIGF discussions) |
|--------|---------------|-----------------------------------------------|
| **1. Inclusive participation** | Ensure that all groups—women, youth, persons with disabilities, rural communities, and marginalized minorities—can meaningfully engage in digital policy‑making and service design. | • Build capacity and expertise among diverse stakeholders so they can participate effectively [7]. <br>• Highlight local and national initiatives that already promote inclusive access [6]. |

| **4. Cross‑sector collaboration** | No single actor can deliver a safe, inclusive digital ecosystem alone; coordinated effort is essential. | • Establish formal mechanisms for joint planning, data‑sharing and policy alignment among governments, industry, civil society and academia [3][5]. <br>• Encourage high‑level dialogue (e.g., the High‑Level Youth IGF) to keep momentum and accountability [5][8]. |

### Concrete Steps for Stakeholders

1. **Governments**  
   - Draft and enforce policies that mandate accessibility standards for digital services.  
   - Allocate budget for digital literacy programmes targeting underserved groups.  
   - Create multi‑stakeholder advisory bodies that include youth and civil‑society representatives [5].

2. **Private Sector**  
   - Integrate inclusive design principles into product development cycles.  
   - Share best‑practice security frameworks with smaller firms and startups.  
   - Invest in community‑based pilots that test inclusive, resilient solutions before scaling [3].

3. **Civil Society & Citizens**  
   - Organise capacity‑building workshops to empower local actors to engage in policy debates [7].  
   - Monitor and report on rights‑respecting implementation of digital services.  
   - Participate in open forums to voice community needs and co‑create solutions [6].

4. **Regional Bodies & International Partners**  
   - Provide technical assistance and funding for cross‑border resilience projects (e.g., shared threat‑intelligence platforms).  
   - Harmonise regulatory frameworks to facilitate safe data flows while protecting user rights.  

### Why This Matters

The WAIGF repeatedly emphasized that **“building a digital future that’s inclusive, human‑centric and resilient”** is not a slogan but a collective responsibility [1][2][4][5][8]. By aligning policy, technology, and capacity‑building around the four pillars above, West Africa—and any region aiming for a safe digital ecosystem—can move from aspirational rhetoric to concrete, measurable outcomes.

</details>

During the first wave of the coronavirus pandemic Norway was already “well‑positioned to meet the crisis,” and the health emergency quickly turned into a catalyst that sped up the country’s digital transition.  After the March 2020 lockdown, “the digitalisation of society and the business sector has accelerated in a number of areas” [2].  The government framed this momentum in a dedicated report – **Our new digital world: Digitalisation in Norway during the coronavirus pandemic** – which maps how the pandemic spurred rapid change across the public sector and key industries such as aquaculture, maritime, agriculture and tourism [1].

Key points from the report include:

* **A pioneering public sector** – Norway’s government bodies were among the first to adopt remote‑working tools, digital service delivery and data‑driven decision‑making, helping keep essential services running while physical contact was limited [1].
* **Sector‑wide digital transformation** – The crisis accelerated digital adoption in traditional Norwegian industries.  For example, aquaculture farms deployed remote monitoring, the maritime sector expanded electronic documentation, and agriculture and tourism businesses turned to online platforms to stay connected with customers [1].
* **Long‑term outlook** – The pandemic is described as a “catalyst for digitalisation” that not only addressed immediate pandemic‑related challenges but also laid the groundwork for a “road ahead” in which digital tools become embedded in Norway’s economic and social fabric [1].

In short, COVID‑19 acted as a trigger that pushed Norway’s already strong digital foundation into a rapid, whole‑society transformation, with the public sector leading the way and a wide range of industries quickly adopting new digital solutions. [1][2]

</details>

During the first wave of the coronavirus pandemic Norway was already “well‑positioned to meet the crisis,” and the shock of the lockdown in March 2020 turned the pandemic into a powerful catalyst for digitalisation across the country [1].  

* **Rapid acceleration** – The pandemic “accelerated the digitalisation of society and the business sector” in a number of areas, as organisations and citizens were forced to move work, services and commerce online [2].  

* **Public‑sector leadership** – Norway’s government framed itself as a “pioneering public sector,” using the emergency to push forward a broader digital transformation agenda that would later be embedded in the country’s long‑term strategy [1].  

* **Policy backing** – Minister of Digitalisation and Public Governance Karianne Tung repeatedly stressed that “digitalisation is really the tool we need to solve huge societal challenges,” positioning the country as “one of the most digitalised countries in the world” and linking the pandemic response to a wider vision of responsible AI and digital governance [6].  

In short, the COVID‑19 crisis did not just force temporary online work in Norway; it amplified an already strong digital foundation, spurred a nationwide push for smarter public services, and reinforced the political narrative that digital tools are essential for tackling large‑scale societal problems.

> **Judge:** Answer B covers a broader set of strategic dimensions (economic, security, information) and provides slightly more concrete details while maintaining comparable accuracy and citation quality.

<details><summary>Baseline answer</summary>

India’s AI strategy is being shaped around a **sovereign, inclusive, and productivity‑driven** model that builds on the country’s existing digital public‑infrastructure (DPI) and avoids the extremes of other global approaches.

**1. Three‑pillar foundation**  
- **Energy sovereignty** – securing cheap, reliable power for large‑scale compute.  
- **Compute & cloud sovereignty** – investing in home‑grown high‑performance compute clusters and cloud platforms.  
- **Services sovereignty** – delivering AI‑enabled public services that are owned and controlled by India.  

These pillars were highlighted by Jeet Adani in a keynote presentation [7].

**2. Leveraging DPI and low‑cost, high‑performing models**  
- India plans to use its Aadhaar identity stack and other DPI (e.g., UPI, digital identity) to roll out free AI services such as virtual doctors, tutors, and agronomists for every citizen before commercialising disruptive applications [1][3].  
- Rather than chasing the trillion‑parameter “AGI race,” the strategy emphasizes **high‑performant, low‑cost models** in the 30‑200 billion‑parameter range, which can be run efficiently on sovereign compute resources [6].

**3. Economic, security, and information goals**  
- AI is seen as a driver of **economic productivity**, a tool for **military power**, and a means of **information control** (e.g., managing misinformation) [1][3][4].  
- The approach seeks to turn AI into a public good that boosts growth while maintaining strategic autonomy.

**4. Governance and regulatory posture**  
- India wants to position itself globally as a **balanced AI governance model**—learning from the successes of its digital infrastructure (UPI, Aadhaar) while steering clear of overly restrictive European rules, laissez‑faire U.S. models, or state‑centric Chinese systems [2].  
- An **agile, risk‑based regulatory framework** is proposed for sectors like banking, ensuring safety without stifling innovation [2].

**5. Skills, collaboration, and data access**  
- The strategy calls for massive **skilling programmes**, industry‑academia collaboration, and **streamlined access to high‑quality data sets** to fuel research and innovation [5][8].

**In short**, India’s AI roadmap is built on sovereign energy, compute, and service capabilities; it leverages its existing digital identity and payments infrastructure to deliver free, high‑impact AI services; it focuses on modest‑size, cost‑effective models; and it seeks a balanced, multistakeholder governance model that promotes inclusive growth while safeguarding strategic interests [1][2][5][6][7][8].

</details>

<details><summary>Graph-enriched answer</summary>

India’s AI strategy is being framed as a **sovereign, people‑first ecosystem** that couples massive public‑sector infrastructure with a pragmatic, low‑cost model‑centric research agenda.  The key elements that repeatedly appear in the source material are:

1. **Three‑pillar sovereign foundation** – the government is building “energy sovereignty, compute and cloud sovereignty, and services sovereignty” as the bedrock for AI development and deployment【7】.  This means investing in domestic data‑centres, renewable‑energy‑backed power supplies, and home‑grown cloud platforms that can host AI workloads without relying on foreign providers.

2. **Leverage existing digital public infrastructure** – India plans to reuse the Aadhaar identity stack and the country’s proven digital‑payments system (UPI) to deliver **free AI‑powered public services** such as virtual doctors, tutors and agronomists to every citizen before commercial‑grade applications are rolled out【1】【3】.  The same “digital public infrastructure experience” (UPI, digital identity) is cited as a model for positioning India globally in AI governance, avoiding the extremes of Europe’s heavy regulation, the U.S. laissez‑faire stance, or China’s state‑controlled approach【2】.

3. **Focus on performant, low‑cost models** – instead of chasing the trillion‑parameter “AGI” race, India is concentrating on “highly‑performant, low‑cost models (30‑200 billion parameters)” that can run efficiently on sovereign compute resources and be affordable for mass adoption【6】.

4. **Three‑pillars of growth beyond infrastructure** – a broader “three‑pillar AI strategy” is also described as (i) **investing in sovereign compute infrastructure and energy**, (ii) **innovating through skilling and collaboration**, and (iii) **evolving governance frameworks with agile regulations**【5】.  This adds a human‑capital and policy‑flexibility dimension to the hardware‑first focus.

5. **Data‑set accessibility for research** – streamlined access to high‑quality datasets is highlighted as a specific component of the strategy, enabling domestic researchers and startups to innovate quickly【8】.

In sum, India’s AI roadmap combines **national‑scale, sovereign infrastructure (energy, compute, cloud, services)**, **leveraging the Aadhaar/UPI digital backbone to deliver free AI services**, **prioritizing modest‑size, cost‑effective models**, and **building an agile governance and talent ecosystem** that can compete globally while staying rooted in public‑interest outcomes.

</details>

5. **Sustained activity** – References in 2016 and the continued availability of registration contacts indicate that the platform has remained active beyond its launch year.

In sum, the Geneva Internet Platform was launched on **8 April 2014** as a Swiss‑backed, DiploFoundation‑run initiative designed to create a neutral, inclusive forum for digital policy makers, with a particular emphasis on bringing smaller and developing nations into the Geneva digital‑governance conversation and supporting evidence‑based research. ### Core purpose (as defined at launch)

- **Neutral, inclusive forum** for digital‑policy debates in International Geneva.  
- **Support for small and developing countries** to take part in Geneva‑based digital policy processes.  
- **Bridge across policy silos** (e.g., between multistakeholder IG bodies, ICT ministries, and civil‑society groups).  
- **Monitor and analyse digital policies** to provide data for evidence‑based, multidisciplinary research.  
- **Develop tools and methods** for both in‑situ and online engagement that could be replicated in other policy spaces worldwide [5][7].

### Governance & ownership

- **Operated by DiploFoundation**, a non‑profit organization that trains diplomats and digital‑policy practitioners.  
- **Swiss‑backed** (the platform is described as a “Swiss initiative”) and situated within the vibrant ecosystem of Geneva‑based Internet governance institutions [5][6][7].

### Evolution

Since its 2014 launch, GIP has moved from an inaugural event to an ongoing platform that:

1. **Continues to host and curate digital‑policy discussions**, keeping the original mission of inclusive, evidence‑based dialogue alive.  
2. **Expands its thematic scope**, now also addressing gender‑rights‑online and other human‑rights dimensions of Internet governance (as reflected in later graph tags).  
3. **Provides practical tools** for both physical and virtual engagement, aiming to be a model for other international policy venues.

In short, the Geneva Internet Platform began in April 2014 as a Swiss‑funded, DiploFoundation‑run initiative to bring together diverse digital actors in Geneva, and over the subsequent years it has solidified its role as a neutral hub for monitoring, researching, and shaping Internet governance and digital policy worldwide [1][2][5][6][8].
