### Q43: How does AI affect international negotiations?

| | Baseline | Graph-Enriched |
|---|---|---|
| Time | 4.69s | 5.07s (+0.11s expansion) |
| Score avg | 4.2/5 | 4.8/5 |
| Expanded docs | — | 3 |
| **Winner** |  | **✓** |

> **Judge:** B covers all key impacts plus adds inclusivity, providing slightly richer detail while remaining accurate and well‑cited.

<details><summary>Baseline answer</summary>

AI is reshaping the way states negotiate by  

* **Mapping and tracking positions in real‑time** – In the UNESCO negotiation of the “Recommendation on the Ethics of Artificial Intelligence,” diplomats could see on a Zoom screen where each of the 193 countries stood on key issues. That visual map made it easier to spot unexpected alignments (e.g., Russia and the UK) and to coordinate outreach [1].

* **Processing massive volumes of input** – The same UNESCO process generated 55 000 public comments. AI was used to ingest, sort and synthesize those comments so that negotiators could work with the full breadth of stakeholder feedback rather than a tiny sample [1].

* **Managing multilateral complexity** – AI tools can automatically track the positions of dozens of parties, scan large bodies of text, and surface historical precedents or counter‑arguments that might otherwise be missed. This reduces the cognitive load on human negotiators and helps keep multilateral talks coherent [6], [8].

* **Augmenting, not replacing, human judgment** – Speakers repeatedly stress that negotiation remains a fundamentally human process. AI is viewed as a “tool for diplomats and negotiators” that can augment human analysis, suggest options, and streamline procedural steps while the ultimate decisions stay with people [2], [3], [7].

* **Opening new agenda items and strategic considerations** – Because AI can surface emerging issues quickly, it puts new topics on the international agenda and can shift geostrategic dynamics. This creates both opportunities (more inclusive, data‑driven dialogue) and concerns (human‑rights protection, bias) that negotiators must address [2], [4], [5].

**In short, AI affects international negotiations by turning them into more data‑rich, transparent, and efficiently managed processes. It helps diplomats track where every participant stands, digest huge comment streams, draw on past precedents, and focus their human expertise on the substantive, value‑laden decisions that only people can make.** [1] [6] [8]

</details>

<details><summary>Graph-enriched answer</summary>

AI is reshaping the way states negotiate by giving diplomats tools that can handle the sheer scale and complexity of modern multilateral talks.

* **Tracking and visualising positions** – In the UNESCO negotiation of the “Recommendation on the Ethics of Artificial Intelligence,” a Zoom‑based view of where 193 countries stood helped the negotiators see alignments that would otherwise have been hidden (e.g., Russia and the UK sharing a stance). AI‑driven dashboards can therefore map the shifting positions of many actors in real time [1].

* **Processing massive comment‑streams** – The same UNESCO process received 55 000 public comments. AI was used to ingest, categorise and synthesize that volume of feedback, turning a flood of text into actionable insights for the final document [1].

* **Managing document overload and historical knowledge** – AI can scan thousands of policy papers, treaty drafts and prior negotiations, surface relevant precedents and flag counter‑arguments, which reduces the manual burden on negotiators and helps them build stronger, evidence‑based arguments [8].

* **Augmenting human judgment** – While negotiation remains a fundamentally human activity, AI is seen as a “tool for diplomats and negotiators” that can augment their work—providing data‑driven suggestions, highlighting gaps, and suggesting compromise language [3][7].

* **Introducing new agenda items and strategic dynamics** – By surfacing emerging technical trends and potential human‑rights implications, AI pushes new topics onto the international agenda and can reshape geostrategic relations, prompting diplomats to address issues that were previously invisible [2].

* **Facilitating co‑creation and inclusivity** – AI‑enabled analysis of diverse regional and disciplinary inputs helped UNESCO develop a recommendation that was “super inclusive,” ensuring that voices from all world regions were reflected in the final text [1].

In sum, AI affects international negotiations by:

1. **Mapping and visualising stakeholder positions** in real time.  
2. **Digesting huge volumes of textual input** (comments, drafts, precedents).  
3. **Providing evidence‑based insights** that support argumentation and compromise.  
4. **Expanding the policy agenda** with new technical and human‑rights considerations.  
5. **Enhancing inclusivity** through systematic integration of worldwide feedback.

These capabilities allow diplomats to navigate more complex, data‑rich negotiations while still retaining the human judgment at the core of diplomatic decision‑making [1][6][8][2].

</details>

### Q45: What is India AI policy

| | Baseline | Graph-Enriched |
|---|---|---|

| Expanded docs | — | 0 |

> **Judge:** Both answers are thorough and accurate, but A presents the information in a clearer, more detailed table and cites sources more explicitly, giving it higher specificity and source usage.

<details><summary>Baseline answer</summary>

**India’s AI policy – a snapshot from the available sources**

1. **National Strategy for Artificial Intelligence (June 2018)**  
   * The first formal government blueprint. It sets out a “strong basis for future regulation of AI” and focuses on applying AI in five priority sectors:  
   – Healthcare  
   – Agriculture  
   – Education  
   – Smart cities & infrastructure  
   – Smart mobility & transportation【1】.  

2. **India AI Mission (announced March 2024)**  
   * A major fiscal commitment – **over US $1.25 billion** – to move from strategy to implementation. The Mission funds four pillars:  
   – **Computing‑infrastructure capacity** (e.g., free GPUs, data‑centre upgrades)  
   – **Skilling & talent development**  
   – **Innovation & indigenous AI models** (e.g., BharatGPT)  
   – **Datasets, safe and trusted AI** (including guidelines on deep‑fakes and generative AI)【1】.  

3. **Regulatory framework – principles, not yet binding law**  
   * India presently has **no AI‑specific legislation that carries the force of law**. Instead, the government is pursuing a **flexible, principles‑based approach** that encourages responsible innovation while leaving space for sector‑specific rules as the technology evolves【1】.  

4. **Supporting legal instruments**  
   * **Digital Personal Data Protection Act (2023)** – provides a data‑privacy foundation that will also shape AI deployments that process personal data【1】.  

5. **Institutional and ecosystem elements**  
   * **IndiaAI portal (launched May 2022)** – a public‑facing website that aggregates AI‑related initiatives, research, datasets and policy updates, acting as a “one‑stop shop” for stakeholders【1】.  
   * **Advisories on deep‑fakes and generative AI** – issued alongside the Mission to guide safe use of emerging generative models【1】.  

6. **Strategic positioning**  
   * Policy discussions (e.g., in banking‑sector papers) stress that India should **leverage its proven digital public‑infrastructure (UPI, Aadhaar, digital identity)** to become a global AI governance model that avoids the extremes of overly restrictive European rules, laissez‑faire U.S. approaches, or state‑controlled Chinese models【3】【5】.  

---

### In brief

India’s AI policy is built around a **strategic, sector‑focused roadmap (the 2018 National Strategy)**, now backed by a **large financial commitment (the 2024 India AI Mission)** to develop infrastructure, talent, and trustworthy AI. The government has adopted a **principles‑based, non‑binding regulatory stance**, complemented by the **Digital Personal Data Protection Act (2023)** and targeted advisories on emerging risks such as deep‑fakes. Institutional tools like the **IndiaAI portal** support transparency and coordination, while the broader vision positions India as a **model for balanced, responsible AI governance** that draws on its existing digital public‑infrastructure strengths【1】【3】.

</details>

<details><summary>Graph-enriched answer</summary>

**India’s AI policy – a snapshot**

| Element | What the policy says / does | Source |
|---------|-----------------------------|--------|
| **National Strategy for Artificial Intelligence** | Adopted in June 2018, it sets the overall vision for AI in India and identifies priority sectors – health‑care, agriculture, education, smart cities & infrastructure, and smart mobility/transport. The strategy is meant to lay the groundwork for future regulation rather than impose binding rules. | [1] |
| **India AI Mission (2024)** | Launched in March 2024 with **US $1.25 billion** earmarked for: <br>• Computing‑infrastructure capacity (e.g., free GPUs for researchers)<br>• Skilling and talent development<br>• Innovation ecosystems and AI‑model funding<br>• Creation of high‑quality datasets<br>• Safe, trustworthy, and responsible AI deployment. | [1] |
| **Regulatory posture** | India **has no binding AI law** yet. Instead it is moving toward a **principles‑based, flexible framework** that encourages responsible innovation while avoiding the heavy‑handedness of some European models, the laissez‑faire of the U.S., or state‑controlled approaches seen elsewhere. | [1] |
| **Advisories & sector‑specific guidance** | Recent government advisories address **deep‑fakes** and **generative AI**, urging developers and users to adopt safeguards and ethical practices. | [1] |
| **Data‑protection backdrop** | The **Digital Personal Data Protection Act (2023)** provides the broader legal environment for personal‑data handling, which underpins many AI‑related activities (training data, model outputs, etc.). | [1] |
| **Public‑access platforms** | **IndiaAI**, a web portal launched in May 2022, aggregates AI‑related initiatives, research, datasets, and policy updates, acting as a single point of reference for stakeholders. | [1] |
| **Strategic positioning** | Policy documents stress leveraging India’s existing **digital public‑infrastructure** (e.g., UPI, Aadhaar) to build trustworthy AI services (free AI‑driven doctors, tutors, agronomists) while maintaining **sovereign control** over critical AI stacks. | [3], [4] |
| **Future regulatory outlook** | The government is drafting **principles for responsible AI** (e.g., transparency, fairness, accountability) that will likely evolve into more formal regulations, but as of the writing **no AI regulation carries the force of law**. | [1] |

### Key take‑aways

1. **Strategic, sector‑focused vision** – AI is seen as a catalyst for growth in health, agriculture, education, smart cities, and mobility.  
2. **Heavy investment in infrastructure & talent** – The 2024 AI Mission’s $1.25 bn budget is earmarked for compute resources, skill‑building, and safe‑AI research.  
3. **Principles‑first, law‑later approach** – India prefers a flexible, principles‑based framework (no binding AI statutes yet) to foster innovation while ensuring trustworthiness.  
4. **Integration with existing digital ecosystem** – AI policy builds on India’s successful digital public‑infrastructure (UPI, Aadhaar) to deliver public‑good AI services.  
5. **Data‑privacy foundation** – The 2023 Digital Personal Data Protection Act provides the legal base for handling personal data in AI projects.  

In short, **India’s AI policy combines a forward‑looking national strategy, substantial public funding, a principles‑based regulatory stance, and integration with its robust digital public‑infrastructure to promote responsible, sovereign AI development**.  

**Sources:** [1] Overview of AI policy in 10 jurisdictions; [3] Secure Finance Risk‑Based AI Policy for the Banking Sector; [4] Invest India Fireside Chat.

**Key fisheries‑related clauses that could be used as reference points in WTO‑level talks for Yaonde**

| Trade agreement (source) | Fisheries provision(s) that are most often cited in WTO‑style negotiations |
|--------------------------|-----------------------------------------------------------------------------|
| **Free Trade Agreement – “considering the importance of the fisheries relationship … the volume of the mutual fisheries possibilities … should continue to be maintained at a satisfactory level”**【1】 | • Explicit separation of the fisheries‑specific regime from the broader trade chapter – a model for “no‑spill‑over” language that assures WTO partners that trade liberalisation will not erode existing fisheries‑co‑operation. |
| **EFTA‑Chile FTA** (Art. e & related paragraphs) | • Commitment to the 2030 Sustainable Development Agenda on fisheries subsidies – prohibition of subsidies that fuel over‑fishing, over‑capacity or IUU (illegal, unreported, unregulated) fishing【2】. <br>• Reference to core multilateral instruments (UNCLOS, UN Fish Stocks Agreement, FAO Compliance Agreement, FAO Port‑State Measures) as the legal baseline【2】. <br>• Promotion of traceability schemes via FAO Voluntary Guidelines for Catch Documentation【2】. |
| **Argentina‑Chile FTA** | • Mutual recognition of the fisheries sector’s socio‑economic importance (including artisanal fisheries) and of the need for conservation and sustainable management for present‑ and future‑generation benefits【3】. |
| **Japan‑Chile Strategic Economic Partnership** | • Detailed “national‑flag” criteria for vessels (ownership, crew, master, officers) that qualify for preferential treatment, plus a dedicated Working Group on Fish and Fishery Products to oversee implementation【4】. |
| **China (Taiwan)‑El Salvador‑Honduras FTA** | • Broad objective to diversify and improve competitiveness of the fishing subsector, and to coordinate policies on agriculture, aquaculture, forestry and fishing for rural development【5】. |
| **Korea‑Colombia FTA** | • Acknowledgement of the social and economic importance of fish and fishery products and a pledge to cooperate in fisheries and aquaculture【6】. |
| **EU‑ACP (Maldives & Mauritius) case study** | • Use of “rules‑of‑origin” compliance to grant tariff‑free access for fish and fish products from ACP states – a practical illustration of how fisheries‑specific origin rules can be built into WTO‑compatible preferential schemes【7】. |
| **WTO‑level discussion on preferences (SIDS study)** | • WTO members are asked to spell out the disciplines they want to improve on, explicitly naming “trade‑distorting practices (including fisheries subsidies)” as a focus for the next negotiation phase【8】. |

### How these clauses can shape Yaonde’s WTO agenda

1. **Maintain a distinct fisheries regime** – Like the clause in [1], Yaonde can ask that any WTO‑wide fisheries provisions be “separate” from the general goods‑trade commitments, preserving existing bilateral/multilateral fisheries agreements.

2. **Sustainable‑development‑linked subsidy discipline** – The EFTA‑Chile language in [2] provides a template for WTO‑level rules that **prohibit** subsidies that contribute to over‑fishing, over‑capacity, or IUU, while allowing “green” subsidies that support conservation.

3. **Reference to multilateral fisheries instruments** – Citing UNCLOS, the UN Fish Stocks Agreement and the FAO Port‑State Measures (as in [2]) gives WTO negotiations a solid legal foundation and signals alignment with the broader ocean‑governance regime.

4. **Traceability and documentation** – The promotion of FAO’s Voluntary Guidelines for Catch Documentation (see [2]) can be introduced as a WTO‑compatible “non‑tariff measure” to improve transparency and combat IUU.

5. **Vessel‑eligibility criteria** – The detailed vessel‑nationality and crew‑composition rules in [4] can be adapted into WTO “origin” or “eligibility” criteria for preferential market access for fish products.

6. **Co‑operation and capacity‑building clauses** – The recognitions of fisheries’ socio‑economic importance in [3], [5] and [6] can be used to argue for technical‑cooperation, capacity‑building, and development‑oriented provisions within the WTO framework, especially for developing or small‑island members.

7. **Preferential market‑access mechanisms** – The EU‑ACP example in [7] shows how “rules‑of‑origin” compliance can be leveraged to grant tariff‑free entry for fish products, a model that could be replicated in WTO “Enabling Clause” arrangements.

8. **Explicit mention of fisheries subsidies in WTO negotiations** – The SIDS study in [8] confirms that WTO members are already framing fisheries subsidies as a “trade‑distorting practice” to be addressed in the next negotiation round, giving Yaonde a ready‑made entry point.

### Bottom line for Yaonde

- **Adopt a “dual‑track” approach:** keep a dedicated fisheries chapter (or annex) that mirrors the separation clause in [1], while embedding sustainability‑linked subsidy bans, traceability, and reference to UNCLOS/FAO instruments as core WTO‑level obligations (modelled on [2]).
- **Use vessel‑nationality and working‑group mechanisms** (from [4]) to define eligibility for any preferential treatment Yaonde may seek.
- **Leverage development‑oriented language** (from [3], [5], [6]) to secure technical assistance and capacity‑building support in WTO negotiations.
- **Highlight the precedent of “rules‑of‑origin”‑based tariff‑free fish imports** (as in [7]) to argue for similar treatment for Yaonde’s fish exports under any future WTO‑wide preferential scheme.

</details>

<details><summary>Baseline answer</summary>

Yes. The Internet Engineering Task Force (IETF) is deliberately structured to be open‑access rather than a traditional membership‑based organization. It does **not** require any formal sign‑up, membership fee, or organizational affiliation; anyone who is interested can join the work as an individual. All participants act as volunteers, contributing on an equal footing and are judged on technical merit rather than on the basis of a formal membership or institutional status.  

Key points from the provided sources:

* The IETF “does not have a formal membership structure, and everyone interested is welcome to participate in the work” [1][2].  
* Participation “does not require a membership fee or any formal sign‑up” and is “accessible to anyone interested in contributing” [3][7].  
* Materials are freely available and “everyone participates as an individual… with equal standing” [4].  
* The organization operates on “principles of individual participation, technical merit‑based decisions, and voluntary adoption,” making it open to all stakeholders regardless of affiliation [5].  
* Both the IETF standards process and the IRTF research groups are “open for participation by all, with no individual or organizational membership requirements” [6].  

Together, these statements confirm that the IETF has **no formal membership requirements**, welcomes **any interested person** to take part on an **equal footing**, and relies on **volunteer contributors** who act as individuals rather than as representatives of a membership body.

</details>

<details><summary>Graph-enriched answer</summary>

Yes. All of the cited material makes clear that the Internet Engineering Task Force (IETF) does **not** have a formal membership structure or fees; participation is open to anyone who wishes to contribute, and contributors act as individual volunteers rather than as representatives of an organization. For example, the IETF “does not have a formal membership structure, and everyone interested is welcome to participate in the work” [1][2]; it “does not require a membership fee or any formal sign‑up” and “this open participation model … makes it accessible to anyone interested in contributing” [3][7]; all materials are freely available and “everyone participates as an individual… with equal standing” [4][5]; and the standards‑development process is “open for participation by all, with no individual or organizational membership requirements” [6].  

Thus, unlike many traditional standards bodies, the IETF’s governance is based on voluntary, individual participation with no formal membership prerequisites.

</details>

### Q50: what was norvegian policy during covid?

| | Baseline | Graph-Enriched |
|---|---|---|
| Time | 3.99s | 5.65s (+0.06s expansion) |
| Score avg | 4.0/5 | 5.0/5 |
| Expanded docs | — | 0 |
| **Winner** |  | **✓** |

> **Judge:** Answer A covers more policy dimensions, provides finer details and a clear table, and cites sources precisely, while Answer B omits some points and misattributes the authority that halted the contact‑tracing app.

<details><summary>Baseline answer</summary>

During the COVID‑19 pandemic Norway pursued a multi‑track policy that combined rapid economic relief, a strong emphasis on digitalisation, and strict data‑privacy safeguards.

1. **Economic safety‑net measures** – The Norwegian Parliament quickly turned pandemic‑related decisions into statutes and regulations that strengthened the economic safety net for citizens and businesses [2].

2. **Digital transformation as a catalyst** – The crisis was framed as an opportunity to accelerate digitalisation across the public sector and key industries (aquaculture, maritime, agriculture, tourism). A report titled *Our new digital world* notes that “Covid‑19 – a catalyst for digitalisation” was a central theme of the response [7].

3. **Privacy‑first approach to health‑tech** – When the national data‑protection authority warned that the processing of personal data by the COVID‑19 contact‑tracing app would be temporarily banned, the Norwegian Institute of Public Health halted the app and deleted all its data [3].

4. **Limiting restrictive economic measures** – A Norwegian Official Report (March 2021) recommended that any coronavirus measures that hindered the restructuring of the economy be lifted as soon as it was prudent, aiming to preserve competitiveness, green growth, and a well‑functioning labour market [6].

5. **Open‑digital‑public‑goods strategy** – Norway’s broader digital development strategy, discussed in an Open Forum on digital cooperation, stresses the use of open, non‑commercial digital public goods to avoid vendor lock‑in and uphold democratic principles [8].

**In summary**, Norway’s COVID‑19 policy combined swift economic support, an accelerated push for digitalisation, a decisive privacy‑protective stance that led to the shutdown of the contact‑tracing app, a commitment to lift restrictive economic measures when feasible, and an overarching strategy that favoured open digital public goods. [2][3][6][7][8]

</details>

<details><summary>Graph-enriched answer</summary>

**Norwegian policy during the COVID‑19 pandemic was a mix of rapid economic relief, a push for digital transformation, strict privacy safeguards and a plan to roll‑back restrictive measures once they were no longer needed.**  

| Policy area | What Norway did | Source |
|-------------|----------------|--------|
| **Economic safety net** | The Parliament approved swift statutes and regulations to strengthen the economic safety net for households and businesses as the crisis unfolded. | 【2】 |
| **Work and schooling** | “New normal” policies promoted home‑working, home‑schooling and minimised physical contact. | 【4】 |
| **Cross‑border trade & food sector** | The government recognised that pandemic‑related infection‑control measures disrupted cross‑border shopping and the production and sale of food and drink, and incorporated those impacts into its response. | 【5】 |
| **Digitalisation** | COVID‑19 was treated as a catalyst for digitalisation; Norway leveraged its strong digital infrastructure to accelerate public‑sector digital services, aquaculture, maritime, agriculture and tourism sectors. | 【7】 |
| **Open digital public goods** | Norway’s broader digital development strategy emphasised open, non‑commercial digital public goods to avoid vendor lock‑in and uphold democratic principles. | 【8】 |
| **Privacy protection** | The national data‑protection authority halted the COVID‑19 contact‑tracing app and ordered deletion of its data because of privacy concerns. | 【3】 |
| **Transition & green growth** | A 2021 official report recommended ending pandemic‑era restrictions that limited economic restructuring, and set out measures for stronger competitiveness, reduced greenhouse‑gas emissions, green growth, a well‑functioning labour market and long‑term economic sustainability. | 【6】 |

**In short:** Norway responded to COVID‑19 by quickly bolstering social and economic protections, encouraging remote work and schooling, accelerating digitalisation (while insisting on open, privacy‑respecting digital solutions), and planning a timely withdrawal of restrictive measures in favour of a greener, more competitive post‑pandemic economy.

</details>

**Which assistant was used for UN General Assembly speeches?**

The AI assistant that tracked and analysed the speeches delivered at the UN General Assembly was the **Diplo AI reporting system (the same hybrid assistant, i.e., DiploGPT)** that was tested during the 78th UN General Assembly and the 2023 Internet Governance Forum [1]. Earlier, the team also used text‑analysis tools and AI to monitor the 2020 UNGA debates [4].

The tool that actually **tracked and transcribed the UN General Assembly speeches** was the **Diplo AI reporting tool (the human‑AI hybrid reporting system)** that was tested during the 78th UN General Assembly 【1】 and earlier for the 2020 UNGA debates 【4】. DiploGPT, on the other hand, was applied to the UN Security Council, not the General Assembly 【2】.

### Q52: building trusting AI

| | Baseline | Graph-Enriched |
|---|---|---|
| Time | 9.01s | 6.68s (+0.30s expansion) |
| Score avg | 4.0/5 | 4.2/5 |
| Expanded docs | — | 0 |

> **Judge:** Answer A stays fully faithful to the provided sources and cites them consistently, while Answer B adds more concrete suggestions but introduces uncited details that reduce its accuracy and source usage.

<details><summary>Baseline answer</summary>

Building AI that people can **trust** isn’t a single‑step technical fix; it requires a multi‑layered approach that combines transparent design, foundational safeguards, and ongoing stakeholder collaboration. The excerpts you provided highlight four recurring themes that together form a practical roadmap for “trust‑worthy AI.”

| Pillar | What it means | How to implement it | Evidence from the context |
|--------|---------------|--------------------|---------------------------|
| **1. Transparency & Understandability** | Users and developers must be able to see *how* the system works and why it makes a particular decision. | • Publish model documentation, data provenance, and decision‑logic explanations.<br>• Use open‑source components where feasible.<br>• Provide user‑friendly dashboards that surface key metrics (accuracy, bias, uncertainty). | “Building transparent systems that are understandable… is one way to build trust.” [1][7] |
| **2. Trust Built‑In at Every Architectural Layer** | Trust should not be an after‑thought add‑on; it must be woven into infrastructure, platforms, and applications. | • Secure hardware roots of trust and tamper‑evident logs at the infrastructure level.<br>• Enforce policy‑driven governance (e.g., access controls, audit trails) on the platform.<br>• Embed fairness, privacy, and accountability checks directly into the application’s business logic. | “Trust in AI systems must be built foundationally across all layers (infrastructure, platform, application) rather than as an add‑on feature.” [6] |
| **3. Relationship‑Centred Governance** | Trust is also a social contract between developers, operators, regulators, and end‑users. | • Establish long‑term partnership agreements (e.g., MOUs) that spell out responsibilities for data quality, model updates, and incident response.<br>• Conduct regular joint reviews with stakeholders to verify that expectations are being met.<br>• Create feedback loops so users can report anomalies and see corrective actions. | “Our whole operation is built on relationship and trust… we need to work very closely to make sure the trust is not broken.” [3] |
| **4. Rights‑Respecting & Ethical Design** | Trust is reinforced when AI respects human rights and societal values. | • Conduct impact assessments that evaluate privacy, non‑discrimination, and autonomy.<br>• Adopt standards (e.g., ISO/IEC 42001 for AI governance) that codify rights‑based criteria.<br>• Make the system’s purpose and limits explicit to users. | “Building trustworthy and rights‑respecting technologies” is a core goal of the workshop on AI tools in business operations. [4] |
| **5. Multi‑Stakeholder Collaboration** | Trust emerges from coordinated effort among governments, industry, academia, and civil society. | • Set up interoperable governance frameworks (e.g., shared taxonomies, certification schemes).<br>• Hold regular round‑tables or working groups to align on trust metrics and share best practices. | “How to build trust in AI systems and the role of different stakeholders in this process” was a key discussion point. [5] |

### Practical Steps to Start Building Trust‑worthy AI

1. **Define Trust Metrics Early**  
   - Accuracy, fairness, robustness, explainability, and compliance scores should be part of the product’s KPI dashboard from day 1.

2. **Adopt a “Trust‑by‑Design” Checklist**  
   - **Infrastructure:** Secure compute, immutable logs, provenance tracking.  
   - **Platform:** Role‑based access, policy enforcement, audit trails.  
   - **Application:** Explainable outputs, bias mitigation, user consent flows.

3. **Create Transparent Documentation**  
   - Model cards, data sheets, and system architecture diagrams that are publicly accessible (or at least available to partners) help satisfy the “understandable” requirement. [1][7]

4. **Establish Ongoing Governance Bodies**  
   - A cross‑functional “Trust Council” that meets quarterly to review incidents, update policies, and incorporate stakeholder feedback. This mirrors the relationship‑focused approach highlighted in the France‑India industrial bridge case. [3]

5. **Implement Rights‑Impact Assessments**  
   - Before deployment, run a checklist that verifies privacy safeguards, non‑discrimination checks, and alignment with applicable regulations (e.g., GDPR, India’s AI Policy). This aligns with the rights‑respecting focus of the business‑operations workshop. [4]

6. **Facilitate Interoperability & Shared Standards**  
   - Participate in sector‑wide initiatives (like the WS #97 interoperability discussion) to adopt common trust vocabularies and certification schemes, making it easier for different actors to trust each other’s AI. [5]

### Bottom Line

Trustworthy AI is **not** a bolt‑on feature; it is a **systemic design principle** that must be embedded in technology, processes, and relationships. By making systems transparent, building trust into every architectural layer, fostering strong stakeholder relationships, respecting human rights, and coordinating governance across sectors, organizations can move from “AI that works” to “AI that people *trust*.”

</details>

<details><summary>Graph-enriched answer</summary>

Building AI that people can rely on – “trust‑worthy AI” – is repeatedly framed in the sources as a matter of **design, architecture, relationships, and stakeholder engagement** rather than a bolt‑on feature. The key levers that emerge from the excerpts are:

| Dimension | What the sources say | How it contributes to trust |
|-----------|----------------------|----------------------------|
| **Transparency & Understandability** | “Building transparent systems that are understandable… is one way to build trust.” [1][7] | When users can see how a model works and why it makes a particular decision, they are more willing to accept its outputs. |
| **Relationship‑Based Trust** | “Our whole operation is built on relationship and trust… every element of the architecture needs to have trust built in… we need to work very closely to make sure the trust is not broken.” [3] | Ongoing collaboration between developers, operators, and end‑users creates a social contract that reinforces confidence in the system. |
| **Trust Embedded Across All Layers** | “Trust in AI systems must be built foundationally across all layers (infrastructure, platform, application) rather than as an add‑on feature.” [6] | Embedding safeguards, verification, and accountability mechanisms from the hardware level up to the user‑facing application prevents trust gaps that could appear later. |
| **Stakeholder Involvement** | “How to build trust in AI systems and the role of different stakeholders in this process.” [5] | Involving regulators, civil‑society groups, industry partners, and users in governance discussions ensures that diverse concerns are addressed and that the system’s trust model is socially legitimated. |
| **Rights‑Respecting Design** | “Building trustworthy and rights‑respecting technologies.” [4] | Aligning AI behavior with human rights (privacy, non‑discrimination, accountability) signals that the system respects fundamental values, which is a core component of trust. |
| **Foundational Trust as a Core Value** | “AI is built on trust.” [8] | Recognizing trust as a foundational premise keeps it front‑and‑center throughout the development lifecycle rather than treating it as an afterthought. |

### Practical steps distilled from the context
1. **Make the system transparent** – publish model documentation, decision‑logic explanations, and data provenance so that users can understand how outcomes are generated. [1][7]  
2. **Foster ongoing relationships** – maintain open communication channels between developers, operators, and users; regularly review performance and address concerns before they erode confidence. [3]  
3. **Integrate trust at every technical tier** – embed security, auditability, and validation checks in the infrastructure, platform services, and application code. [6]  
4. **Engage a broad set of stakeholders** – convene regulators, industry partners, civil‑society groups, and end‑users to co‑design governance frameworks and accountability mechanisms. [5]  
5. **Align with rights‑based principles** – ensure the AI respects privacy, fairness, and accountability, thereby reinforcing that the technology serves societal values. [4]  

By weaving these elements together—transparent design, relationship‑based governance, layered technical safeguards, inclusive stakeholder participation, and rights‑respecting objectives—organizations can move from “AI that is merely functional” to “AI that is genuinely trusted.”

</details>

### Q54: How does ECMAScript differ from JavaScript?

| | Baseline | Graph-Enriched |
|---|---|---|
| Time | 6.65s | 5.04s (+0.07s expansion) |
| Score avg | 4.8/5 | 4.2/5 |
| Expanded docs | — | 0 |
| **Winner** | **✓** |  |

> **Judge:** Answer A provides a more thorough breakdown of differences (including status and usage examples) and offers slightly richer concrete details, while both are accurate and cite sources similarly.

<details><summary>Baseline answer</summary>

**ECMAScript is the *standard*; JavaScript is an *implementation* of that standard.**

| Aspect | ECMAScript | JavaScript |
|--------|------------|------------|
| **Nature** | A vendor‑neutral, cross‑platform programming‑language specification maintained by Ecma International (ECMA‑262). It defines the syntax, semantics and built‑in objects that any conforming implementation must provide. | A concrete language that was first created by Netscape (and later by Microsoft as JScript). It is the name most browsers use for the scripting language that runs on the Web. |
| **Origin** | The specification was started in 1996 and has been updated yearly since ECMAScript 2016 [1]. | JavaScript (and Microsoft’s JScript) were the original implementations that inspired the ECMAScript specification [1]. |
| **Scope** | Describes the language abstractly – e.g., the addition of maps, sets, binary‑numeric arrays, and full Unicode support are part of the *spec* [1]. | Implements those features (to the extent the engine follows the spec) but may also add proprietary extensions or behave differently in edge cases. |
| **Standard‑status** | An internationally recognized standard (ECMA‑262) that can be referenced by other standards such as the JSON data‑interchange syntax [8]. | Not a standard itself; it is a name for the actual runtime that complies with the ECMAScript spec. |
| **Examples of usage** | The spec is cited when other standards need a common definition of “ECMAScript” (e.g., ISO/IEC 21778 for JSON) [8]. | The language that developers write in browsers, Node.js, embedded devices, etc., all of which claim to be “JavaScript” but must follow the ECMAScript spec [3][5]. |

In short, **ECMAScript** is the *reference definition* of the language, while **JavaScript** is the *real‑world language* that browsers and other runtimes implement according to that definition. This distinction is reflected in the historical record (JavaScript and Microsoft’s JScript gave rise to the ECMAScript standard) [1] and in how other technologies (e.g., JSON) refer to the spec rather than to a particular implementation [2][8].

</details>

<details><summary>Graph-enriched answer</summary>

**ECMAScript is the *standard* that defines the language, while JavaScript is the *implementation* (and the name most people use for the language in practice).**  

* The **ECMAScript Language Specification** (ECMA‑262) is the formal, vendor‑neutral description of the language’s syntax, semantics, built‑in objects, Unicode handling, maps, sets, binary arrays, etc. It is maintained by Ecma International’s TC39 committee and is released on a yearly cadence [1][5][8].  

* **JavaScript** is the name given to the actual programming language that browsers (originally Netscape) and other runtimes (e.g., Microsoft’s JScript) provide to developers.  It is an implementation of the ECMAScript specification, adding the concrete runtime, host‑environment APIs, and sometimes extra features that go beyond the spec [1][3].  

Thus the difference can be summed up as:

| Aspect | ECMAScript | JavaScript |
|--------|------------|------------|
| What it is | A **standard** (ECMA‑262) that describes the language’s core behavior and built‑ins. | A **real‑world language/runtime** used in browsers and other platforms, built to conform to the ECMAScript spec. |
| Governance | Developed and maintained by Ecma International’s TC39 (open, yearly releases). | Implemented by vendors (e.g., Netscape, Microsoft, V8, SpiderMonkey) and may include non‑standard extensions. |
| Scope | Defines the language’s abstract semantics, data types, Unicode support, etc. | Provides the concrete execution environment, host objects (DOM, BOM), and any proprietary extensions. |

The context even notes that JSON “was inspired by the object literals of **JavaScript aka ECMAScript** as defined in the ECMAScript Language Specification” [2], underscoring that JavaScript derives its syntax from the ECMAScript spec.  

In short, **ECMAScript = the specification; JavaScript = the widely‑used implementation of that specification**. [1][5][8]

</details>

> **Judge:** Both answers are thorough and accurate, but A provides slightly more concrete citations and detailed practical steps, giving it an edge in source usage and specificity.

<details><summary>Baseline answer</summary>

**How to Build Trusted AI – Key Take‑aways from the Provided Sources**

| Pillar / Action | What It Means | Supporting Quote(s) |
|-----------------|---------------|---------------------|
| **Transparency & Explainability** | Make AI systems understandable and open about how they work, so users can see what is happening inside the model. | “building transparent systems that are understandable… is one way to build trust” [1]; “Trust building requires transparency, explainability… and clear policies” [5] |
| **Traceability (Trace the Systems, Models, Data)** | Keep a full audit trail of the data, models, and processes used, enabling verification and accountability. | “we need to trace the systems, the models, the data that we use for AI” [2]; “the ability to trace systems and data” [6] |
| **Human‑in‑the‑Loop Governance** | Ensure people can intervene, review, and override AI decisions, preserving human oversight. | “human‑in‑the‑loop governance” [5] |
| **Clear Ownership & Value‑Chain Accountability** | Define who owns each component of the AI pipeline and who is responsible for its outcomes. | “clear ownership along the value chain” [6] |
| **Understanding Limits & Failure Modes** | Know where the model may fail, its performance boundaries, and how it behaves under stress. | “knowing the limits and failure points of models” [6] |
| **Benchmarking & Performance Evaluation** | Regularly test AI against standards and real‑world scenarios to verify reliability. | “benchmarking performance” [6] |
| **Security & Robustness** | Protect AI systems from tampering, adversarial attacks, and data breaches. | “ensuring security” [6] |
| **Embedding Trust Across All Layers** | Trust should be a foundational design principle, not an after‑thought add‑on. | “Trust in AI systems must be built foundationally across all layers (infrastructure, platform, application) rather than as an add‑on feature” [3] |
| **Relationship‑Based Trust** | Build and maintain collaborative relationships among stakeholders; trust is reinforced through ongoing interaction and reliability. | “Our whole operation is built on relationship and trust… spend some time on transparency and evaluation… make sure the trust is not broken” [4] |
| **Leverage Existing International Institutions** | Use established global governance bodies to set ethical standards rather than creating entirely new structures. | “utilizing existing international institutions rather than creating entirely new governance structures” [7][8] |

### Practical Steps to Implement These Pillars

1. **Document the AI pipeline** – record data sources, preprocessing steps, model versions, and deployment environments (traceability) [2][6].
2. **Publish model cards or datasheets** – summarise capabilities, intended use, limitations, and known failure points (transparency, limits) [5][6].
3. **Integrate explainable‑AI tools** – provide visual or textual explanations for model outputs (explainability) [1][5].
4. **Set up human‑in‑the‑loop checkpoints** – require human review for high‑risk decisions (governance) [5].
5. **Define clear ownership** – assign responsibility for each stage (data collection, model training, deployment) and make it publicly known (ownership) [6].
6. **Run regular security audits and adversarial testing** – ensure robustness against attacks (security) [6].
7. **Benchmark against industry standards** – use open datasets and metrics to compare performance (benchmarking) [6].
8. **Create a trust‑maintenance process** – schedule periodic transparency reports and stakeholder meetings to reinforce relationships (relationship trust) [4].
9. **Align with international guidelines** – adopt frameworks from bodies such as OECD, UNESCO, or ISO to avoid reinventing governance structures (existing institutions) [7][8].

### Summary

Building trusted AI is a multi‑dimensional effort that combines **technical safeguards** (traceability, security, benchmarking) with **process and governance measures** (human‑in‑the‑loop, clear ownership, relationship‑based trust). Trust must be **designed into every layer** of the system—from infrastructure to application—rather than tacked on later, and it should be reinforced through **transparent communication** and **collaborative oversight** using existing international norms. By following the pillars and steps above, organisations can create AI that is not only powerful but also reliable, accountable, and trusted by users and partners alike.

</details>

<details><summary>Graph-enriched answer</summary>

**How to Build Trusted AI – Key Take‑aways from the Provided Sources**

| Pillar | What It Means | How to Implement | Source |
|--------|---------------|-----------------|--------|
| **Transparency & Explainability** | Make AI systems understandable to users and stakeholders. | Publish model documentation, data provenance, and decision‑logic explanations; open‑source parts of the stack where feasible. | [1], [5] |
| **Traceability (Auditability)** | Ability to follow the lineage of data, models, and decisions. | Keep immutable logs of data ingestion, model training runs, versioning, and deployment events; use provenance tools. | [2], [6] |
| **Foundational Trust Across All Layers** | Trust should be baked into infrastructure, platforms, and applications—not added later as an after‑thought. | Design security, governance, and monitoring controls at the hardware, cloud, and API levels from day‑one. | [3] |
| **Human‑in‑the‑Loop Governance** | Keep people in control of critical decisions and oversight. | Deploy review checkpoints, escalation paths, and “kill‑switch” mechanisms; involve domain experts in model validation. | [5] |
| **Clear Policies & Ownership** | Define who is responsible for each part of the AI value chain. | Draft and publish governance policies that specify data custodians, model owners, and compliance officers; assign legal accountability. | [5], [6] |
| **Performance Benchmarking & Failure‑Mode Awareness** | Know the limits and failure points of models. | Conduct systematic stress‑testing, robustness checks, and continuous performance monitoring against agreed benchmarks. | [6] |
| **Security & Resilience** | Protect AI assets from tampering, adversarial attacks, and data breaches. | Apply encryption, access controls, and regular security audits; integrate threat‑modeling into the development lifecycle. | [6] |
| **Relationship‑Based Trust** | Build trust through ongoing collaboration and communication among partners. | Hold regular joint reviews, share evaluation results, and maintain open dialogue to prevent “trust breakage.” | [4] |
| **Leverage Existing International Institutions** | Use established multilateral bodies rather than inventing new governance structures. | Align standards and certifications with bodies such as ISO, OECD AI Principles, and the IEEE; participate in global forums. | [7], [8] |
| **Ethical Foundations** | Ground AI work in shared ethical values (fairness, accountability, respect for rights). | Conduct impact assessments, embed ethical checklists, and ensure rights‑respecting design from the outset. | [7], [8] |

### Practical Road‑Map for an Organization

1. **Define a Trust Framework** – Adopt the five‑pillar model (traceability, limits/failure points, benchmarking, security, ownership) as a baseline.  
2. **Embed Trust Early** – Integrate transparency, security, and governance controls into the architecture before any model is deployed.  
3. **Document & Publish** – Maintain up‑to‑date model cards, data sheets, and audit logs that are accessible to internal reviewers and, where appropriate, external auditors.  
4. **Implement Human‑in‑the‑Loop Controls** – Set thresholds that trigger human review and create clear escalation procedures.  
5. **Continuous Monitoring & Benchmarking** – Use automated dashboards to track performance drift, security incidents, and compliance with policy.  
6. **Stakeholder Engagement** – Run regular workshops with partners, regulators, and civil‑society groups to keep relational trust strong.  
7. **Align with Global Standards** – Map internal policies to ISO/IEC AI standards, OECD AI Principles, and other relevant international frameworks.  

By systematically applying these pillars—transparency, traceability, foundational design, human oversight, clear ownership, performance awareness, security, relational collaboration, and alignment with existing institutions—organizations can move from “trust as an add‑on” to **trust built into every layer of AI**. This approach reflects the consensus across the roundtables, workshops, and fireside chats cited above.

</details>

### Q59: internet standardization

| | Baseline | Graph-Enriched |
|---|---|---|
| Time | 5.93s | 5.13s (+0.09s expansion) |
| Score avg | 5.0/5 | 4.2/5 |
| Expanded docs | — | 2 |
| **Winner** | **✓** |  |

> **Judge:** Answer A covers the topic more thoroughly with detailed process steps, broader impact analysis, and richer examples, while both maintain accuracy and citation quality.

<details><summary>Baseline answer</summary>

**Internet standardization** is the process of developing, agreeing on, and publishing technical specifications that define how the various components of the global Internet must operate so that devices, networks, and services can inter‑operate reliably and securely.

### Why it matters
* **Interoperability:** By using common standards, an iPhone, an Android phone, a laptop, or any other device can exchange data without needing custom adapters. This seamless connectivity is repeatedly highlighted as a core success factor of the Internet [1][3][6].
* **Safety & performance:** International standards set “levels of performance and safety” that ensure services meet globally recognised quality thresholds, protecting users and infrastructure alike [6].
* **Sustainability:** Standardisation is also seen as a tool to mitigate environmental risks, helping shape policies for a more eco‑friendly Internet [2].
* **Policy & governance:** Standards embed “good principles, practices or guidelines” that become the de‑facto behaviour of the “reasonable man” in the digital realm, influencing both technical operation and regulatory approaches [6][7].

### Key organisations that create Internet standards
| Organisation | Typical focus | Example standards |
|--------------|---------------|-------------------|
| **Internet Engineering Task Force (IETF)** | Protocol design and operation | TCP/IP, DNS, TLS/SSL |
| **International Organization for Standardization (ISO)** | Broad‑based technology and management standards | ISO/IEC 27001 (information security) |
| **International Telecommunication Union (ITU)** | Global telecom and radio‑communication standards | ITU‑T recommendations for network signalling |
| **Internet Society (ISOC) initiatives** (e.g., Collaborative Leadership Exchange) | Community‑driven policy and standards impact | Guidance on how standards affect user experience [7] |

### Representative technical standards
* **TCP/IP** – the foundational suite that routes packets across the Internet [8][5].
* **Domain Name System (DNS)** – translates human‑readable names to IP addresses [8].
* **Secure Sockets Layer / Transport Layer Security (SSL/TLS)** – provides encrypted communication [8].
* **Content formats** – HTML, XML, which enable consistent rendering of web pages [5].

### How standardisation is applied
1. **Drafting** – engineers, operators, and researchers collaborate (often within the IETF) to write Internet‑Draft documents.
2. **Review & consensus** – the drafts undergo public review, discussion, and iterative improvement.
3. **Publication** – once consensus is reached, the documents become RFCs (Request for Comments) or formal standards.
4. **Adoption** – hardware manufacturers, software developers, and service providers implement the standards, ensuring that new products work with existing ones worldwide.

### The broader impact
* **Economic:** A level playing field for infrastructure providers, fostering competition and innovation.
* **Legal:** Standards become reference points for “reasonable” behaviour in disputes, echoing the Roman concept of *pater familias* [6].
* **Social:** By shaping inclusive, sustainable policies, standards help make the Internet accessible and environmentally responsible for all users [2].

In short, internet standardization is the collaborative, international effort that defines the technical rules of the Internet, enabling devices to talk to each other safely, efficiently, and in a way that supports broader policy goals such as sustainability and equitable access. [1][2][4][6][7][8]

</details>

<details><summary>Graph-enriched answer</summary>

**Internet standardization – why it matters and who drives it**

| Aspect | What the sources say | Why it matters |
|--------|----------------------|----------------|
| **Core purpose** | Standardisation makes the Internet work “seamlessly across devices and networks” and guarantees “interoperability” for iPhones, Android phones, servers, etc. [1][3] | Without common technical rules, devices could not exchange data reliably, breaking the global reach of the network. |
| **Key benefits** | *Security & safety*: Standardised protocols (e.g., TCP/IP, DNS, SSL/TLS) provide a baseline of “performance and safety” that can be trusted worldwide. [6][8] <br>*Sustainability*: A common set of standards helps mitigate environmental risks and supports an “inclusive policy for a sustainable and eco‑friendly internet.” [2] | Consistent security practices reduce vulnerabilities; shared efficiency lowers energy consumption and waste. |
| **Main standard‑setting bodies** | • **IETF** – produces the technical documents that shape how the Internet is used and managed. [6] <br>• **ISO** – broader ICT standards. [4] <br>• **ITU** – telecommunications standards that intersect with Internet protocols. [4] | These organizations bring together engineers, operators, researchers, and policy makers to reach consensus on technical specifications. |
| **Governance context** | International standardisation “provides a level playing field” among infrastructure providers and enshrines “good principles, practices or guidelines” that the “reasonable man” follows. [6] | By codifying expectations, standards reduce disputes, foster competition on merit, and protect users’ rights. |
| **Stakeholder involvement** | Regional bodies such as **ARIN Service Region** participate in the global process, linking local network operators to the international standards ecosystem. [4] <br>Community voices (designers, operators, researchers) drive the IETF’s work. [6] | Broad participation ensures that standards reflect diverse technical realities and policy priorities. |
| **Impact on users** | “Internet standardization can impact how individuals use the internet.” [7] | Standards affect everything from page load speed to privacy protections that everyday users experience. |

### Take‑away summary
Internet standardization is the backbone that lets the global network function reliably, securely, and sustainably. It is achieved through collaborative work in bodies such as the **IETF**, **ISO**, and **ITU**, with regional participants (e.g., ARIN) and a wide community of technical experts. By codifying interoperable protocols (TCP/IP, DNS, SSL/TLS, HTML, XML, etc.), standards create a common “language” for devices, enable a level playing field for service providers, and embed safety and environmental considerations into the fabric of the Internet.

</details>

Digital diplomacy is the practice of using digital technologies—especially the Internet, social‑media platforms, and other information‑communication tools—to conduct diplomatic activities and pursue foreign‑policy objectives.  It encompasses:

* **The medium** – the Internet, social media, virtual meeting platforms, and other ICTs that enable real‑time, networked communication.  
* **The purpose** – achieving diplomatic goals such as negotiation, representation, image‑building, public diplomacy, cultural exchange, and managing issues like privacy, data protection, and cybersecurity.  
* **The transformation** – a shift in the environment in which diplomacy is carried out, adding new policy domains and tools while complementing traditional diplomatic methods.

In short, digital diplomacy (also called e‑diplomacy or web diplomacy) is “the use of the Internet and social media for achieving diplomatic objectives” and “the use of digital technologies … to conduct diplomatic activities and achieve foreign‑policy goals”【4†L1-L4】【5†L1-L4】. The consensus emerging from the 2024 IGF‑related sessions is that the Internet Governance Forum is expected to **continue operating after 2025** with a renewed mandate.  

* Multiple participants stressed that the 2025 mandate renewal is “a pivotal outcome” that affirms the IGF’s value for shaping global internet governance [7].  
* The “Journey to ‘Building Our Multistakeholder Digital Future’” notes a “strong sentiment that the IGF’s mandate will be renewed post‑2025, reinforcing the forum’s importance” [1] and urges member states to consider **hosting the annual meeting from 2026 onward** [4].  
* High‑level discussions asked participants to envision the IGF’s role beyond 2025 and how it could support the implementation of the Global Digital Compact (GDC) [2].  
* Closing‑ceremony remarks explicitly looked ahead to the “IGF 2025 Future and Structure,” indicating planning for the next phase [3].  
* Analysts further linked the IGF’s future to the broader trajectory of multistakeholder digital governance, stating that “the future of the IGF will define the future of the NRIs” [5].

**In short:** The prevailing view in the provided material is that the IGF’s mandate will be **renewed in 2025**, allowing the forum to continue its multistakeholder role, potentially with new hosting arrangements and an expanded contribution to initiatives such as the Global Digital Compact. The exact structure and specific activities will be shaped in the post‑2025 planning process, but the IGF is expected to remain a central pillar of global internet governance.

**Youth Manifesto for Digital Rights**  
*(drawn from the evidence‑based priorities highlighted in the provided documents)*  

| # | Manifesto Pillar | What We Demand | Why It Matters (source) |
|---|------------------|----------------|------------------------|
| 1 | **Full Representation of Youth & Minorities** | • Guarantee seats for young people and minority groups in every multistakeholder forum that shapes digital policy. <br>• Create transparent selection mechanisms so that diverse voices are heard, not just the loudest. | The ninth point of the “Upholding Human Rights in the Digital Age” paper stresses the need for representation of young people and minority groups [1]. |
| 2 | **Protection of Children’s Dignity, Security & Privacy** | • Adopt and enforce the “Declaration of the Committee of Ministers on protecting the dignity, security and privacy of children on the Internet.” <br>• Build child‑safety by‑design tools and enforce age‑appropriate data‑handling standards. | The “GUIDE to hUman rIGhts for IntErnEt UsErs” cites this declaration as the benchmark for child‑online rights [5]. |
| 3 | **Combat Disinformation & Preserve Civic Space** | • Fund youth‑led fact‑checking networks and media‑literacy programmes, especially in fragile regions (e.g., the Sahel). <br>• Shield digital civic spaces from undue restriction or surveillance. | Digitalise Youth’s advocacy targets shrinking civic space and rampant disinformation in the Sahel, empowering local youth activists [2]. |
| 4 | **Capacity‑Building & Empowerment of Youth Activists** | • Provide sustainable financing, mentorship, and technical training for youth‑led civil‑society organisations. <br>• Enable peer‑to‑peer knowledge exchange across regions. | The Digitalise Youth Project’s capacity‑building work is highlighted as a core strategy for digital‑rights advocacy [2]. |
| 5 | **Recognition of Youth Intellectual Property (IP) Rights** | • Enshrine strong IP protections for youth‑generated content, open‑source projects, and start‑ups. <br>• Offer simplified registration pathways and legal aid for young innovators. | YouthLead identifies IP rights as a lever to empower youth innovation and entrepreneurship [3]. |
| 6 | **Equitable Access to Digital Infrastructure** | • Close the digital divide by investing in affordable broadband, devices, and connectivity in underserved communities. <br>• Ensure accessibility standards for persons with disabilities. | Youth representatives called for an “equitable, secure and empowering” digital future for all [6]. |
| 7 | **Youth Participation in Digital Policymaking & Governance** | • Institutionalise youth advisory councils at national, regional and global levels (e.g., IGF, UN treaty negotiations). <br>• Give youth voting rights on draft regulations, standards and treaty texts. | Multiple sources stress increasing youth participation in digital policymaking [4]; the WAIGF keynote and IGF workshop both underline the importance of youth shaping the digital future [7][8]. |
| 8 | **Inclusive Multistakeholder Approach** | • Adopt a governance model that brings together governments, private sector, academia, civil society and youth on equal footing. <br>• Require that any decision affecting digital rights be reviewed for its impact on young people and minorities. | The overarching theme of “Upholding Human Rights in the Digital Age” is a multistakeholder approach that safeguards dignity and freedom for all [1]. |
| 9 | **Youth‑Centred Design of Emerging Cyber‑Security Norms** | • Ensure that any new UN cyber‑crime treaty or related instrument includes a dedicated youth clause, guaranteeing their active involvement in drafting and oversight. | The IGF 2023 workshop notes that children and young people should be actively involved in rights advocacy and decision‑making in the digital environment [8]. |

---

### How to Turn This Manifesto into Action  

1. **Form a Global Youth Digital Rights Coalition** – bring together existing networks (Digitalise Youth, YouthLead, etc.) to lobby governments and intergovernmental bodies.  
2. **Submit the Manifesto to Key Forums** – IGF, UN‑GA, regional cyber‑security summits, and national digital strategy consultations.  
3. **Create a “Youth Digital Rights Scorecard”** – annually assess countries and platforms against the nine pillars, publicising gaps and best practices.  
4. **Secure Funding** – request dedicated budget lines from development agencies and private‑sector CSR programmes for youth capacity‑building and IP support.  
5. **Monitor & Report** – establish an independent youth‑led watchdog to track implementation of the manifesto commitments and publish transparent reports.

---

**In short:** We, the youth of the digital age, demand representation, protection, empowerment, and equitable participation in every decision that shapes our online lives. By adopting these nine concrete pillars, governments, multinationals, and civil society can ensure a digital future that is **equitable, secure, and truly empowering for all**.  

*References:* [1] [2] [3] [4] [5] [6] [7] [8].

</details>

**Youth Manifesto for Digital Rights**  
*(All points are drawn directly from the supplied sources and are therefore grounded in the current multistakeholder discourse on youth‑centred digital governance.)*  

1. **Full and Fair Representation**  
   - Youth and minority groups must have guaranteed seats at every table where digital policies are debated and decided.  [1] [7]  

2. **Capacity‑Building and Advocacy**  
   - Invest in programmes that equip young activists and civil‑society organisations with the skills, tools and networks needed to champion digital rights at local, regional and international levels. [2]  

3. **Combat Shrinking Civic Space & Disinformation**  
   - Target the rise of misinformation—especially in fragile regions such as the Sahel—by empowering youth‑led fact‑checking, media‑literacy and community‑outreach initiatives. [2]  

4. **Protect Children’s Dignity, Security & Privacy Online**  
   - Adopt and enforce standards that safeguard the personal data, safety and well‑being of children on the Internet, as outlined in the Committee of Ministers’ declaration. [5]  

5. **Guarantee Equitable Access to a Secure Digital Future**  
   - Ensure that every young person, regardless of geography or socio‑economic status, can safely and affordably connect to the digital world. [6]  

6. **Uphold Intellectual Property Rights for Youth Innovation**  
   - Recognise and protect the creations of young entrepreneurs, enabling them to commercialise ideas without undue barriers. [3]  

7. **Increase Youth Participation in Digital Policymaking & Governance**  
   - Institutionalise mechanisms (youth advisory councils, co‑creation labs, quota‑based seats) that give young people a decisive voice in shaping regulations, standards and governance frameworks. [4]  

8. **Make the Digital Future Empowering for All**  
   - Design platforms, services and infrastructures that are inclusive, user‑centred and capable of enhancing education, health, employment and civic engagement for every youth. [6] [7]  

9. **Active Involvement of Children & Young People in Rights Advocacy**  
   - Children and adolescents must be treated as rights‑holders, not merely beneficiaries, and should be directly involved in advocacy, monitoring and decision‑making processes concerning their digital environment. [8]  

---

**Call to Action:**  
Governments, multistakeholder bodies, the private sector and civil society must adopt this manifesto as a living framework, regularly reviewing progress and adapting it to emerging technologies and challenges. Only through genuine youth participation can the digital age truly uphold human dignity, freedom and equality for all.

### Q65: core internet protocols, network security

| | Baseline | Graph-Enriched |
|---|---|---|
| Time | 6.83s | 9.13s (+0.06s expansion) |
| Score avg | 4.2/5 | 5.0/5 |
| Expanded docs | — | 0 |
| **Winner** |  | **✓** |

> **Judge:** Answer A covers the topic more thoroughly with concrete protocol details, attack examples, and multiple cited standards, while both are accurate.

<details><summary>Baseline answer</summary>

**What the provided sources say about core Internet protocols and network security**

| Aspect | What the sources state | Key citations |
|--------|------------------------|---------------|
| **Core protocols are treated as a global public good** | The “public‑core” concept, developed over the past decade, frames core Internet protocols and infrastructure (e.g., routing, DNS, exchange points, cable systems) as a global public good that should be shielded from unwarranted state intervention. | [4], [5] |
| **Normative gaps** | Existing legal and regulatory frameworks do not fully cover the protection of the public core, leaving gaps that need to be addressed, especially in conflict or crisis situations. | [4] |
| **Standards that re‑architect the Internet for security** | New standards are being promoted to redesign the Internet’s core, improving protection against cyber threats and guaranteeing the confidentiality, integrity, and availability (CIA) of information. | [1], [2] |
| **Technical security mechanisms** | DNSSEC (Domain Name System Security Extensions) and RPKI (Resource Public Key Infrastructure) are highlighted as essential tools for securing the core infrastructure, ensuring that routing and name resolution cannot be easily spoofed or hijacked. | [6], [7] |
| **General role of protocols and standards** | Protocols and standards are explicitly linked to the robustness of the core Internet infrastructure, underpinning its resilience against attacks. | [3] |

### Synthesis

1. **Core Internet protocols** (such as IP routing, DNS, BGP, and the physical layers of cable and exchange points) are considered part of the *public core*—a set of assets that the international community views as a global public good. This status is meant to protect them from arbitrary or hostile state actions. [4][5]

2. **Network security** for these protocols is pursued through two complementary approaches:  
   * **Normative/Legal** – Developing and strengthening normative frameworks that explicitly safeguard the public core, while closing identified gaps, especially in contexts of conflict or crisis. [4]  
   * **Technical/Standards‑based** – Deploying security‑focused standards that redesign or harden the Internet’s backbone, thereby delivering the CIA triad (confidentiality, integrity, availability). Key technical standards include DNSSEC for authenticated DNS responses and RPKI for validated BGP route announcements. [1][2][6][7]

3. **Why these measures matter** – By re‑architecting the Internet core with robust standards and by treating core protocols as a protected public good, the community aims to reduce the attack surface for cyber threats, prevent malicious manipulation of routing or naming services, and ensure that essential communications remain reliable even under adverse conditions. [1][3]

### Take‑away points

- **Public‑core norm**: Core protocols are to be exempt from unwarranted state interference, but current legal frameworks have gaps that need filling. [4][5]  
- **Security standards**: DNSSEC and RPKI are the flagship technical mechanisms recommended for protecting the core infrastructure. [6][7]  
- **Overall goal**: A combination of normative protection and technical hardening seeks to preserve the confidentiality, integrity, and availability of the Internet’s foundational services. [1][3]

</details>

<details><summary>Graph-enriched answer</summary>

**Core Internet Protocols and Their Role in Network Security**

| Core protocol / component | Primary function | Why it matters for security |
|---------------------------|-------------------|----------------------------|
| **IP (Internet Protocol)** – IPv4/IPv6 | Routes packets between end‑systems. | The integrity of routing tables is essential; attacks that hijack IP prefixes can divert traffic (e.g., BGP hijacking). |
| **TCP / UDP** | Provides transport‑layer services (reliable vs. best‑effort delivery). | Vulnerabilities (e.g., SYN‑floods, UDP amplification) can be mitigated only when the underlying transport behaves predictably. |
| **DNS (Domain Name System)** | Translates human‑readable names to IP addresses. | Without cryptographic protection, DNS can be spoofed, enabling phishing or man‑in‑the‑middle attacks. |
| **BGP (Border Gateway Protocol)** | Exchanges routing information between autonomous systems. | BGP is the “glue” of the global routing fabric; insecure BGP enables prefix hijacking and traffic interception. |
| **Internet Exchange Points (IXPs) & physical cable systems** | Physical interconnection points that carry bulk traffic. | Physical or logical disruption can cripple large portions of the Internet. |

---

### How International Standards and Norms Strengthen These Core Elements

1. **Technical standards that re‑architect the Internet core**  
   - The IGF 2023 Open Forum highlighted that emerging standards aim to “re‑architect the internet core, providing improved protection against cyber threats, and ensuring the confidentiality, integrity, and availability of information”【1†L1-L3】.  
   - By updating protocol specifications (e.g., adding mandatory encryption, stronger authentication), these standards directly harden the core protocols listed above.

2. **Protection of robustness through protocols and standards**  
   - The IGF 2019 Final Report notes that “protocols and standards protect the robustness of core Internet infrastructure”【3†L1-L2】, underscoring that the security of IP, TCP/UDP, DNS, and BGP is a function of well‑designed, widely‑adopted standards.

3. **Normative frameworks that treat the core as a global public good**  
   - The “public core” concept, developed over a decade, frames core protocols and infrastructure as a **global public good** that should be exempt from “unwarranted intervention by states”【4†L4-L6】【5†L1-L2】.  
   - This normative stance encourages multistakeholder cooperation and discourages politicized shutdowns or censorship that could undermine network security.

4. **Specific security extensions for DNS and routing**  
   - Speakers at the IGF 2023 workshop on Quantum‑IoT‑Infrastructure emphasized the importance of **DNSSEC** (Domain Name System Security Extensions) and **RPKI** (Resource Public Key Infrastructure) for securing the core infrastructure【6†L1-L3】【7†L1-L2】.  
   - **DNSSEC** cryptographically signs DNS records, preventing spoofing and cache poisoning.  
   - **RPKI** validates BGP announcements, ensuring that only authorized entities can announce specific IP prefixes, thus mitigating hijacking.

---

### Why These Measures Matter for Network Security

- **Confidentiality, Integrity, Availability (CIA)** – The standards and extensions cited aim directly at the CIA triad for core services: encrypted/authenticated routing (integrity), authenticated DNS (confidentiality of name resolution), and resilient architecture (availability)【1†L1-L3】.
- **Resilience in Conflict and Crises** – In contexts of armed conflict or political crisis, protecting the “public core” helps maintain essential communications and prevents state‑orchestrated disruptions of the Internet’s backbone【4†L1-L4】.
- **Multistakeholder Trust** – By positioning core protocols as a public good and adopting transparent security extensions (DNSSEC, RPKI), the global community builds trust that the Internet will remain open and secure, even when individual actors might be tempted to intervene.

---

### Take‑away Summary

- **Core internet protocols** (IP, TCP/UDP, DNS, BGP, IXPs, cable systems) are the technical foundation of global connectivity.  
- **Network security** for these protocols is achieved through a combination of **technical standards** (e.g., newer protocol specifications, DNSSEC, RPKI) and **normative frameworks** that treat the core as a **global public good** protected from unwarranted state interference.  
- International fora such as the IGF repeatedly stress that **robust, standards‑based, and norm‑driven approaches** are essential to safeguard the confidentiality, integrity, and availability of the Internet’s core infrastructure.

</details>

<details><summary>Baseline answer</summary>

**What are ITU‑T Recommendations?**  

- They are formal technical documents produced by the **International Telecommunication Union – Telecommunication Standardisation Sector (ITU‑T)**.  
- Each Recommendation contains **provisions, guidelines, reference points or attributes** that network operators, service providers and other stakeholders are expected to follow when designing, interconnecting or operating telecommunication services.  
- The text of a Recommendation often **incorporates other ITU‑T Recommendations or external references**; by doing so, those incorporated provisions become part of the Recommendation itself [1].  

**Key characteristics drawn from the supplied excerpts**

| Feature | Detail (source) |
|----------|-----------------|
| **Number in force** | More than **2 600** ITU‑T Recommendations are currently active [3]. |
| **Living documents** | All Recommendations are **subject to revision**; users are urged to check for the most recent edition before applying them [1], [7]. |
| **Regular publication of valid list** | The ITU‑T regularly publishes an up‑to‑date list of the **currently valid Recommendations** [1], [5], [7]. |
| **Series organization** | Recommendations are grouped into **series** that reflect thematic focus: |
| – **Y‑Series** (e.g., Y.140 2000, Y.140.1 2004) – deals with **global information infrastructure (GII) reference points and interconnection frameworks** [1]. | |
| – **X‑Series** – provides **suggestions for Internet Service Providers** and related operational guidance [4], [8]. | |
| – **F‑Series** – focuses on **accessibility**, offering guidelines for **older persons and persons with disabilities** and serving as a reference for other ITU‑T work on accessibility [6]. | |
| **Purpose** | To give **technical, operational and policy guidance** that helps ensure interoperability, reliability, and inclusiveness of telecommunication networks worldwide. |

**Take‑away**

ITU‑T Recommendations are a **dynamic, structured body of standards** that underpin global telecom and internet infrastructure. They are continuously updated, organized by thematic series (Y, X, F, etc.), and more than 2 600 of them are presently in force, forming the backbone of international telecommunication policy and practice.

</details>

<details><summary>Graph-enriched answer</summary>

**What are ITU‑T Recommendations?**  

- **Standard‑setting documents** produced by the **International Telecommunication Union – Telecommunication Standardisation Sector (ITU‑T)**.  
- They are **referenced in other texts** and, by that reference, become the operative provisions of the citing document [1][5][7].  
- **All Recommendations are subject to revision**; users are urged to verify that they are applying the most recent edition of each Recommendation [1][7].  
- The **ITU‑T maintains a regularly‑published list of the currently valid Recommendations**; at the time of the cited material **more than 2 600 Recommendations are in force** [3].

3. When drafting policies, contracts, or technical specifications, **cite the relevant ITU‑T Recommendation**; the citation itself incorporates the Recommendation’s provisions into your document [1][5][7].  

These points capture the essence of the ITU‑T Recommendations as presented in the provided context.
