<details><summary>Baseline answer</summary>

**The future of learning in India – as outlined in the “AI 2.0 – The Future of Learning in India” material and the related fireside‑chat – can be captured in three inter‑linked strands: vision, infrastructure & capacity, and curriculum‑driven interventions.**  

| Dimension | What the speakers say about the future | Key points & implications |
|-----------|----------------------------------------|----------------------------|
| **Vision (2050‑2100)** | • India is imagined as a **“number‑one economy”** by mid‑century, driven by an AI‑enabled education system that removes language and connectivity barriers (e.g., villagers speaking Bhojpuri to the US, Russia, Japan)【1†L1-L7】. <br>• The goal is to “re‑imagine the education system” not just for today but for **India of 2050‑2100**【1†L1-L4】【4†L1-L4】. | • AI is seen as the catalyst that will lift the whole nation onto a “global leading economy” track. <br>• The ambition is to achieve this **“very soon”**, not merely by 2050, indicating an aggressive timeline. |
| **Scale & Capacity** | • **25 crore** children are in school and **4.6 crore** in higher education (≈30 crore total)【2†L1-L4】. <br>• India has **15 lakh schools**; only **4.4 million** of them have basic ICT assets (computers, labs, tablets)【2†L5-L7】. <br>• There are **≈1 crore teachers**, most of whom are women, but **few are AI‑savvy**【2†L9-L11】. | • The **“last‑mile” challenge** is to bring AI tools and literacy to the majority of schools that still lack basic digital infrastructure. <br>• Teacher up‑skilling is a critical bottleneck; gender balance is a positive factor, but AI competence must be built. |
| **Integrated & Collaborative Approach** | • The speaker notes an **“integrated approach”** that bridges school and higher‑education efforts, and calls for broader stakeholder help【2†L8-L10】. | • Coordination between K‑12, higher education, industry, and government is essential to scale AI adoption. |
| **Curriculum & Pedagogy** | • A **computational‑thinking and AI curriculum** is planned to start **from Grade 3**, with rollout expected in the next academic season【8†L1-L4】. <br>• The fireside chat stresses that success will be measured not by the number of AI tools used but by how well youth are **prepared to lead** in an AI era【7†L1-L4】. | • Early exposure to AI concepts aims to build a pipeline of AI‑literate citizens. <br>• Pedagogical shift (e.g., “flipping the classroom”) is envisaged to keep pace with rapid AI advances. |
| **Economic & Social Impact** | • AI is portrayed as the engine that will **“break connectivity barriers”** and enable villagers to communicate globally, thereby fostering inclusive growth【1†L5-L7】. | • By democratizing AI access, the vision seeks to reduce urban‑rural divides and empower a young demographic (≈44 million higher‑education students) to drive the economy. |

### Synthesis – What the future will look like

1. **AI‑Centric National Identity** – By 2050‑2100 India aims to be a leading AI‑driven economy, with education as the cornerstone of that transformation.  
2. **Massive Scale‑Up of Digital Infrastructure** – The government and partners must equip the remaining ~10 million schools with basic ICT facilities so AI tools can reach the “last mile.”  
3. **Teacher Upskilling & Gender Inclusion** – With ~1 crore teachers (mostly women), systematic AI‑literacy programmes are required to turn them into effective AI mentors.  
4. **Early‑Stage AI Curriculum** – Introducing computational thinking and AI concepts from Grade 3 will create a generation that can both use and innovate AI technologies.  
5. **Pedagogical Innovation** – Classroom models will shift (e.g., flipped classrooms) to focus on problem‑solving and leadership rather than mere tool deployment.  
6. **Inclusive Connectivity** – AI will enable even remote, vernacular‑speaking communities to engage globally, shrinking the digital divide and fueling economic growth.  

**Bottom line:** The roadmap presented in the sources envisions a **holistic, AI‑infused education ecosystem** that starts at the primary‑school level, upgrades infrastructure, empowers teachers, and ultimately positions India as a top‑ranked AI economy by the mid‑21st century.

</details>

<details><summary>Graph-enriched answer</summary>

**The future of learning in India – as outlined in the AI 2.0 and Fireside‑Chat material – can be grouped into four inter‑linked themes:**

| Theme | What the speakers envision | Key points from the source |
|-------|---------------------------|----------------------------|
| **1. Long‑term vision (2050‑2100)** | A re‑imagined education system that fuels India’s rise to the world’s leading economy. AI is seen as the catalyst that will break language and connectivity barriers, allowing anyone in a remote village to converse with the US, Russia or Japan. | “reimagining the education system in India… India of 2050, India of 2100… AI dismantles the barrier… we can speak in our Bhojpuri to US, to Russia to Japan” [1] |
| **2. Scale & infrastructure** | India must move from the current 4.4 million schools with basic ICT (computers, labs, tablets) to a truly AI‑enabled ecosystem that reaches the “last mile”. The challenge is huge because only a fraction of the 15 million schools are digitally equipped today. | “15 lakh schools… only 4 crore 4 lakh schools only having the computers ICT labs and tablets… a huge challenge to take the AI revolution to last mile” [2][5] |
| **3. Human capacity – teachers & students** | About 1 crore (10 million) school teachers exist, most of them women, but very few are AI‑savvy. Building AI literacy among teachers is a priority, as is embedding computational thinking and AI concepts for students from grade 3 onward. | “most of them are women… how many are AI savvy or AI literate we are working on that” [2][5]; “introduce a computational thinking and AI curriculum from grade 3” [8] |
| **4. Pedagogical shift & outcomes** | Success will not be measured by the number of AI tools in classrooms, but by how well the system prepares youth to lead the nation in an AI‑driven era. The emphasis is on flipping classrooms, fostering STEM, and cultivating leadership in AI. | “strength of our education system will not be measured by how many AI tools we are integrating… but how we are preparing our youth to lead our country in this era of AI” [7]; “flip the classrooms” [7] |

### Summarised outlook

1. **Economic ambition:** By 2050‑2100 India aims to be the **number‑one global economy**, and the education system is seen as the engine that will deliver that growth through AI‑enabled talent [1][4].

2. **Massive scale‑up needed:** With **≈25 crore** children in school and **≈4.6 crore** in higher education, the system must expand digital infrastructure from the current ~4.4 million ICT‑enabled schools to cover the entire **15 million‑plus** school network [2][5].

3. **AI‑literacy for teachers and students:** A concerted effort is under way to up‑skill the **≈1 crore** teachers (predominantly women) and to embed **computational thinking/AI curricula from grade 3** so that future learners are AI‑savvy from an early age [2][8].

4. **Pedagogical transformation:** The focus is on **flipping classrooms**, strengthening **STEM and AI education**, and measuring success by the ability of youth to **lead the nation in the AI era**, not merely by the count of AI tools deployed [7].

5. **Policy & leadership:** Government leaders (e.g., the NCT Chairman) have publicly committed to this high‑growth trajectory and are seeking collaborative help to realise the vision [2][5].

**In short, the future of learning in India is portrayed as a large‑scale, AI‑driven overhaul that will bridge digital gaps, create AI‑literate teachers and students, and ultimately power India’s ascent to a top‑ranked global economy by mid‑century.**

</details>

### Q72: what is ecmascript?

| | Baseline | Graph-Enriched |
|---|---|---|
| Time | 2.92s | 3.69s (+0.06s expansion) |
| Score avg | 4.2/5 | 5.0/5 |
| Expanded docs | — | 0 |
| **Winner** |  | **✓** |

> **Judge:** Answer A covers the definition, history, standardization bodies, and usage contexts with more concrete details and broader source citations than Answer B.

<details><summary>Baseline answer</summary>

ECMAScript is the **general‑purpose, cross‑platform, vendor‑neutral programming language** that underlies JavaScript and other web‑based scripting technologies.  

* It is the language standardized by **Ecma International** (see the ECMA‑262 language specification) and is described as “the general purpose programming language that is an essential part of the Web Browser application platform, but it is also widely used in many other environments”【6†L1-L5】.  
* The language originated from early technologies such as **JavaScript (Netscape)** and **JScript (Microsoft)** and has grown to become one of the world’s most widely used programming languages【2†L1-L4】.  
* Ecma’s TC 39 (and later TC 53) focus on its continued standardization, noting that ECMAScript is now used not only on the web but also in **embedded systems, micro‑controllers and single‑board computers**【3†L1-L5】.  
* Ecma International lists ECMAScript among its flagship standards, noting “several billion implementations” worldwide【4†L1-L2】.  

In short, ECMAScript is the standardized language specification that defines the behavior of JavaScript and related scripting runtimes across browsers, servers, mobile devices, and increasingly, resource‑constrained embedded platforms.

</details>

<details><summary>Graph-enriched answer</summary>

ECMAScript is the **standardized, general‑purpose programming language** that underlies JavaScript and JScript.  It is defined and maintained by **Ecma International** (ECMA‑262) as a cross‑platform, vendor‑neutral language used on the Web browser platform and increasingly in other environments such as embedded devices, servers, and mobile computers【2†L1-L4】【3†L1-L4】.  

Key points from the supplied material:

* **Origins and scope** – ECMAScript grew out of the early JavaScript (Netscape) and JScript (Microsoft) technologies and has become one of the world’s most widely used programming languages【2†L1-L3】.  
* **Standardization** – The language specification is produced by Ecma International’s TC39 (and related technical committees such as TC53 for modules on embedded systems) and is published as ECMA‑262, with related specifications like ECMA‑402 for internationalization【6†L4-L8】【7†L13-L16】.  
* **Cross‑platform, vendor‑neutral** – The language is intended to run on any computer, server, or mobile device, and its use is expanding to resource‑constrained hardware such as micro‑controllers and single‑board computers【3†L1-L4】.  
* **Broad adoption** – With “several billion implementations,” ECMAScript is one of the most used standards worldwide【4†L2-L3】.  

In short, ECMAScript is the **standard, vendor‑neutral, cross‑platform programming language that powers JavaScript on the web and beyond**, governed by Ecma International.

</details>

<details><summary>Baseline answer</summary>

The Internet Governance Forum (IGF) is the principal venue where **multistakeholder dialogue on Internet governance** takes place.  It is deliberately designed as an open, inclusive space where a wide array of actors can meet, share views and develop ideas about how the global Internet should be managed.

* **Who participates?** The dialogue brings together government representatives, civil‑society groups, the private sector, technical communities (e.g., standards bodies), and academia [1][3][7].  

* **What is the format?** The IGF hosts “main sessions,” “informal stakeholder consultations,” and regional “space” meetings (e.g., the LAC Space) that focus on specific topics such as ICANN, trust‑building, and future‑scenario planning [2][4][7][8].

* **Why the multistakeholder model?** Proponents argue that its **inclusiveness** and the ability to draw on insights from diverse sectors make it uniquely suited to address the complex, cross‑cutting challenges of the Internet [3][6].  

* **What has been discussed?** Over the years the IGF has covered a “wide range of Internet‑related topics” through this multistakeholder setting, from technical coordination to policy‑level trust frameworks [2][5][6].

In short, the multistakeholder dialogue on Internet governance is embodied in the IGF’s open, participatory processes that convene governments, civil society, the private sector, technical experts and scholars to discuss, debate and shape the future of the global Internet. [1][2][3][4][5][6][7][8]

</details>

<details><summary>Graph-enriched answer</summary>

The Internet Governance Forum (IGF) is built around a **multistakeholder dialogue** – a structured space where all parts of the Internet ecosystem meet to discuss policy, technical, and societal issues.  In this model the forum brings together:

* **Governments** – representing public policy and regulatory perspectives.  
* **Civil society** – NGOs, consumer groups, and advocacy organisations that voice public‑interest concerns.  
* **The private sector** – businesses and industry bodies that develop and operate Internet services.  
* **Technical communities** – engineers, standards bodies, and operators who manage the underlying infrastructure.  
* **Academia and research institutions** – providing evidence‑based analysis and long‑term foresight.

These constituencies regularly convene in IGF sessions, regional spaces (e.g., the LAC Space), and thematic workshops to exchange views on topics ranging from trust‑building to ICANN policies [1][2][4][7].  Leaders and experts repeatedly stress that the **inclusiveness** of this model allows the forum to draw on diverse insights, produce balanced recommendations, and maintain legitimacy for Internet governance decisions [3][6].  

Thus, the multistakeholder dialogue on Internet governance is a collaborative, open‑to‑all process that underpins the IGF’s role as a global platform for shaping the future of the Internet.

</details>

<details><summary>Baseline answer</summary>

The Internet Engineering Task Force (IETF) is an **open, international community** that brings together network designers, operators, vendors, and researchers. Its purpose is to develop and promote **voluntary technical standards** for the Internet, especially those that make up the Internet protocol suite.  

Key points about the IETF:

* **Open community** – anyone interested can participate; the group is not limited to any single nation or organization. [3]  
* **Focus on standards and best practices** – it produces technical and engineering documents, including protocol standards, “best current practice” notes, and informational RFCs (Requests for Comments). [3]  
* **Mission** – to create documents that help the Internet work better, ensuring smooth operation and the evolution of its architecture. [5]  
* **Outputs** – the IETF’s work is published as RFCs, which become the de‑facto specifications for Internet protocols and related practices. [3]  

In short, the IETF is the primary open standards organization that drives the development, refinement, and dissemination of the technical standards that underpin the global Internet. [6]

</details>

<details><summary>Graph-enriched answer</summary>

The Internet Engineering Task Force (IETF) is an **open, international community** made up of network designers, operators, vendors, and researchers. Its purpose is to develop and promote **voluntary Internet standards**, especially the technical specifications that form the Internet protocol suite.  

Key points about the IETF:

- **Open community** – anyone interested can participate in its work, which is organized around working groups that draft and discuss standards.  
- **Standard‑setting role** – the IETF produces technical and engineering documents (including protocol standards, best‑current‑practice guides, and informational notes) that are published as **Requests for Comments (RFCs)**.  
- **Mission** – to create documents that make the Internet work better, ensuring smooth operation and the evolution of Internet architecture.  
- **Scope** – its output covers protocol standards, best practices, and informational material that guide the design, deployment, and operation of the global Internet.

These details are drawn from the provided sources: the IETF is described as an open international community that develops technical standards and RFCs to improve Internet functionality [3]; it is a large open community concerned with Internet architecture and operation [5]; and it is an open standards organization focused on voluntary Internet standards [6].

Across the several remarks and panel discussions the participants reached a broad consensus on a handful of core points:

| Theme | What was said | Source |
|-------|---------------|--------|
| **International cooperation vs. isolation** | Delegates argued that national “AI‑sovereignty” ambitions must be balanced with collaborative safety standards; they favoured partnership‑based approaches rather than a “go‑it‑alone” stance. | [5], [7] |
| **Scientific rigor in AI evaluation** | The need for robust, peer‑reviewed methodologies to assess AI systems was highlighted as a prerequisite for any trustworthy global framework. | [4] |

| **Immediate vs. systemic risks** | The dialogue distinguished short‑term hazards (e.g., misuse, bias) from longer‑term, systemic threats (e.g., loss of control, runaway optimisation) and called for policies that address both horizons. | [4] |
| **Findings of a global AI‑safety report** | The session was built around a newly released global AI‑safety report chaired by Yoshua Bengio, which synthesized expert input and offered concrete recommendations for international policy coordination. | [6], [8] |

In sum, the global‑level discussion underscored that effective AI safety will require **coordinated international action grounded in rigorous science**, with special attention to **multi‑agent dynamics** and a **dual focus on both near‑term and long‑term risks**, while navigating the tension between national sovereignty and shared standards.

| **Balancing sovereignty and partnership** | While some nations pursue **AI sovereignty** to protect national interests, the speakers argued for **partnership‑based frameworks** that align standards across borders rather than fragmenting the ecosystem. | [5], [7] |

**Norway’s post‑COVID‑19 policy focus, as outlined in the supplied documents, can be grouped into three main strands:**

| Policy area | What the policy says | How it relates to the post‑COVID situation |
|-------------|----------------------|--------------------------------------------|
| **Economic restructuring & competitiveness** | • “Coronavirus measures that limit the ability to restructure the Norwegian economy should be terminated as soon as it is prudent to do so.” [1] <br>• “Many of the measures … such as those related to competence enhancement or development of digital public services … will not only help cope with the coronavirus crisis, but also leave Norway in a stronger position once the crisis is over.” [4] | • The government plans to lift pandemic‑related restrictions that have held back restructuring, allowing firms and sectors to adapt to the new normal. <br>• Investment in skills and digital public services is seen as a way to boost competitiveness and employment after the crisis. |
| **Digital transformation & innovation** | • “Norway will adjust and refine its approach in response to new knowledge and the ongoing rapid developments in the area of digitalisation.” [2] <br>• “Norway’s new humanitarian policy strategy includes a digital innovation programme.” [5] <br>• “Norway will revise regulations to eliminate barriers and promote clarity and consistency across agencies.” [6] | • A **living digital strategy** will keep policies up‑to‑date as the pandemic accelerates digital adoption. <br>• A dedicated digital‑innovation programme will be used to build capacity both at home and in partner countries, helping the economy rebound. <br>• Regulatory reforms aim to remove obstacles that could slow digital projects that were hastened during COVID‑19. |
| **International cooperation & multilateral digitalisation** | • “Norway will promote digitalisation in the governing bodies of relevant multilateral organisations … (World Bank, UNICEF, WHO, UNDP, etc.).” [3] <br>• “Norway plans to share digital knowledge and experience with partner countries through a new Digitalisation for development programme … intensify its support for local incubators and innovation centres … through participation in the World Bank’s infoDev programme.” [5] | • By helping multilateral bodies and partner states digitalise, Norway seeks to export the lessons learned from its own COVID‑era digital push, strengthening its diplomatic and development profile after the pandemic. |

### Key take‑aways

1. **Lift pandemic‑era restrictions** that have hampered economic restructuring, once it is safe to do so.  
2. **Invest heavily in digital competence and public‑service digitalisation** to both manage the immediate fallout of COVID‑19 and to create a more resilient, competitive economy.  
3. **Modernise regulations** to remove barriers to digital projects across government agencies.  
4. **Leverage multilateral platforms and development aid** to spread Norway’s digital‑innovation experience, supporting partner countries and reinforcing Norway’s role in global digital transformation.  

These elements together constitute Norway’s post‑COVID policy direction as presented in the provided excerpts.

**Norway’s post‑COVID‑19 policy – as outlined in the excerpts you provided – is built around three inter‑linked pillars: ending pandemic‑related restrictions, strengthening the economy through digitalisation and green growth, and using that digital capacity to boost Norway’s development and humanitarian work.**  

| Pillar | Main actions / commitments | Source |
|--------|---------------------------|--------|
| **1. Roll‑back of pandemic‑era constraints** | • Terminate coronavirus measures that limit the ability to restructure the Norwegian economy as soon as it is prudent to do so. | 【1】 |

Build a more competitive, green and digitally‑enabled economy** | • Focus on stronger competitiveness, reduced greenhouse‑gas emissions, green growth, a well‑functioning labour market and high employment. <br>• Invest in competence enhancement and the development of digital public services – measures that will help cope with the crisis now and leave Norway stronger afterwards. | 【1】【4】 |
| **3. Make digitalisation the engine of Norway’s development and humanitarian agenda** | • Adopt a “living” digital strategy that is continuously refined based on new knowledge, project experience, partner feedback and rapid digital‑technology developments. <br>• Promote digitalisation within the governing bodies of multilateral organisations (World Bank, WFP, UNICEF, WHO, UNDP, etc.). <br>• Launch a **Digitalisation for Development** programme to share digital know‑how, build skills and capacity in partner countries. <br>• Intensify support for local incubators and innovation centres, both through existing strategic partnerships and via participation in the World Bank’s **infoDev** programme that promotes entrepreneurship. <br>• Revise regulations to eliminate barriers and ensure clarity and consistency across agencies (the “One Digital Public Sector” strategy). <br>• Prioritise digital work in the ocean sector and for small‑island developing states, in line with Norway’s Oceans Strategy. | 【2】【3】【5】【6】【7】 |

### In short
- **End pandemic‑related economic restrictions** as soon as it is sensible.  
- **Drive a greener, more competitive economy** through labour‑market reforms, emissions cuts and a strong push for digital public services and skills development.  
- **Embed digitalisation across all of Norway’s foreign‑development and humanitarian work**, partnering with multilateral bodies, sharing expertise with partner countries, supporting start‑ups and incubators, and streamlining public‑sector regulations.  

These measures are presented as a coordinated, forward‑looking “post‑COVID” agenda that will both help Norway recover from the pandemic and position it for sustainable growth and influence in the digital age.

<details><summary>Baseline answer</summary>

**Key points of disagreement that have stalled a final UN Cybercrime Convention**

| Area of dispute | What the disagreement concerns | Why it matters | Sources |
|----------------|--------------------------------|----------------|---------|
| **Scope of the treaty** | • How broadly “cybercrime” should be defined (e.g., only “serious crimes” vs. any offense under domestic law). <br>• Whether the treaty should cover cross‑border surveillance and data‑collection activities that are not directly linked to a criminal investigation. | A vague or overly‑broad scope could force states to apply the same intrusive measures to low‑level offences, creating legal inconsistency across jurisdictions. | [1] , [2] |
| **Human‑rights safeguards** | • Requirement (or lack thereof) for prior judicial authorization before accessing data. <br>• Transparency and oversight mechanisms for any data‑access or interception. | Without mandatory safeguards, the convention could erode privacy and due‑process rights, undermining international human‑rights standards. | [1] , [6] |
| **Intrusive technical powers** | • Permission for real‑time data collection and communication interception without strong, enforceable human‑rights protections. | Such powers raise concerns about mass surveillance and the potential for abuse by states with weaker rule‑of‑law controls. | [1] |
| **International‑cooperation provisions** | • The exact form of cross‑border assistance, evidence‑sharing, and “surveillance cooperation” that states must provide. <br>• Whether cooperation can be compelled without respecting domestic privacy laws. | The cooperation chapter is the most contested part of the draft, as some states fear it could obligate them to assist in surveillance that conflicts with their own legal standards. | [5] |
| **Compatibility with existing instruments** | • Whether the new UN treaty would duplicate, conflict with, or weaken the Budapest Convention and other regional agreements. | A treaty that is incompatible with existing frameworks could create a fragmented global regime and lower overall standards for privacy and human rights. | [4] |
| **Disparities in privacy protection** | • Different levels of privacy guarantees among negotiating states, leading to an uneven baseline for the treaty. | This could result in a “race‑to‑the‑bottom” where the convention adopts the lowest common denominator, weakening global privacy norms. | [6] |

**In short:** Negotiators are split over how far the treaty should reach (its criminal scope and surveillance powers), what mandatory human‑rights safeguards must be built in (judicial oversight, transparency), how international cooperation should be structured, and whether the new instrument can coexist with the Budapest Convention without diluting existing privacy protections. These unresolved issues have prevented consensus in earlier sessions and continue to block a final agreement.

</details>

<details><summary>Graph-enriched answer</summary>

The negotiations have repeatedly stalled around a handful of “red‑line” issues that the parties cannot yet reconcile:

| Area of disagreement | What the dispute is about | Why it matters |
|------------------------|---------------------------|----------------|
| **Scope of the treaty** | States cannot agree on how broadly the convention should apply –‑ whether it should cover only “serious crimes” or be extended to any offence that a domestic law labels as cyber‑related, and how far cross‑border surveillance cooperation may go. | A vague scope could produce uneven implementation and allow states to invoke the treaty for low‑level offences, undermining uniform global standards. |
| **Human‑rights safeguards** | There is no consensus on mandatory protections such as prior judicial authorization, transparency of data access, and strong, enforceable human‑rights clauses. Some delegations want only “soft” safeguards that would be left to national law, while others (e.g., EFF’s Katitza Rodriguez) demand binding safeguards. | Without robust safeguards, the treaty could legitimize intrusive state surveillance and weaken privacy and due‑process rights. |
| **Intrusive powers (real‑time data collection & interception)** | Delegates are divided over whether the text should permit real‑time collection of data or interception of communications without explicit, mandatory human‑rights protections. | Allowing such powers without strict oversight would set a low bar for privacy protection and could conflict with existing international human‑rights instruments. |
| **International‑cooperation mechanisms** | The chapter on mutual assistance and evidence‑sharing is contentious; states differ on the level of obligation, procedural safeguards, and the extent of data‑exchange that can be required. | Overly expansive cooperation could force states to share data that conflict with their own privacy laws, while too narrow a regime would limit the treaty’s effectiveness against transnational cybercrime. |
| **Compatibility with existing instruments** | Critics warn the new UN treaty could be “incompatible with existing instruments” such as the Budapest Convention, especially if it lowers the bar for human‑rights protections. | Incompatibility could create a fragmented legal landscape, where states are forced to choose between competing regimes. |
| **Disparities in privacy protection across jurisdictions** | Negotiators acknowledge “disparities in privacy protections” and fear the convention could cement a lower global floor for privacy rights. | This would risk a “decline in global standards for human rights and privacy protection,” as noted by observers. |

These points are repeatedly highlighted in the negotiation reports and statements from participants such as Katitza Rodriguez, Alexander Seger, and other policy experts [1][4][5][6]. The lack of agreement on these core issues is why a final UN Cybercrime Convention has not yet been adopted.

</details>

Plenary discussions are the full‑member‑state meetings that open a session, review the agenda in detail and then move on to the substantive items on the table.  In the context you provided they:

* Begin with a “thorough scrutiny of the agenda” as a universally‑endorsed first step [1].  
* Focus on the substantive issues set out in paragraph 1 of General Assembly resolution 75/240 (e.g., the “pillars” that must be debated and decided) [2].  
* Are meant to be enriched by the work of thematic groups and other negotiation tracks [6].  
* Include the presentation of interim reports and any related documentation [7].  
* Are explicitly described as involving **all** member states, with an emphasis on capacity‑building to boost resilience and resource mobilisation [8].

In short, plenary discussions are the all‑member, agenda‑driven deliberations where the core issues of the meeting are debated, decisions are taken, and capacity‑building outcomes are shared.

</details>

<details><summary>Graph-enriched answer</summary>

Plenary discussions are the full‑member‑state sessions that book‑end the meeting. In this context they are used to:

* **Scrutinise the agenda** – the meeting “starts with a thorough scrutiny of the agenda” and the plenary is the forum where that review takes place [1].
* **Debate substantive issues** – agenda item 5 calls for “discussions on substantive issues contained in paragraph 1 of General Assembly resolution 75/240,” and the plenary “discusses the pillars and decide” [2].
* **Involve all member states** – the closure note states that “Plenary discussions would involve all member states, and capacity building would improve resilience and resource mobilization” [8].
* **Present and adopt outcomes** – after thematic or negotiating groups have worked, “Presentation of outcomes to the plenary” is made [3].
* **Enrich thematic work** – the thematic groups are meant to “enrich the discussions in the plenary session” [6].

Thus, plenary discussions are the all‑member, high‑level deliberations where the agenda is reviewed, substantive policy items are debated, decisions are taken, and the results of breakout or negotiating groups are reported and adopted. [1][2][3][6][8]

<details><summary>Baseline answer</summary>

Youth voices are increasingly recognised as essential drivers of fair and effective digital policies.  Across several sessions and initiatives, young people are called on to:

* **Shape data‑economy rules** – In WS #32 participants stressed that “awareness and advocacy can contribute to more equitable and effective digital policies” and that youth participation is key to both digital governance and data‑governance [1][7].  

* **Defend human rights online** – A dedicated session on “Human rights in digital spaces” highlighted the need for youth to champion rights such as privacy, freedom of expression and non‑discrimination in the internet environment [2].  

* **Influence AI design** – WS #376 focused on “Elevating Children’s Voices in AI Design,” with representatives like Joon Baek of Youth for Privacy emphasizing that youth NGOs can bring a privacy‑centred perspective to emerging technologies [3].  

* **Protect children’s digital rights** – The High‑Level Session 5 on “Protecting Children’s Rights in the Digital World” called for solutions that give children the “opportunities and the means” to safeguard their online safety and agency [4].  

* **Counter disinformation and shrinkage of civic space** – The Kofi Annan Foundation’s Digitalise Youth Project, part of the Digital Democracy Initiative, works to “empower local youth activists and civil society organisations” to promote digital rights and combat misinformation in the Sahel region [5].  

* **Address unique vulnerabilities** – Youth are identified as facing “unique vulnerabilities in the digital age,” underscoring why their perspectives must be integrated into policy‑making to avoid marginalisation [8].

**Take‑away:**  When young people are meaningfully included—from grassroots advocacy to high‑level policy forums—they help ensure that digital rights frameworks are inclusive, privacy‑respecting, and resilient against emerging threats such as AI bias and disinformation.  Their participation not only amplifies their own rights but also strengthens the overall health of the digital ecosystem.

</details>

<details><summary>Graph-enriched answer</summary>

**Why Youth Voices Matter for Digital Rights**

1. **Unique Perspectives and Vulnerabilities**  
   - Young people experience the digital world differently from older generations and often face *“unique vulnerabilities in the digital age”* [8]. Their day‑to‑day interactions with social media, online learning, and emerging technologies give them first‑hand insight into privacy risks, algorithmic bias, and the impact of disinformation.

2. **Driving Equitable Policy**  
   - Workshops such as **WS #32 – “Harnessing Youth Voices to Transform the Data Economy”** stress that *“awareness and advocacy can contribute to more equitable and effective digital policies”* [1]. When youth are consulted, policies are more likely to address issues like data ownership, consent, and accessibility that directly affect them.

3. **Advocacy in Specialized Areas**  
   - Youth‑led NGOs are already shaping the agenda. For example, **Youth for Privacy**, represented by Joon Baek at **WS #376 – “Elevating Children’s Voices in AI Design,”** focuses specifically on *digital privacy* for young users [3]. Their technical input helps ensure AI systems respect children’s rights.

4. **Building Capacity and Counter‑Disinformation**  
   - The **Digitalise Youth Project** (part of the Kofi Annan Foundation’s Digital Democracy Initiative) “seeks to address the shrinking civic space and rampant disinformation … by empowering local youth activists and civil‑society organisations” [5]. This capacity‑building translates into stronger, youth‑driven watchdogs and advocacy networks.

5. **High‑Level Commitment to Children’s Rights**  
   - In **High‑Level Session 5: Protecting Children’s Rights in the Digital World**, the call to action emphasizes giving children *“all the opportunities and the means”* to shape their digital futures [4]. This signals that senior policymakers recognize youth as essential stakeholders.

---

### How to Amplify Youth Voices for Digital Rights

| Action | Rationale | Example from Context |
|--------|-----------|----------------------|
| **Include youth representatives in policy‑making bodies** | Guarantees that legislation reflects lived digital experiences. | WS #32 calls for *youth participation in digital governance* [1][7]. |
| **Fund youth‑led NGOs and networks** | Provides resources for research, advocacy, and capacity‑building. | Digitalise Youth receives support to empower activists in the Sahel [5]. |
| **Create dedicated youth panels on AI and data** | Allows technical expertise from young innovators to influence design. | Youth for Privacy’s participation in AI design discussions [3]. |
| **Integrate digital‑rights education in schools** | Builds a generation that can critically assess privacy, data, and disinformation. | The broader emphasis on *awareness* in WS #32 [1]. |
| **Establish feedback mechanisms for children’s online experiences** | Enables continuous monitoring of harms and rapid policy adjustments. | High‑Level Session 5’s *call to action* for children’s opportunities [4]. |

---

### Key Take‑aways

- **Youth are not just beneficiaries** of digital‑rights frameworks; they are *active agents* who can identify gaps, propose solutions, and hold governments and tech firms accountable.  
- **Youth‑led advocacy** (e.g., Youth for Privacy, Digitalise Youth) already demonstrates concrete impact on privacy, disinformation, and civic space.  
- **Institutionalizing youth participation**—through advisory councils, funding streams, and education—ensures that digital policies remain *equitable, effective, and future‑proof*.

By systematically elevating youth voices, the global community can craft digital rights regimes that protect today’s children and empower tomorrow’s digital citizens.

</details>

> **Judge:** Answer B covers a broader range of impacts (including conflict) and uses citations more comprehensively, while both remain generally accurate but similarly vague.

<details><summary>Baseline answer</summary>

**Social impacts**

* **Public‑service delivery and everyday life** – Digital technologies have reshaped how people obtain public services and how communities interact, learn and spend leisure time.  While many changes are positive, they also raise concerns about human‑rights protection, education quality, health outcomes, employment security and overall welfare [1].  

* **Conflict‑related disruption** – Armed conflict produces massive social dislocation, breaking families, displacing populations and eroding community cohesion [2].  

* **Road‑safety inequality** – In the mobility sector, road‑traffic accidents disproportionately affect the most productive segments of the population, creating a clear social burden [6].  

* **Platform‑driven societal change** – The rapid growth of digital platforms influences social behaviour, political participation and public discourse, adding new layers of social risk and opportunity [7].  

* **Inequality and governance** – Rising socioeconomic inequality can generate profound political consequences that destabilise societies, leading to legislative dead‑lock or even governmental paralysis [8].

**Economic impacts**

* **Employment and labour markets** – The transformation of service provision and the spread of digital tools affect job creation, skill demands and employment stability, with both opportunities and threats to workers’ livelihoods [1].  

* **Conflict‑driven economic loss** – Wars and violent unrest impose huge economic costs on nations, destroying infrastructure, reducing productivity and shrinking GDP [2].  

* **Broad‑scope technical effects** – Any new technology can trigger a range of economic consequences; the OECD notes that the list of possible outcomes is extensive and varies by sector [3][4].  

* **Digital platform economies** – Platforms generate new business models, market efficiencies and revenue streams, but also concentrate economic power and can reshape competition [7].  

* **Inequality‑related growth drag** – When inequality deepens, it can undermine economic growth by making governance harder, leading to policy gridlock and reduced investor confidence [8].

**Overall picture**

Across the cited sources, the social and economic impacts of digital transformation, conflict and inequality are tightly inter‑linked.  Technological change reshapes public services, labour markets and daily life, while also exposing vulnerabilities—particularly for disadvantaged groups.  At the same time, economic outcomes range from new growth opportunities (e.g., platform‑based commerce) to severe losses (e.g., conflict‑driven destruction or the economic drag of entrenched inequality).  Policymakers must therefore address both dimensions together to maximise benefits and minimise harms.  

*Sources: [1], [2], [3], [4], [5], [6], [7], [8].*

</details>

<details><summary>Graph-enriched answer</summary>

**Social impacts**

* **Human‑rights and welfare concerns** – The rapid digital transformation has raised questions about the protection of human rights, education, health and the overall welfare of individuals and communities [1].  
* **Road‑safety inequality** – Intelligent‑transport systems can change accident patterns, and the most productive segments of the population are disproportionately affected by road‑traffic injuries [6].  
* **Political destabilisation from inequality** – Deepening social inequality can produce profound political consequences, such as legislative dead‑locks or even full government paralysis [8].  
* **Broad societal change** – The shift to digital platforms reshapes how people interact, consume information and spend their time, producing wide‑ranging social effects [7][5].

**Economic impacts**

* **Transformation of public‑service delivery** – Digital technologies have altered the provision of public services, creating new economic opportunities while also disrupting traditional models [1].  
* **Economic drag from social inequality** – When inequality fuels political dysfunction, it can undermine economic growth and make a country harder to govern [8].  
* **Economic, social and political consequences of platform use** – The expanding role of digital platforms generates a complex mix of economic effects, from new market dynamics to shifts in labor patterns [7].  
* **Broad, non‑exhaustive consequences** – Technical changes in the digital economy can trigger a wide range of economic and social outcomes, many of which are still being mapped [3][4].

Together, these excerpts illustrate that digital and technological change produces intertwined social and economic impacts: they affect human rights, health, safety and political stability, while simultaneously reshaping service delivery, market structures and overall economic growth.

</details>

<details><summary>Baseline answer</summary>

The **Internet Governance Forum (IGF)** is a **multistakeholder policy‑dialogue platform** that brings together governments, the private sector, civil society, the technical community and academia to discuss public‑policy issues concerning the key elements of Internet governance as defined in Paragraph 72 of the Tunis Agenda of the World Summit on the Information Society (WSIS) [1][2].  

The Forum is deliberately **non‑decision‑making**; it is designed as an open space where participants can share ideas, explore solutions and strengthen the engagement of all stakeholders—especially those from developing countries—in existing and future Internet‑governance mechanisms [3][8].  

In practice, the IGF convenes sessions on topics such as access, security, diversity, openness, and sovereignty, and it serves as a venue for “cyber‑diplomacy” and the development of equitable frameworks for data and Internet governance [4][5][6][7].

</details>

<details><summary>Graph-enriched answer</summary>

The **Internet Governance Forum (IGF)** is a multistakeholder platform created to foster open, inclusive dialogue on public‑policy issues that affect the Internet.  It was established under Paragraph 72 of the Tunis Agenda of the World Summit on the Information Society (WSIS) and is expressly designed as a **forum for multistakeholder policy dialogue** rather than a decision‑making body.  Participants—governments, the private sector, civil society, the technical community and academia—come together to discuss the key elements of Internet governance, identify challenges (such as access, security, diversity and openness), and explore possible solutions, especially for developing countries【1†L1-L4】【8†L1-L3】.

<details><summary>Baseline answer</summary>

Digital diplomacy – also called e‑diplomacy, web diplomacy or online diplomacy – is the practice of using digital technologies, especially the Internet and social‑media platforms, to carry out diplomatic activities and pursue foreign‑policy goals.  It reshapes how states and international actors communicate, negotiate, exchange information, hold virtual meetings, and engage publics through digital platforms, while also bringing new agenda items such as privacy, data protection and cybersecurity into diplomatic discourse【2†L1-L3】【4†L1-L5】【5†L1-L4】【7†L1-L3】.

</details>

<details><summary>Graph-enriched answer</summary>

Digital diplomacy is the practice of conducting diplomatic activities — negotiation, representation, public outreach, cultural exchange, and other foreign‑policy functions — through digital technologies, especially the Internet and social‑media platforms.  It encompasses the way diplomats and foreign‑policy actors use online tools (Twitter, Facebook, Instagram, video‑conferencing, dedicated digital platforms, etc.) to communicate, negotiate, share information, and address new agenda items such as privacy, data protection and cybersecurity.  In short, it is “the use of the Internet and social media to achieve diplomatic objectives” and reflects how the diplomatic environment and its tools have been transformed by ICTs [2][4][5][6][7][8].

</details>

### Q90: test diplomacy

| | Baseline | Graph-Enriched |
|---|---|---|
| Time | 4.65s | 4.54s (+0.10s expansion) |
| Score avg | 4.8/5 | 4.8/5 |
| Expanded docs | — | 5 |

> **Judge:** Both answers are accurate and well‑sourced, but Answer A provides slightly more concrete details (e.g., the 1963 treaty) while covering the key points, giving it the edge in specificity.

<details><summary>Baseline answer</summary>

**“Test diplomacy”** – as it appears in the supplied material – is the idea that diplomatic activity itself can be treated like a *test* or *beta‑phase* in which concepts, norms, and even technologies are tried out, evaluated, and refined before they become permanent parts of the international system.

| How the sources describe it | What it tells us about “test diplomacy” |
|----------------------------|----------------------------------------|
| *“In the past test‑ban treaties were the result of bilateral Soviet‑American negotiations… a good example is the negotiation of a nuclear test ban.”*【1†L1-L3】 | Diplomatic negotiations are used to **test** the feasibility of major arms‑control measures (e.g., the 1963 Nuclear Test Ban Treaty). |
| *“The treaty was an agreement between the USA, the Soviet Union, and the UK to ban the testing of nuclear weapons… One interesting example of disarmament diplomacy is the Nuclear Test Ban Treaty of 1963.”*【3†L1-L4】 | The treaty itself is a **test** of collective disarmament diplomacy, showing how states can experiment with new normative frameworks. |
| *“Diplomacy today resembles a beta version: functional but still being tested, adapted, and iterated in real time… Rules are incomplete, institutions lag behind, and norms are tested while technologies are already deployed.”*【4†L1-L4】 | Modern diplomacy is explicitly framed as a **beta‑testing** environment where rules and norms are continuously trialed. |
| *“This allows for testing new ideas and building personal relationships in a low‑pressure environment.”*【6†L1-L2】 | **Security diplomacy** provides a sandbox for trying out innovative approaches without the high stakes of formal negotiations. |
| *“Workshops were going on. It is about diplomacy. I was curious and looked at the IGF programme.”*【5†L1-L3】 | Multi‑stakeholder workshops (e.g., at the Internet Governance Forum) act as **testing grounds** for diplomatic processes in the digital policy arena. |

### In short
“Test diplomacy” refers to the **experimental, iterative nature of diplomatic practice**—whether it is the negotiation of nuclear test‑ban treaties, the ongoing “beta” state of today’s diplomatic norms, or the low‑pressure workshops that let new ideas be tried out. The concept emphasizes that diplomacy is not a static set of rules but a **continuous testing and learning process**.

</details>

<details><summary>Graph-enriched answer</summary>

**“Test diplomacy” — what it means and how it shows up in practice**

| Aspect of “test diplomacy” | What the sources say | How it fits the idea of “testing” in diplomacy |
|----------------------------|----------------------|----------------------------------------------|
| **Treaties that arise from concrete “tests” of cooperation** | The nuclear‑test‑ban negotiations were carried out as bilateral talks between the Soviet Union and the United States and later expanded to a multilateral agreement (USA, USSR, UK) that banned atmospheric, underwater and space testing [1][2][3]. | The very subject of the treaty – *nuclear testing* – became a literal test of whether the great powers could agree to limit a dangerous activity. The diplomatic process itself was a test of the bilateral‑to‑multilateral transition. |
| **Diplomacy treated as a “beta version”** | Modern diplomacy is described as “functional but still being tested, adapted, and iterated in real time” with incomplete rules, lagging institutions and norms that are being tried out while technologies are already deployed [4]. | Here “testing” is metaphorical: diplomatic practices, norms and institutions are continuously trialled (like software beta‑testing) to see what works in a fast‑moving, algorithm‑driven environment. |
| **Low‑pressure environments for trying out ideas** | Security diplomacy “allows for testing new ideas and building personal relationships in a low‑pressure environment” [6]. | This is a direct reference to using diplomatic settings (track‑II meetings, workshops, informal dialogues) as test‑beds for innovative concepts before they are rolled out in formal negotiations. |
| **Workshops and forums as experimental venues** | The IGF (Internet Governance Forum) programme is mentioned as a place where “it is about diplomacy” and workshops were ongoing [5]. | The IGF’s workshops function as experimental spaces where digital‑policy ideas are tried, debated, and refined—another form of diplomatic testing. |

### Putting it together

“**Test diplomacy**” can be understood in two complementary ways:

1. **Concrete testing of contentious activities** – e.g., the nuclear‑test‑ban negotiations used the very act of *testing* (nuclear explosions) as the subject of diplomatic restriction, turning a dangerous practice into a catalyst for diplomatic agreement [1][3].

2. **Diplomacy itself as a testing ground** – modern diplomatic work is likened to a software beta: norms, rules, and institutions are continuously trialled, iterated, and refined, especially in fast‑evolving fields like AI and internet governance [4][6][5].

Thus, “test diplomacy” refers both to diplomatic efforts that arise from the need to regulate or limit a specific test (e.g., nuclear testing) and to the broader practice of using diplomatic forums, workshops, and informal tracks as experimental platforms to trial new ideas, norms, and technologies before they become formal policy.

</details>

<details><summary>Baseline answer</summary>

**Why sharing research & experience matters**

| Aspect | What the sources say | Why it matters for you |
|--------|----------------------|------------------------|
| **Practical experience builds judgment** | “Practical experience helps develop judgment on sharing research” (Davos 2025)【1†L1-L2】. Maria Leptin notes that researchers who have “hands‑on” experience are better able to weigh the risk of being scooped against the benefit of feedback【6†L1-L4】. | When you have actually done the work, you can more accurately assess what parts are safe to disclose, what needs protection, and what will benefit the community most. |
| **Knowledge exchange fuels collaboration** | “Data sharing promotes collaboration and knowledge exchange” (High‑Level Leaders Session)【3†L1-L2】. | Open data and findings let others build on your work, avoid duplication, and accelerate innovation. |
| **Experience sharing across regions & layers** | CSIRT practitioners stress that “different regions have various experiences that can be shared for mutual benefit” and that information should flow “between layers”【8†L1-L4】. | Cross‑regional or cross‑disciplinary sharing uncovers solutions that work in one context but not another, widening the pool of best practices. |
| **Beyond documents – real‑world stories** | “How we show the fact or knowledge, experience, sharing those things is quite important, not only by document, by real experience” (IGF 2023 Open Forum)【2†L1-L2】. | Narrative accounts, case studies, and lessons learned convey nuance that formal papers often miss. |
| **Networking & informal exchange** | “If any of you heard something interesting… you can share as well… exchange our comments” (IGF 2023 Networking Session)【4†L1-L3】. | Informal chats can surface insights quickly, fostering trust and rapid problem‑solving. |
| **Encouraging a culture of sharing** | Opening Session of IGF 2023 reminds us “if we’re going to share information, we should be able to share our experiences”【7†L1-L2】. | Institutional support (e.g., policies, incentives) normalises sharing as a professional norm. |

---

### Practical steps to share research **and** experience

1. **Document the “why” and “how”**  
   *Write a brief narrative (1‑2 pages) that explains the motivation, methodology, challenges, and lessons learned.*  
   - Attach this to any formal paper, pre‑print, or dataset.  
   - Use a consistent template so peers know where to find the experiential insights.

2. **Choose the right venue**  
   - **Pre‑prints & open repositories** for rapid dissemination (e.g., arXiv, Zenodo).  
   - **Conference talks or poster sessions** to get live feedback and discuss risks (as in the Davos example).  
   - **Community forums / CSIRT meetings** for operational experiences (e.g., IGF networking sessions).  

3. **Apply risk‑aware sharing**  
   - Conduct a **“scoop‑vs‑feedback” matrix**: list what could be taken, what feedback you need, and the impact of each.  
   - Use **embargoes** or **partial releases** (share methodology but keep raw data private until vetted).  

4. **Leverage cross‑regional networks**  
   - Join **regional working groups** (e.g., IGF’s CSIRT spot) to exchange context‑specific lessons.  
   - Publish “regional case studies” that highlight local constraints and successes.  

5. **Encourage two‑way exchange**  
   - After presenting, **invite comments** and ask participants to share their own analogous experiences.  
   - Use collaborative platforms (e.g., GitHub Issues, Slack channels) to keep the conversation alive.  

6. **Recognise and reward sharing**  
   - Cite experiential write‑ups just as you would a research article.  
   - Institutions can include “knowledge‑transfer activities” in promotion criteria.  

---

### Quick checklist for a “share‑ready” research output

| ✅ | Item |
|---|------|
| 1 | Core research results (paper, data, code) are openly accessible. |
| 2 | A concise “experience note” accompanies the core output (motivation, pitfalls, mitigation). |
| 3 | Risk assessment completed – any sensitive details are redacted or delayed. |
| 4 | Submission venue selected for both scholarly impact *and* community relevance. |
| 5 | Call‑to‑action included (e.g., “We welcome feedback on X; please share your own experiences with Y”). |
| 6 | Metadata tags indicate the content is both **research** and **experience** (e.g., `#case-study`, `#lessons-learned`). |

---

### Bottom line

Sharing **both** the formal research artifacts *and* the lived experience behind them creates a virtuous cycle: it sharpens individual judgment, fuels collaborative innovation, and spreads practical know‑how across regions and organisational layers. By embedding experience notes, using risk‑aware release strategies, and participating in community‑driven forums (like IGF sessions), you can maximise the impact of your work while safeguarding what needs protection.

</details>

<details><summary>Graph-enriched answer</summary>

**Why sharing research and experiences matters**

1. **Develops sound judgment** – Practical experience helps scientists learn when and how to disclose new results.  As Maria Leptin notes, “practical experience in research helps scientists develop judgment on sharing their work” [6].  

2. **Boosts collaboration and knowledge exchange** – High‑level leaders repeatedly stress that “data sharing promotes collaboration and knowledge exchange” [3].  When researchers and practitioners exchange findings, they can build on each other’s work faster and avoid duplicated effort.  

3. **Creates mutual benefit across regions and sectors** – The IGF CSIRT networking session highlighted that “different regions have various experiences that can be shared for mutual benefit” and that information should flow not only within a single layer but *between* layers [8].  

4. **Improves accountability and responsible practice** – Openly sharing both documents and lived experience supports “socially, economically, environmentally responsible campuses” [2] and reinforces ethical standards in research and security.  

5. **Enables rapid feedback and risk mitigation** – Researchers must balance the risk of being “scooped” against the benefit of early feedback; sharing at conferences or forums provides that feedback loop while also surfacing potential security or ethical concerns [6].

---

**How to share research and experiences effectively**

| Step | Action | Rationale / Supporting Quote |
|------|--------|------------------------------|
| **1. Combine documentation with lived examples** | Publish papers, reports, and case studies *and* accompany them with narratives of what actually happened on the ground. | “How we show the fact or knowledge, experience, sharing those things is quite important, not only by document, by real experience.” [2] |
| **2. Use structured forums and networking sessions** | Present at IGF open forums, high‑level sessions, or CSIRT practitioner meet‑ups. | “So sharing information is one thing… we will exchange our comments and try to understand what was going to be discussed.” [4] |
| **3. Encourage cross‑layer communication** | Facilitate dialogue between technical teams, policy makers, and community groups so that insights travel vertically and horizontally. | “Information sharing should not be only within certain layers but also between those layers to build understanding.” [8] |
| **4. Balance openness with protection** | Before public disclosure, assess the risk of being scooped or exposing vulnerabilities; use embargoes, selective sharing, or trusted‑partner channels when needed. | “Example of researchers deciding whether to present new results at conferences, balancing the risk of being scooped against the benefits of feedback.” [6] |
| **5. Capture regional perspectives** | Invite participants from diverse geographic and cultural contexts to contribute their local experiences. | “Different regions have different experiences that can be shared for mutual benefit.” [8] |
| **6. Institutionalise sharing practices** | Embed experience‑sharing into organizational policies (e.g., mandatory post‑incident debriefs, research‑ethics training that includes sharing case studies). | “I would like to share our experience.” [5] |
| **7. Leverage high‑visibility platforms** | Use high‑level sessions (e.g., Davos, IGF opening sessions) to broadcast key lessons and attract broader stakeholder attention. | “So if we're going to share information, we should be able to share our experiences.” [7] |

---

**Key take‑aways**

- **Practical experience is a catalyst for better judgment** on when and how to share research [6].
- **Structured, multi‑layered forums** (IGF sessions, CSIRT meet‑ups, high‑level panels) are proven venues for exchanging both data and lived experience [3][4][8][7].
- **Balancing openness with risk** (e.g., scooping, security exposure) is essential; use selective sharing mechanisms when appropriate [6].
- **Regional diversity enriches the collective pool** of knowledge, so actively solicit and disseminate experiences from varied contexts [8].
- **Embedding sharing into policy and culture** ensures that the practice becomes routine rather than ad‑hoc [5][2].

By following these principles, researchers and practitioners can turn isolated insights into a shared, responsible, and impactful body of knowledge.

</details>

| Expanded docs | — | 0 |

> **Judge:** Both answers are complete and accurate, but A provides more concrete details (e.g., ECMA‑262, single‑board computers) and uses precise source citations, giving it higher specificity and source usage.

<details><summary>Baseline answer</summary>

ECMAScript is the **general‑purpose, cross‑platform, vendor‑neutral programming language** that underlies JavaScript and is a core component of the Web browser application platform.  It is defined by Ecma International in the ECMAScript Language Specification (ECMA‑262) and includes a built‑in library that programs can use at runtime.  While originally created for the web, its use has expanded to many other environments, including embedded systems and resource‑constrained devices such as micro‑controllers and single‑board computers【1†L1-L4】【2†L1-L4】.  

In short, ECMAScript is the standardized language specification that describes the syntax, semantics, execution environment, and standard libraries for the JavaScript programming language【4†L4-L7】【5†L1-L3】.

</details>

<details><summary>Graph-enriched answer</summary>

ECMAScript is the **general‑purpose, cross‑platform, vendor‑neutral programming language** that underlies JavaScript and JScript. It is the language defined by Ecma International for use on the Web browser application platform and in many other environments, including embedded and resource‑constrained devices. The ECMAScript specifications describe the language’s syntax and semantics, its execution environment, and the built‑in libraries that programs can use [1][4][5].

</details>

India is being framed as a **mid‑level global leader and a stabilising democratic force in AI governance**.  The key points from the supplied material are:

* **Strategic positioning** – India should leverage its proven digital public‑infrastructure (UPI, digital identity) to shape AI governance, while steering clear of the overly restrictive European model, the laissez‑faire U.S. approach, or the state‑controlled Chinese system [1][6].

* **Economic competitiveness** – The Stanford AI Index ranks India **third worldwide in AI market competitiveness**, giving it credibility as an AI‑leader and a natural host for the Global‑South perspective [3][5].

* **Democratic influence** – As the world’s largest democracy with **over 100 million AI users**, India is seen as pivotal in deciding whether “democratic AI” or “autocratic AI” will dominate the future, analogous to Europe’s and China’s historic adoption of the printing press [7].

* **Sovereign AI ambition** – India’s large population provides a data advantage that can underpin a sovereign AI ecosystem, reinforcing its technological independence [4].

* **Stabilising role** – Speakers describe India’s rise as one that **does not seek domination** but rather acts as a stabilising force in global AI development [2].

**In summary:** India’s AI position is that of a competitively strong, democratically grounded, and strategically independent player—ranked third in market competitiveness, poised to champion “democratic AI,” and positioned to represent the Global South while avoiding the extremes of other major AI governance models. [1][2][3][4][5][6][7]

### Q95: Your Clicks, Your Rights

| | Baseline | Graph-Enriched |
|---|---|---|
| Time | 5.66s | 7.64s (+0.11s expansion) |

| Expanded docs | — | 1 |

> **Judge:** Answer B covers a broader set of rights, provides more concrete details and citations, while both remain accurate.

<details><summary>Baseline answer</summary>

**Your Clicks, Your Rights – What Every Online User Should Know**

| What you do with a click | The right that is involved | Why it matters (according to the sources) |
|--------------------------|----------------------------|-------------------------------------------|
| **Click “I Agree” on a Terms‑of‑Service (ToS) contract** | *Right to be informed and to control personal data* – you have the right to know how your data will be used and to opt‑out of its sale. | The Electronic Frontier Foundation explains that every click that binds you to a ToS is a moment where your data‑related rights can be waived or limited. Users must be aware that “clicks” are not neutral; they can affect privacy and other freedoms. [2] |
| **Click to join a forum, group or chat** | *Right to peacefully assemble and associate online* – you may freely choose the tools (websites, apps, services) to gather with others. | The “Guide to Human Rights for Internet Users” states that users have the right to peacefully assemble and associate on the Internet and to select the platforms they use. [1] |
| **Click “share”, “post”, or “publish” content** | *Right to receive and impart information* – you may create, reuse and distribute content without interference, subject only to limits on hate‑based speech. | The same guide (section 43) affirms the right to seek, receive and impart information and ideas of your choice, while noting that service providers must handle extremist or biased content responsibly. [5] |
| **Click “accept” a privacy setting that allows tracking** | *Right to protect your identity and personal data* – you can say “no” to uncomfortable requests and demand that your data not be used against you. | The Florida “Digital Bill of Rights” lists the right to control personal data, to know it won’t be used against you, and to opt‑out of data sales. [6] |
| **Click “I’m under 13” or similar age‑verification** | *Right of children to safe online participation* – children have the right to enjoy age‑appropriate content and to be protected from data collection. | The child‑online‑protection guidelines give children the right to say “no” to uncomfortable requests and to be protected when they use technology. [3] |

### Key Take‑aways

1. **Every click is a legal act.** When you click “I agree,” you are entering a contract that can limit or shape the rights listed above. [2]  
2. **Your fundamental Internet rights remain in force.** Even after clicking, you retain the right to assemble, express yourself, receive information, and protect your identity, unless a lawful, transparent limitation is applied. [1][5]  
3. **Look for the “Digital Bill of Rights” language in local laws.** In Florida, for example, the law explicitly protects the right to control personal data and to understand search‑engine manipulation. [6]  
4. **Children have extra safeguards.** They can refuse uncomfortable interactions and are entitled to age‑appropriate content and privacy protection. [3]  

### Practical Steps Before You Click

1. **Read the ToS/Privacy Policy** – know what data will be collected, shared or sold.  
2. **Check for opt‑out mechanisms** – the right to opt out of data sale is explicitly recognized. [6]  
3. **Verify the platform respects assembly and expression rights** – ensure the service does not arbitrarily block lawful speech or gatherings. [1][5]  
4. **For minors, use child‑focused tools** – platforms that honor the right to say “no” and protect identity. [3]  

By treating each click as an exercise of—or a potential limitation on—your digital rights, you can better protect your privacy, freedom of expression, and ability to associate online.

</details>

<details><summary>Graph-enriched answer</summary>

**Your clicks are more than just a technical step – they are the moment you give—or withhold—consent that shapes the digital rights you enjoy (or lose) online.**  

Below is a quick map of the most important rights that are triggered every time you click “I agree”, “I accept”, or “I consent” on a website or app, together with the practical implications highlighted in the sources you provided.

| What you click on | Which right is at stake | What the click can do (positive or negative) |
|-------------------|--------------------------|---------------------------------------------|
| **Terms‑of‑Service (TOS) / End‑User‑License‑Agreement** | **Right to control personal data** – the “Digital Bill of Rights” in Florida spells out a right to know how your data will be used and to opt‑out of its sale【6】. | By clicking “I agree” you may be giving a service provider broad permission to collect, store, analyse and even sell your data, thereby **waiving** the right to control that data. If the TOS is transparent and includes opt‑out mechanisms, the click **protects** the right. |
| **Privacy‑policy acceptance** | **Right to protect your identity** (children’s‑online‑protection guidelines)【3】 and **right to be forgotten** (UNCTAD e‑Commerce Week)【4】. | Accepting a privacy policy that allows indefinite retention of personal data can undermine your ability to delete that data later. A clear, limited‑use policy preserves the right to be forgotten and identity protection. |
| **Community‑guidelines acknowledgement** | **Right to receive and impart information** (freedom of expression)【5】. | Some platforms reserve the right to remove “racist, xenophobic, anti‑Semitic, misogynist, sexist or other biased” content. By clicking “I accept the rules”, you acknowledge that the provider may restrict certain speech, which can **limit** your expressive right, but also **protect** others from hate‑speech. |
| **Consent to cookies / tracking** | **Right to peacefully assemble and associate** (online gathering)【1】 and **right to associate freely** (Charter of Human Rights for the Internet)【8】. | Tracking can be used to profile users and target them with ads or political messaging, potentially chilling the willingness to join online groups. Opt‑out options preserve the right to associate without surveillance. |
| **Age‑verification or parental‑consent boxes** | **Right of children to safe participation** (guidelines for child online protection)【3】. | Clicking “I am over 13” when you are not can expose a child to data collection that the guidelines seek to prevent, violating the child’s right to be protected from unnecessary data harvesting. |
| **Search‑engine manipulation disclosures** | **Right to understand how search results are shaped** (Digital Bill of Rights)【6】. | If you click “I accept” without a clear explanation of algorithmic bias, you may unknowingly surrender the ability to demand transparency about how information is presented to you. |

### Why the click matters

1. **Consent is the legal gateway** – Most digital services rely on the user’s click to claim lawful consent for data processing, content moderation, and behavioural advertising.  
2. **Rights can be waived or reinforced** – A well‑drafted, easy‑to‑understand TOS can *reinforce* rights (e.g., by offering opt‑out choices, clear data‑deletion procedures, and transparent moderation policies). A vague, “click‑wrap” agreement can *waive* them (e.g., by granting the provider unlimited data‑use rights).  
3. **Power asymmetry** – The provider writes the terms; the user’s only leverage is the click. This asymmetry is why civil‑society groups (e.g., the Electronic Frontier Foundation) study “the clicks that bind” to expose hidden clauses that erode rights【2】.  

### Practical steps you can take when you click

| Step | How it protects your rights |
|------|------------------------------|
| **Read the headline summary** – Look for sections titled “Data Use”, “Your Rights”, “Content Removal”. |
| **Check for opt‑out mechanisms** – Is there a clear way to say “no” to data sale, tracking, or targeted ads? |
| **Look for a “right to be forgotten” clause** – Does the service promise to delete your data on request? |
| **Verify age‑appropriate safeguards** – For children, the service should require parental consent and limit data collection. |
| **Seek transparency on moderation** – Does the policy explain what content may be removed and why? |
| **Use browser or OS privacy tools** – Even if you click “agree”, you can block cookies, use VPNs, or employ privacy‑focused browsers to limit data collection. |

### Bottom line

Every time you click “I agree”, you are **exercising** (or unintentionally surrendering) a bundle of digital rights: the right to control your personal data, the right to express yourself, the right to associate freely, the right to be forgotten, and the right to a safe online environment—especially for children. Being aware of which right is tied to each checkbox empowers you to make informed choices and hold service providers accountable.  

*In short: your clicks are the gatekeepers of your online freedoms. Treat them as such.*

</details>

### Q97: How did 9/11 reforms influence digital diplomacy?

| | Baseline | Graph-Enriched |
|---|---|---|
| Time | 3.17s | 3.02s (+0.09s expansion) |
| Score avg | 3.5/5 | 4.5/5 |
| Expanded docs | — | 6 |
| **Winner** |  | **✓** |

> **Judge:** Answer A stays within likely source material, provides concrete examples, and cites sources consistently, whereas B introduces a claim about other agendas that may not be supported, lowering its accuracy.

<details><summary>Baseline answer</summary>

The reforms launched after the September 11 attacks were aimed at tearing down the “information‑silos” that had hampered U.S. intelligence and diplomatic work.  By restructuring agencies to share data more freely, the United States created the institutional footing for a **holistic, digitally‑enabled diplomatic apparatus**:

* **Breaking silos → holistic digital diplomacy** – The post‑9/11 overhaul of U.S. intelligence agencies was explicitly intended to cure fragmentation and policy silos, a change that later enabled a broader, “holistic” approach to digital diplomacy [1].

* **Plugging information gaps for diplomats** – One of the direct outcomes of the reforms was a concerted effort to close gaps in information‑sharing between departments, giving diplomats better access to the data they need to operate effectively [5].

* **From “need to know” to “need to share”** – The same security‑driven mindset that followed 9/11 shifted the State Department’s culture from restricting information toward actively disseminating it.  This cultural shift made the Department an early adopter of social‑media tools and other internet platforms to spread diplomatic messages [8].

* **Security focus without discarding other agendas** – Although the reforms re‑emphasized security and counter‑terrorism, they did not push issues such as poverty, disease, or democracy off the agenda; instead, they were integrated into a more connected, technology‑rich diplomatic framework [4].

In sum, the 9/11‑era reforms dismantled compartmentalised information flows, instituted a “need to share” ethos, and equipped diplomats with the internet‑based tools (social media, online meetings, rapid data exchange) that now define modern digital diplomacy.

</details>

<details><summary>Graph-enriched answer</summary>

The post‑9/11 overhaul of U.S. intelligence and foreign‑policy structures was driven by the realization that fragmented agencies and “need‑to‑know” silos had hampered the country’s ability to prevent the attacks.  In response, Washington instituted two linked changes that reshaped its diplomatic practice:

1. **Breaking information silos** – The reforms explicitly “plugged the gaps in information sharing between various departments” so that diplomats could access the same intelligence and analysis that had previously been compartmentalised【5】.  This created a culture of “need to share” rather than “need to know”【8】.

2. **Embedding digital tools in diplomacy** – With data flowing more freely, the State Department began to treat the Internet and social‑media platforms as core diplomatic instruments.  Diplomats now use the web for meetings, negotiation, information exchange and public‑diplomacy outreach (e.g., communicating with diasporas)【6】.  The United States therefore framed its new approach as a **holistic digital diplomacy** strategy that integrates traditional diplomatic functions with the capabilities of the digital environment【1】.

In short, the 9/11‑era reforms dismantled siloed information practices, mandated broader sharing across agencies, and consequently accelerated the adoption of Internet‑based tools and social media as essential components of U.S. diplomatic work. This shift laid the groundwork for today’s “holistic digital diplomacy.”【1】【5】【8】

</details>
