> **Judge:** Answer B covers more aspects, adds concrete examples, and cites multiple sources, making it more complete, specific, and better referenced.

<details><summary>Baseline answer</summary>

The Geneva Internet Platform (GIP) is a **Swiss‑run initiative operated by the DiploFoundation** that serves as a neutral, inclusive hub for digital‑policy dialogue and research. Its core purpose is to bring together a wide range of digital actors—governments, civil society, the private sector and academia—to:

* **Foster digital governance** and monitor emerging digital policies.  
* **Provide a neutral space for policy debates**, both in‑person and online.  
* **Strengthen the participation of small and developing countries** in Geneva‑based digital policy processes.  
* **Support the work of Geneva‑based Internet‑governance and ICT institutions** (e.g., the Internet Governance Forum).  
* **Facilitate evidence‑based, multidisciplinary research** for digital policy.  
* **Bridge policy silos** and offer tools and methods that can be used by other policy venues worldwide【1†L1-L9】【5†L1-L5】.  

In short, the GIP is a platform that connects stakeholders, encourages inclusive dialogue, and helps shape digital policy through research, monitoring, and capacity‑building activities.

</details>

<details><summary>Graph-enriched answer</summary>

The Geneva Internet Platform (GIP) is a **Swiss‑initiated programme run by the DiploFoundation** that serves as a neutral, inclusive hub for digital‑policy dialogue and research. Its core purposes are to:

* **Engage a wide range of digital actors** and foster collaborative digital governance.  
* **Monitor and analyse digital policies** and provide evidence‑based, multidisciplinary research.  
* **Strengthen the participation of small and developing countries** in Geneva‑based Internet‑governance processes.  
* **Support the work of Geneva‑based IG and ICT institutions**, bridge policy silos, and offer tools and methods for both in‑situ and online engagement that can be replicated in other policy spaces worldwide【1】.  

In short, the GIP is a Swiss‑backed, DiploFoundation‑operated platform that creates a neutral space for digital policy debates, amplifies under‑represented voices, and helps coordinate research and engagement on Internet governance and digital policy.

</details>

### Q99: what was the norvegian policy during covid?

| | Baseline | Graph-Enriched |
|---|---|---|
| Time | 3.61s | 4.16s (+0.08s expansion) |
| Score avg | 4.5/5 | 4.5/5 |
| Expanded docs | — | 0 |
| **Winner** | **✓** |  |

> **Judge:** Answer B explicitly includes public‑health and social measures, making it more complete, while both answers are accurate and similarly specific; A has slightly better source coverage, but completeness carries more weight.

<details><summary>Baseline answer</summary>

During the COVID‑19 pandemic Norway pursued a multi‑track policy that combined public‑health restrictions, rapid economic‑support legislation, and an accelerated push for digitalisation – while also guarding privacy rights.

| Policy area | What Norway did | Source |
|------------|----------------|--------|
| **Public‑health & social measures** | The “new normal” quickly became home‑working, home‑schooling and minimal physical contact after the virus hit the country. | 【2】 |
| **Economic response** | The Parliament rapidly turned emergency decisions into statutes and regulations that strengthened the economic safety net for households and businesses. | 【5】 |
| **Digital transformation** | The crisis was described as a catalyst for digitalisation; Norway was already well‑positioned to meet the emergency and used the period to accelerate digital services across the public sector, aquaculture, maritime, agriculture and tourism. | 【6】 |
| **Contact‑tracing app** | Norway’s COVID‑19 tracking app was shut down and all data deleted after the national data‑protection authority warned that processing of personal information would be temporarily banned, citing privacy concerns. | 【4】 |
| **Open digital public goods** | Norway’s broader digital strategy emphasised open, non‑commercial digital public goods to avoid vendor lock‑in and to promote democratic principles in the development arena. | 【8】 |
| **Long‑term outlook** | A 2021 official report (“Norway towards 2025”) recommended ending pandemic‑related restructuring limits as soon as prudent, and called for measures that boost competitiveness, green growth, a strong labour market and economic sustainability. | 【7】 |

**In short:** Norway’s COVID‑19 policy combined strict health measures (home‑based work and schooling, limited contact), swift legislative action to protect incomes and businesses, a strong push to digitise public services, a precautionary shutdown of its contact‑tracing app over privacy issues, and a strategic focus on open digital public goods to sustain long‑term economic and environmental goals.

</details>

<details><summary>Graph-enriched answer</summary>

During the pandemic Norway pursued a **multifaceted policy that combined rapid economic relief, a push for digitalisation, and strict data‑privacy safeguards**.

| Policy area | What Norway did | Source |
|-------------|----------------|--------|
| **Economic safety net** | The Parliament quickly turned pandemic‑related measures into statutes and regulations that expanded the economic safety net for households and businesses. | [5] |
| **Digital transformation & remote life** | The “new normal” was defined by home‑working, home‑schooling and minimal physical contact, and the country leveraged its already‑strong digital infrastructure to keep public services running. | [2] |
| **Support for key sectors** | The crisis affected cross‑border shopping and food‑and‑drink production, prompting targeted interventions to keep those supply chains operating. | [3] |
| **Digital‑public‑goods strategy** | Norway’s broader digital strategy emphasised open, non‑commercial digital public goods to avoid vendor lock‑in and uphold democratic principles. | [8] |
| **Contact‑tracing app** | The Norwegian Institute of Public Health halted the COVID‑19 tracking app and deleted its data after the data‑protection authority warned that processing personal information would be temporarily banned. | [4] |
| **Long‑term economic restructuring** | An official report (“Norway towards 2025”) recommended that pandemic‑induced limits on restructuring be lifted when prudent, focusing on competitiveness, green growth, a robust labour market and sustainability. | [7] |
| **Overall digital push** | The pandemic acted as a catalyst for further digitalisation across the public sector, aquaculture, maritime, agriculture and tourism, positioning Norway to meet the crisis effectively. | [6] |

**In short:** Norway’s COVID‑19 policy combined swift legislative action to protect the economy, an accelerated shift to digital work and services, a commitment to open digital public goods, and a precautionary stance on privacy that led to the shutdown of its contact‑tracing app. The government also planned to unwind temporary restrictions on economic restructuring once conditions allowed, aiming for a greener, more competitive post‑pandemic economy.

</details>

<details><summary>Baseline answer</summary>

Below are the concrete programmes and collaborative mechanisms that the sources identify as actively **advancing digital rights**:

| Initiative | What it does to promote digital rights | Key partners / actors | Source |
|------------|----------------------------------------|-----------------------|--------|
| **Advancing Digital Democracy (ADD) – USAID** | Builds an open, secure and inclusive digital ecosystem by: <br>• Strengthening legal and regulatory frameworks for data and emerging technologies <br>• Providing targeted support to software engineers, tech firms and researchers to embed human‑rights and democratic values throughout the tech lifecycle | USAID, national governments, private‑sector firms, civil‑society organisations, academic/research institutions | [2] |
| **IRPC Human Rights Law & the Global Digital Compact (IGF 2023)** | Raises awareness of digital rights through volunteer‑driven outreach and collaborates with a range of organisations to promote a rights‑based approach to digital policy | IRPC, partner NGOs, volunteer networks | [3] |
| **International Institute for Democracy and Electoral Assistance (IDEA)** | Highlights the need to embed digital rights and freedoms in democratic processes; its advocacy informs policy‑making and capacity‑building at the international level | IDEA, member states, electoral bodies, civil‑society groups | [4] |
| **Digitalise Youth Project – Kofi Annan Foundation (Digital Democracy Initiative)** | • Enhances digital skills and civic‑tech capacities among youth <br>• Raises awareness of online political engagement <br>• Advocates for digital‑rights protections at local, regional and international fora, especially in the Sahel and neighboring regions | Kofi Annan Foundation, youth activists, CSOs, regional partners | [6] |
| **Global Digital Rights Coalition (GDRC) coordination & Rights & Risk Forums** | Provides a platform for multistakeholder dialogue on regulatory implementation, shares best‑practice tools, and coordinates advocacy across regions | GDRC members (NGOs, academia, tech firms), EU diplomatic channels, other international actors | [8] |
| **EU diplomatic initiatives on digital rights** | Uses diplomatic outreach to promote coherent regulatory frameworks that protect digital freedoms and align with human‑rights standards | European Union institutions, partner governments, civil‑society networks | [8] |

### Why these matter
- All of the above initiatives link digital‑rights work to broader development and governance goals (e.g., SDG 9, SDG 16, SDG 17) and call for **legislation, capacity‑building, and multistakeholder cooperation** to embed rights into the digital sphere [1][7].
- They combine **policy reform, technical assistance, advocacy, and youth empowerment**, creating a holistic approach that addresses both the legal foundations and the practical skills needed to safeguard digital freedoms.

</details>

<details><summary>Graph-enriched answer</summary>

**Key initiatives that are actively advancing digital rights**

| Initiative | Main actions for digital‑rights advancement | How it fits the digital‑rights agenda |
|------------|--------------------------------------------|--------------------------------------|
| **Advancing Digital Democracy (ADD) – USAID** | • Builds partnerships with governments, the private sector and civil society to strengthen legal and regulatory frameworks for data and digital technologies.<br>• Provides targeted support to software engineers, tech firms and researchers to embed human‑rights and democratic values throughout the tech lifecycle. | Directly links digital‑rights protection to open, secure and inclusive digital ecosystems, echoing the U.S. International Cyberspace & Digital Policy Strategy. [2] |
| **IRPC Human Rights Law & the Global Digital Compact (IGF 2023)** | • Engages NGOs, volunteers and other stakeholders to raise awareness of digital rights.<br>• Collaborates with a range of organisations to promote a rights‑based approach to digital governance. | Demonstrates a multi‑stakeholder model for embedding human‑rights norms in global digital policy discussions. [3] |
| **Digitalise Youth Project – Kofi Annan Foundation (Digital Democracy Initiative)** | • Enhances digital skills and civic‑tech capacities of youth in the Sahel and neighboring regions.<br>• Conducts advocacy to promote digital rights at local, regional and international levels.<br>• Tackles shrinking civic space and disinformation by empowering youth activists and civil‑society groups. | A capacity‑building and advocacy effort that directly links digital‑rights promotion with democratic participation and resilience against misinformation. [6] |
| **Global Digital Rights Coalition (GDRC) coordination & Rights and Risk Forums** | • Coordinates a coalition of actors working on digital‑rights issues.<br>• Hosts Rights and Risk Forums that discuss regulatory implementation and share best practices.<br>• Supports EU diplomatic initiatives that embed digital‑rights considerations in foreign‑policy tools. | Provides a concrete platform for policy dialogue, coalition‑building and diplomatic action on digital rights. [8] |
| **International Institute for Democracy and Electoral Assistance (IDEA) – advocacy at the Summit of the Future** | • Highlights the importance of promoting digital rights and freedoms in democratic processes. | While not a program per se, IDEA’s high‑level advocacy helps keep digital rights on the agenda of global governance forums. [4] |

**Why these initiatives matter**

- They **translate abstract human‑rights principles into concrete programs** (e.g., legal‑framework support, capacity‑building, coalition coordination).  
- They **mobilise multi‑stakeholder partnerships**—governments, private sector, civil society, and youth—ensuring that digital‑rights advances are inclusive and sustainable.  
- Many are **aligned with the Sustainable Development Goals** (SDG 9, 16, 17), linking digital‑rights progress to broader development outcomes. [1][7]  

Together, these efforts illustrate a growing ecosystem of policy, technical, and advocacy actions that collectively push the global digital agenda toward stronger protection and promotion of digital rights.
